is sufficiently large, the first term in the sum in (
4
) is more than the absolute value of the sum of the other terms, so we conclude that
F(t)>F(t^{\prime})
, as desired.

As the integers
F(t)
with
t\in I_{0}\times\cdots\times I_{k-1}
are distinct modulo
P(m)
, the number of distinct residue classes
F(t)\pmod{P(m)}
is at least

\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}|I_{j}|\geq c\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\ell_{j}/w_{j}\geq c_{k}x^{k}\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\alpha^{2^{j+1}}\geq c_{k}\alpha^{2^{k+1}}x^{k},

where
c_{k}>0
depends only on
k
. Here we used
y_{j}\leq 2x/\ell_{j-1}
,
w_{j}=2^{k-j}y_{j+1}
by the definition of
w_{j}
and the bound (
2
) on
\ell_{j}
.

Note now that
P_{0}(x_{0,i})=P(x_{i})\in P(T)
. We will show, inductively, that for
j\geq 1
we have
P_{j}(x_{j,i})\in 2^{j-1}P(T)-2^{j-1}P(T)
for all
0\leq i\leq\ell_{j}-1
. Indeed,

P_{j}(x_{j,i})=P_{j-1}(x_{j,i}+y_{j})-P_{j-1}(x_{j,i})=P_{j-1}(x_{j-1,t_{i}+1})-P_{j-1}(x_{j-1,t_{i}})\in 2^{j-1}P(T)-2^{j-1}P(T),

recalling that there exist indices
t_{i}
such that
x_{j,i}=x_{j-1,t_{i}}
and
x_{j,i}+y_{j}=x_{j-1,t_{i}+1}
. As each
F(t)
is the sum of
k
terms in which the
j^{\textrm{th}}
term is of the form
P_{j}(x_{j,i})
, we have that each
F(t)
is in the set
P(T)+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}(2^{j-1}P(T)-2^{j-1}P(T))=2^{k-1}P(T)-(2^{k-1}-1)P(T)
. The set
2^{k-1}P(T)-2^{k-1}P(T)=-P(T)+2^{k-1}P(T)-(2^{k-1}-1)P(T)
is the union of
|-P(T)|
translates of
2^{k-1}P(T)-(2^{k-1}-1)P(T)
. Hence,

\left|2^{k-1}P(T)-2^{k-1}P(T)\right|\geq c_{k}\alpha^{2^{k+1}}x^{k}>\alpha^{C_{k}}P(m)

for an appropriate constant
C_{k}
depending only on
k
, completing the proof.
∎

Remark.
A Hilbert cube of dimension
k
(or simply a
k
-cube) is a set
H(a_{0},e_{1},\dots,e_{k})
of the form
\{a_{0}+\sum_{i\in I}e_{i}:I\subseteq[k]\}
with
a_{0}
an integer and
e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}
positive integers (see
[
27
]
for more on the long history of these objects). The first step in the proof of Lemma
2.10
was to iteratively build many Hilbert cubes of dimension
k
consisting of elements of
T
, all with
e_{j}=y_{j}
and where we can take
a_{0}
to be any
x_{k,i}
.
An alternative approach to this step is to build many
k
-cubes in
T
with small
e_{1},\dots,e_{k}
and then to use the pigeonhole principle to show that one can pick out many such
k
-cubes with the same
e_{1},\dots,e_{k}
.

As in the previous subsection, we will deduce Lemma
2.9
from a modular analogue, which we now state.
Recall that
\Sigma_{m}(S)
is the set of subset sums modulo
m
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
P
be a complete
polynomial of degree
k
with integer coefficients in its binomial
representation. Fix
c\geq k2^{k+4}
and assume
x
is sufficiently large (depending on
P
). Let
w=(\log x)^{1/2}
and let
X
be the set of
y\in[x,(1+1/k)x)
such that
P(y)
has no prime divisor at most
w
. Let
m\in X
. If
S=(s_{1},\dots,s_{q})
is a sequence of
q=c\log x
elements
chosen uniformly and independently at random from
X
and the sequence
P(S)=(P(s_{1}),\dots,P(s_{q}))
is viewed as
a sequence of elements in
\mathbb{Z}_{P(m)}
, then
|\Sigma_{P(m)}(P(S))|<P(m)/4
with probability
at most
\left(\log x\right)^{-(c-k2^{k+3})(\log x)/(8C_{k})}
, where
C_{k}
is the constant defined in Lemma
2.10
.

We will need the following estimate for the proof of Lemma
3.2
.

Lemma 3.3
.

For each positive integer
k
, there is
c_{k}>0
such that the following holds. Suppose
P
is a complete polynomial of degree
k
with integer coefficients in its binomial representation. If
x
is sufficiently large and
1<w<(\log x)/2
is an integer, then the set
X
of
y\in[x,(1+1/k)x)
such that
P(y)
has
no prime divisor at most
w
satisfies
|X|\geq c_{k}(\log w)^{-k}x
.

Proof.

For each prime
q\leq k
, let
v_{q}
be the largest integer
v
such that
q^{v}\mid k!
. For
i\leq k
, we have
\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}(q^{2v_{q}}+(x-j))=\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}(x-j)+q^{2v_{q}}z
for some integer
z
, so

\binom{x+q^{2v_{q}}}{i}-\binom{x}{i}=\frac{1}{i!}\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}(q^{2v_{q}}+(x-j))-\frac{1}{i!}\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}(x-j)=\frac{q^{2v_{q}}z}{i!}.

Letting
v_{q,i}
and
r_{i}
be integers such that
i!=q^{v_{q,i}}r_{i}
and
\gcd(r_{i},q)=1
, we have, since
\binom{x+q^{2v_{q}}}{i}-\binom{x}{i}
is an integer, that
r_{i}\mid z
. Moreover,
v_{q,i}\leq v_{q}
, since
i!\mid k!
. Hence,

\binom{x+q^{2v_{q}}}{i}-\binom{x}{i}=\frac{q^{2v_{q}}z}{i!}=q^{v_{q}}\cdot q^{v_{q}-v_{q,i}}\frac{z}{r_{i}}\equiv 0\pmod{q^{v_{q}}}.

That is,
\binom{x}{i}\pmod{q^{v_{q}}}
is periodic every
q^{2v_{q}}
and, therefore,
P(x)\pmod{q}
is periodic every
q^{2v_{q}}
. Since
P
is complete, for each prime
q\leq k
, there exists an integer
x\in[1,q^{2v_{q}}]
such that
P(x)
is coprime to
q
. Using that
\prod_{q\leq k,\textrm{ $q$ prime}}q^{2v_{q}}=k!^{2}
, we have, by the Chinese Remainder Theorem, that there exists an integer
y\in[1,k!^{2}]
such that
P(y)
is coprime to all primes
q\leq k
. We also have that
P(x)\pmod{q}
is periodic every
k!^{2}
for all primes
q\leq k
. Moreover, for each prime
q>k\geq i
,
\binom{x}{i}\pmod{q}
is periodic every
q
. Therefore, letting

R_{k,w}=k!^{2}\prod_{k<q\leq w,\,q\textrm{ prime}}q
, we have that
P(x)\pmod{\prod_{q\leq w,\,q\textrm{ prime}}q}
is periodic every
R_{k,w}
.

Let
W
be the set of positive integers
y
at most
R_{k,w}
such that

P(y)
is coprime to all primes at most
w
. Let
m_{P,q}
be the
number of roots of
P(x)\pmod{q}
, which is at most
k
for each prime
q
,
where we used that
P
has degree
k
and is nonzero modulo
q
by completeness. By the Chinese Remainder Theorem, the fraction of
y\in[\prod_{k<q\leq w,\,q\textrm{ prime}}q]

such that
P(y)
is coprime to
\prod_{k<q\leq w,\,q\textrm{ prime}}q
is then

\prod_{k<q\leq w,\,q\textrm{ prime}}\left(1-\frac{m_{P,q}}{q}\right)\geq c^{\prime}_{k}(\log w)^{-k}

for some constant
c^{\prime}_{k}>0
, where we used the bound
1-\varepsilon>e^{-\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{2}}
for
0<\varepsilon<1/2
and Merten’s second theorem, which implies that
\sum_{q\leq w,\,q\textrm{ prime}}1/q=\log\log w+O(1)
. Furthermore, as shown above, there exists
y\in[k!^{2}]
such that
P(y)
is coprime to
\prod_{q\leq k,\,q\textrm{ prime}}q
. Since
P(y)\pmod{\prod_{q\leq k,\,q\textrm{ prime}}q}
is periodic every
k!^{2}
and
\gcd(k!^{2},\prod_{k<q\leq w,\,q\textrm{ prime}}q)=1
, the Chinese Remainder Theorem implies that the fraction of
y\in[R_{k,w}]
such that
P(y)
is coprime to
R_{k,w}
is
at least
(c^{\prime}_{k}/k!^{2})(\log w)^{-k}
. Hence,
|W|\geq(c^{\prime}_{k}/k!^{2})(\log w)^{-k}R_{k,w}
. Since the integers
y
for which
P(y)
has no prime factor at most
w
are periodic every
R_{k,w}
and
R_{k,w}\leq x^{1/2+o(1)}
by the assumption
w\leq(\log x)/2
, we have that
|X|\geq(|W|/R_{k,w})(x/k)-|W|\geq c_{k}(\log w)^{-k}x
for an appropriate
c_{k}>0
depending only on
k
, as required.
∎

The proof of Lemma
3.2
now proceeds along broadly similar lines to the proof of Lemma
3.1
.

Proof of Lemma
3.2
.

Let
S_{i}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{i})
denote the sequence consisting of the first
i
terms of
S
and let
T_{i}=(P(s_{1}),P(s_{2}),\dots,P(s_{i}))
. We also write
T
as a shorthand for
T_{q}=P(S)
. Call
i\in[2,q]

bad
if

•

|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i})|\leq(1+2^{-k-1})|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|
and
|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|<\frac{P(m)}{2w}

or

•

|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i})|\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{2^{k+1}w}\right)|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|
and
\frac{P(m)}{2w}\leq|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|<\frac{P(m)}{4}
.

The following claims are the key components in the proof. Here
C_{k}
is the constant from Lemma
2.10
.

Claim 1.
The probability that
i
is bad conditioned on the choice of
S_{i-1}
is at most
p:=(\log x)^{-1/(4C_{k})}
.

Claim 2.
If
|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T)|<P(m)/4
, then the number of integers in
[2,q]
which are not bad is less than
k2^{k+3}\log x
.

By Claim 1, for any
B\subset[2,q]
, the probability that all elements in
B
are bad is at most
p^{|B|}
. By Claim 2, if
|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T)|<P(m)/4
, then there is a set
B
of
q-k2^{k+3}\log x=(c-k2^{k+3})\log x
integers
i\in[2,q]
which are bad. Taking a union bound over all choices of
B
, the probability that
|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T)|<P(m)/4
is at most

\displaystyle\binom{q}{q-k2^{k+3}\log x}p^{|B|}

\displaystyle=\binom{q}{k2^{k+3}\log x}p^{|B|}

\displaystyle\leq(ec)^{k2^{k+3}\log x}(\log x)^{-(c-k2^{k+3})(\log x)/(4C_{k})}

\displaystyle<(\log x)^{-(c-k2^{k+3})(\log x)/(8C_{k})}.\qed

Therefore, in order to complete the proof of the lemma, it suffices to prove Claims 1 and 2. It is here, in the proof of Claim 1, that Lemma
2.10
comes into play.

Proof of Claim 1.
Fix
S_{i-1}=(s_{1},\dots,s_{i-1})
. Conditioned on this choice of
S_{i-1}
, we bound the probability that
i
is bad. If
|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|\geq P(m)/4
, then
i
cannot be bad (so the probability that
i
is bad is zero). The proof now splits into two cases, when
|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|<\frac{P(m)}{2w}
and when
\frac{P(m)}{2w}\leq|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|<P(m)/4
.

Case 1.

|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|<\frac{P(m)}{2w}
.

Let

V=\left\{t\in[x,(1+1/k)x):\,|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1}\cup\{P(t)\})|\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\right)|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|\right\}.

Observe that
i
is bad conditioned on
S_{i-1}
if and only if
s_{i}\in V
. We will show that
|V|\leq\alpha x
, where
\alpha=w^{-1/C_{k}}
and
C_{k}
is again the constant from Lemma
2.10
.

Suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that
|V|>\alpha x
. Lemma
2.10
then implies that

|2^{k-1}P(V)-2^{k-1}P(V)|>\alpha^{C_{k}}P(m)=P(m)/w,

where
P(V)=\{P(v):v\in V\}
. Note now that if
U\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{P(m)}
and
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{P(m)}
, then
|\Sigma_{P(m)}(U\cup\{u\})|=|\Sigma_{P(m)}(U\cup\{-u\})|
. Thus, for each
z\in P(V)\cup(-P(V))
, we have

|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1}\cup\{z\})|\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{2^{k+1}}\right)|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|

and Lemma
2.7
implies that, for each
y\in 2^{k-1}P(V)-2^{k-1}P(V)
,

|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1}\cup\{y\})|\leq\left(1+\frac{2^{k}}{2^{k+1}}\right)|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|=\frac{3}{2}|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|.

(5)

However, by Lemma
2.6
, the number of
y\in\mathbb{Z}_{P(m)}

satisfying (
5
)
is at most
2|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|<P(m)/w
. But this contradicts the bound
|2^{k-1}P(V)-2^{k-1}P(V)|>P(m)/w
, so we must indeed have that
|V|\leq\alpha x
.

Case 2.

P(m)/2w\leq|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|<P(m)/4
.

Let

V=\left\{t\in[x,(1+1/k)x):|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1}\cup\{P(t)\})|\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{2^{k+1}w}\right)|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|\right\}.

Observe again that
i
is bad conditioned on
S_{i-1}
if and only if
s_{i}\in V
.
As in Case 1, we will show that
|V|\leq\alpha x
. Indeed,
suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that
|V|>\alpha x
. Then,
by Lemma
2.10
, we again have that

|2^{k-1}P(V)-2^{k-1}P(V)|>P(m)/w
.
By our assumption that
P(m)
has no prime divisor at most
w
,

2^{k-1}P(V)-2^{k-1}P(V)
cannot be contained in a coset of a proper subgroup of

\mathbb{Z}_{P(m)}
. Hence, by Lemma
2.3
,

|2^{k-1}wP(V)-2^{k-1}wP(V)|>P(m)/2.

However, again using Lemma
2.7
, for all elements
y\in 2^{k-1}wP(V)-2^{k-1}wP(V)
, we have

|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1}\cup\{y\})|\leq\left(1+\frac{2^{k}w}{2^{k+1}w}\right)|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|=\frac{3}{2}|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|.

But, by Lemma
2.6
, the number of such elements is at
most
2|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|<P(m)/2
, a contradiction.

Therefore, in either case, the set
V
of bad choices satisfies
|V|\leq\alpha x
. By using Lemma
3.3
, which says that

|X|\geq c_{k}(\log w)^{-k}x
for an appropriate
c_{k}>0
,
this implies that the probability
i
is bad conditioned on the choice of
S_{i-1}
is at most

|V|/|X|\leq\alpha x/\left(c_{k}(\log w)^{-k}x\right)=\alpha c_{k}^{-1}(\log w)^{k}=c_{k}^{-1}(\log x)^{-1/(2C_{k})}\left(\frac{1}{2}\log\log x\right)^{k}<(\log x)^{-1/(4C_{k})}=p,

as required.
∎

Proof of Claim 2.

As
S_{i-1}\subset S_{i}
for
i\in[2,q]
,
\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})\subseteq\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i})
and, hence,
1\leq|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{1})|\leq\cdots\leq|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{q})|=|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T)|<P(m)/4
. Therefore, the number of
i
which are not bad with
|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|<P(m)/2w
and
|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i})|\geq(1+2^{-k-1})|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|
is at most
\frac{\log(P(2x)/2w)}{\log(1+2^{-k-1})}\leq k2^{k+2}\log x
. Moreover, the number of
i
which are not bad with
P(m)/2w\leq|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|<P(m)/4
and
|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i})|\geq\left(1+\frac{1}{2^{k+1}w}\right)|\Sigma_{P(m)}(T_{i-1})|
is at most
\frac{\log(2w)}{\log(1+2^{-k-1}w^{-1})}\leq\log x
, where we used that
w=(\log x)^{1/2}
. Therefore, the number of
i\in[2,q]
which are not bad is at most
k2^{k+2}\log x+\log x<k2^{k+3}\log x
. ∎

We conclude this subsection and the proof of Theorem
1.2
by using Lemma
3.2
to prove Lemma
2.9
. To this end, suppose that
P
is a complete polynomial of degree
k
with integer coefficients in its binomial representation,
C(k)=k2^{k+15}
,
\epsilon\in(0,1/2]
and
X
is the set of
y\in[x,(1+1/k)x)
such that
P(y)
has no prime divisor at most
(\log x)^{1/2}
. Our aim is to show that if a sequence
S
of
C(k)\epsilon^{-1}\log x
elements in
X
is chosen independently and uniformly at random, then, with high probability,
S
has distinct terms and, for any subsequence
S^{\prime}
of
S
of size
\epsilon|S|
, the set
\Sigma(P(S^{\prime}))
contains all integers in the interval
[\frac{e}{9}P(x)|S^{\prime}|,\frac{8}{9}P(x)|S^{\prime}|]
.

Proof of Lemma
2.9
.

As
P
is a complete polynomial, its leading coefficient is positive. Hence, for
x
sufficiently large,
P
will be positive and strictly increasing on the interval
[x,(1+1/k)x]
. We may therefore assume that
P
is injective on the interval
[x,(1+1/k)x)
and, for any
y
in this interval,
P(y)\in[P(x),P((1+1/k)x))\subset[P(x),eP(x))
.

By the birthday paradox, as
|S|=o(\sqrt{|X|})
,
P(S)
has distinct terms with high probability.
Fix a choice of subset
I^{\prime}
of
[C(k)\epsilon^{-1}\log x]
of size
C(k)\log x
and consider the subsequence
S^{\prime}
of
S
given by
(s_{i})_{i\in I^{\prime}}
. Let
I^{\prime\prime}
be the smallest
|S^{\prime}|/9
elements in
I^{\prime}
and let
S^{\prime\prime}
be given by
(s_{i})_{i\in I^{\prime\prime}}
. Let
\ell=64
. Arrange
I^{\prime\prime}
in increasing order and partition
I^{\prime\prime}
into
\ell
sets
I^{\prime\prime}_{1},\dots,I^{\prime\prime}_{\ell}
of consecutive terms so that each set
I^{\prime\prime}_{j}
for
j\in[\ell]
has size
|I^{\prime\prime}|/\ell
. This gives a partition of
S^{\prime\prime}
into
\ell
subsequences
S^{\prime\prime}_{1},\dots,S^{\prime\prime}_{\ell}
, where
S^{\prime\prime}_{j}=(s_{i})_{i\in I^{\prime\prime}_{j}}
. Note that each element of
\Sigma(P(S^{\prime\prime}_{j}))
is nonnegative and at most
P((1+1/k)x)|S^{\prime\prime}_{j}|<eP(x)|S^{\prime\prime}_{j}|
. We shall prove below that, with high probability, the sequence
S

has the property that, for all possible choices of
I^{\prime}
and

j
,
|\Sigma(P(S^{\prime\prime}_{j}))|\geq P(x)|S^{\prime\prime}_{j}|/8
. Assuming
this, we can show that
\Sigma(P(S_{j}^{\prime\prime}))
is not contained in an arithmetic
progression with common difference larger than
1
. Indeed, if
\Sigma(P(S_{j}^{\prime\prime}))
is contained in an arithmetic progression
with common difference
d>1
, then
d\leq\frac{P((1+1/k)x)|S^{\prime\prime}_{j}|}{P(x)|S^{\prime\prime}_{j}|/8-1}\leq 9e
.
Moreover, if
\Sigma(P(S_{j}^{\prime\prime}))
is contained in an arithmetic progression
with common difference
d>1
, then all elements in
\Sigma(P(S_{j}^{\prime\prime}))

are congruent modulo
d
, from which it follows that all elements
of
P(S_{j}^{\prime\prime})
are divisible by
d
. This contradicts the fact that
no element of
P(S_{j}^{\prime\prime})
has a prime factor at most
(\log x)^{1/2}
.
Hence, for each
j
,
\Sigma(P(S_{j}^{\prime\prime}))
is not contained in an arithmetic
progression with common difference larger than
1
. Therefore, by Lemma
2.2
, as
\Sigma(S^{\prime\prime})=\Sigma(S^{\prime\prime}_{1})+\cdots+\Sigma(S^{\prime\prime}_{\ell})
, the set
\Sigma(S^{\prime\prime})
contains the integers in an interval of length at least
\ell\left(P(x)|S^{\prime\prime}_{j}|/8-1\right)+1>P((1+1/k)x)
. Finally, by Lemma
2.1
,
\Sigma(P(S^{\prime\prime})\cup P(S^{\prime}\setminus S^{\prime\prime}))
contains all integers in the interval
[eP(x)|S^{\prime}|/9,8P(x)|S^{\prime}|/9]
, where we used that all elements of
S^{\prime}
are at most
P((1+1/k)x)
, the elements of
\Sigma(S^{\prime\prime})
are at most
P((1+1/k)x)|S^{\prime\prime}|=P((1+1/k)x)|S^{\prime}|/9<eP(x)|S^{\prime}|/9
and the sum of the elements in
S^{\prime}
is at least
P(x)|S^{\prime}\setminus S^{\prime\prime}|=8P(x)|S^{\prime}|/9
.

It remains to show that, with high probability, the sequence

S
has the property that, for all possible choices of
I^{\prime}
and

j
,
|\Sigma(P(S^{\prime\prime}_{j}))|\geq P(x)|S^{\prime\prime}_{j}|/8
. Fix
an index
1\leq j\leq\ell
and partition the index set
I^{\prime\prime}_{j}
of
S^{\prime\prime}_{j}
into two consecutive blocks
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
of equal size. Let
Q_{1}=(s_{i})_{i\in J_{1}}
and
Q_{2}=(s_{i})_{i\in J_{2}}
, so
|Q_{1}|=|Q_{2}|=\frac{|S_{j}^{\prime\prime}|}{2}=\frac{C(k)\log x}{18\ell}>k2^{k+4}\log x
. Recall that
X
is the set of integers in
[x,(1+1/k)x)
such that
P(x)

has no prime divisor at most
(\log x)^{1/2}
. Consider
m\in X
.
We note that when we fix the subset of indices
I^{\prime}
of
[C(k)\epsilon^{-1}\log x]
of size
C(k)\log x

and the index
j
, then
J_{1}
is determined as a particular subsequence of
I^{\prime}
.
Moreover, each element in
Q_{1}
is uniformly
and independently distributed in
X
. Taking a union bound over all

x\ell\binom{C(k)\epsilon^{-1}\log x}{C(k)\log x}
choices of
I^{\prime}
,
j\in[\ell]
and
m\in X
, Lemma
3.2
implies that
the probability
|\Sigma_{P(m)}(P(Q_{1}))|<\frac{P(m)}{4}

for some choice of
I^{\prime}
,
j\in[\ell]
and
m\in X
is at most

\displaystyle x\ell\binom{C(k)\epsilon^{-1}\log x}{C(k)\log x}\cdot\left(\log x\right)^{-k2^{k}C_{k}^{-1}\log x}

\displaystyle\leq x\ell(e/\epsilon)^{C(k)\log x}\cdot\left(\log x\right)^{-k2^{k}C_{k}^{-1}\log x}=o_{x}(1),

where
o_{x}(1)
tends to
0
as
x
tends to infinity. Thus, with high probability, the sequence
S
is such that
|\Sigma_{P(m)}(P(Q_{1}))|\geq\frac{P(m)}{4}
for all possible choices of
I^{\prime}
,
j\in[\ell]
and
m\in X
. In this case, by repeated application of Lemma
2.5
, for all
j\in[\ell]
,

|\Sigma(P(S_{j}^{\prime\prime}))|\geq\sum_{m\in Q_{2}}P(m)/4=|Q_{2}|P(m)/4=|S^{\prime\prime}_{j}|P(m)/8\geq P(x)|S^{\prime\prime}_{j}|/8.

Therefore, with high probability, the sequence
S
is such that
|\Sigma(P(S^{\prime\prime}_{j}))|\geq P(x)|S^{\prime\prime}_{j}|/8
for all possible choices of
I^{\prime}
and
j
, as required.
∎

3.3
Proof of the lower bound in Theorem
1.1

We first prove a useful lemma.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
S
be a sequence of positive integers and
m
and
q
be positive integers. Then

\left|\Sigma(S)\cap[m]\right|\leq 2^{m/q}\prod_{a\in S\cap[m]}\left(1+2^{-a/q}\right)\leq 2^{m/q}\exp\left(\sum_{a\in S\cap[m]}2^{-a/q}\right).

Proof.

Let
r_{S}(s)
denote the number of ways of representing
s
as a sum of distinct elements from
S
. So if
s\in\Sigma(S)
, then
r_{S}(s)\geq 1
, while
r_{S}(s)=0
otherwise. For each
s\in\Sigma(S)\cap[m]
, we get a contribution of one to the leftmost expression. For the middle expression, by expanding the product, for each
s\in\Sigma(S)\cap[m]
we get a contribution of
r_{S}(s)\cdot 2^{m/q}\cdot 2^{-s/q}\geq 1
, proving the desired inequality. We then get the last inequality by using
1+z\leq e^{z}
for
z\geq 0
.
∎

Using the above lemma, we prove the following theorem, giving the lower bound in Theorem
1.1
.

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
r\geq 2
be an integer. If a sequence of positive integers
A
satisfies
A(n)\leq\frac{r-1}{140}(\log_{2}n)^{2}
for all sufficently large
n
, then
A
is not
r
-Ramsey complete.

Proof.

By replacing
r
by
r-1
if
r
is odd, it suffices to prove that, for
r\geq 2
even, a sequence of positive integers
A
with
A(n)\leq\frac{r}{70}(\log_{2}n)^{2}
for all sufficiently large
n
is not
r
-Ramsey complete.

By reordering, we may suppose that
A=(a_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
is in increasing order
a_{1}\leq a_{2}\leq\cdots
. Define an
r/2
-coloring of
A
, which we call the
hue coloring
, by assigning
a_{\ell}
hue
h\in\mathbb{Z}_{r/2}
if
\ell\equiv h\pmod{r/2}
. For a positive integer
j
, define a red/blue-coloring
C_{j}
of
A
where
C_{j}(a)
is red if
a\leq 2^{j}
and blue otherwise. Let
c_{j}
be the product coloring formed from the hue coloring and the red/blue-coloring
C_{j}
. That is,
c_{j}
is an
r
-coloring of
A
given by the hue and whether or not the term is at most
2^{j}
.

The largest positive integer that can be written as a sum of red elements of the same hue in coloring
c_{j}
is at most

2^{j}+\frac{2}{r}\sum_{a\in A\cap[2^{j}]}a.

(6)

This follows since, for any two hues
h
and
h^{\prime}
, the elements of
A\cap[2^{j}]
with hue
h
and those with hue
h^{\prime}
interlace and are bounded by
2^{j}
, so the sum of elements of hue
h
is at most
2^{j}
more than the sum of elements of hue
h^{\prime}
and, therefore, at most
2^{j}
more than the average sum of elements taken over all hues.

Let the
cost
of
a\in A\cap[2^{j}]
for the coloring
c_{j}
be
a/2^{j}j
. Over all colorings
c_{j}
with
j\geq 1
, the total cost of
a>2
is
\sum_{j\geq\log_{2}a}a/2^{j}j\leq 2/\log_{2}a
, while the cost of each
a\in\{1,2\}
over all such
c_{j}
is at most
2
. If any number larger than
2^{j-1}(j+2)
can be written as a sum of monochromatic red elements in coloring
c_{j}
(so they are also of the same hue), then, by (
6
), we have

2^{j}+\frac{2}{r}\sum_{a\in A\cap[2^{j}]}a>2^{j-1}(j+2),

or, equivalently,
\sum_{a\in A\cap[2^{j}]}a>r2^{j-2}j
, so the total cost of all elements in
A\cap[2^{j}]
for the coloring
c_{j}
is at least
r/4
.

Let
i
be a sufficiently large positive integer. The total cost of the elements
a\in A\cap[2^{i}]
for the colorings
c_{1},\ldots,c_{i}
is at most

O(1)+\sum_{a\in A\cap(2,2^{i}]}\frac{2}{\log_{2}a}<\frac{ri}{32},

(7)

where the
O(1)
term comes from considering the cost of the terms
a\in\{1,2\}
. To prove inequality (
7
), we use Abel’s summation formula

\sum_{x_{0}<n\leq x}t_{n}f(n)=T(x)f(x)-T(x_{0})f(x_{0})-\int_{x_{0}}^{x}T(y)f^{\prime}(y)dy,

where
f
is a continuously differentiable function on
[x_{0},x]
and
T(y)=\sum_{n\leq y}t_{n}
. Using Abel’s summation formula with
t_{n}=1
if
n\in A\cap(2^{i_{0}},2^{i}]
and
t_{n}=0
otherwise, where
i_{0}
is chosen so that
A(n)\leq\frac{r}{140}(\log_{2}n)^{2}
for all
n\geq 2^{i_{0}}
, and
f(x)=\frac{1}{\log_{2}x}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in A\cap(2,2^{i}]}\frac{1}{\log_{2}a}

\displaystyle=O(1)+\frac{A(2^{i})}{i}-\frac{A(2^{i_{0}})}{i_{0}}+\int_{2^{i_{0}}}^{2^{i}}\frac{A(x)\log 2}{x(\log x)^{2}}dx

\displaystyle\leq O(1)+\frac{ri^{2}}{140i}+\int_{2^{i_{0}}}^{2^{i}}\frac{r(\log_{2}x)^{2}\log 2}{140x(\log x)^{2}}dx

\displaystyle\leq O(1)+\frac{2ri}{140}<\frac{ri}{65},

where we assume in the last inequality that
i
is sufficiently large.

Thus, fewer than
(ri/32)/(r/4)=i/8
of the
i
colorings
c_{j}
with
1\leq j\leq i
have the property that there is a number greater than
2^{j-1}(j+2)
that can be expressed as a sum of elements which are red of the same hue. We call
j

red-strong
if there is a number greater than
2^{j-1}(j+2)
that can be expressed as a sum of elements which are red of the same hue in the coloring
c_{j}
.

For a non-negative integer
j
, define

g(j)=\sum_{a\in A,2^{j}<a\leq 2^{j}j}2^{-a/2^{j+4}}.

For each
a\in A
, the contribution of
a
to the various
g(j)
is

\sum_{j:2^{j}<a\leq 2^{j}j}2^{-a/2^{j+4}}\leq\sum_{h\geq 0}2^{-2^{h-4}}<5,

where we used the change of variables
h=\lfloor\log_{2}a\rfloor-j
.
Hence,

\sum_{j=1}^{i}g(j)<\sum_{a\in A\cap[2^{i}i]}5=5A(i2^{i}).

For
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{r/2}
, let
A_{s}
be the subset of
A
consisting of elements of hue
s
. Let
A_{s,>t}=\{a\in A_{s}:a>t\}
. Let
b(j)
denote the number of elements of
[2^{j}j]
which can be written as a sum of blue elements in coloring
c_{j}
of the same hue, so
b(j)=|\bigcup_{s=1}^{r/2}\Sigma(A_{s,>2^{j}})\cap[2^{j}j]|\leq\sum_{s=1}^{r/2}|\Sigma(A_{s,>2^{j}})\cap[2^{j}j]|
. Applying Lemma
3.4
with
S=A_{s,>2^{j}}
,
m=2^{j}j
and
q=2^{j+4}
, we have

\left|\Sigma(A_{s,>2^{j}})\cap[2^{j}j]\right|\leq 2^{j/16}\exp\left(\sum_{a\in A_{s,>2^{j}}\cap[2^{j}j]}2^{-a/2^{j+4}}\right)\leq 2^{j/16}\exp\left(1+\frac{2}{r}g(j)\right),

where we have again used the fact that, for any two hues
h
and
h^{\prime}
, the blue elements with hue
h
interlace the blue elements of hue
h^{\prime}
together with the observation that the function
x\mapsto 2^{-2^{x-4}}
is monotone and bounded above by
1
.
We thus have
b(j)\leq\frac{r}{2}2^{j/16}\exp\left(1+\frac{2}{r}g(j)\right)
.
Hence, for
i
sufficiently large,

\prod_{j=1}^{i}b(j)\leq\prod_{j=1}^{i}\frac{r}{2}2^{j/16}\textrm{exp}\left(1+\frac{2}{r}g(j)\right)\leq r^{i}2^{i^{2}/31}\exp\left(\frac{2}{r}\sum_{j=1}^{i}g(j)\right)\leq r^{i}2^{i^{2}/31}\exp\left(\frac{10}{r}A(i2^{i})\right)<2^{i^{2}/4}.

(8)

If at least
3i/4
of the
i
colorings
c_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,i
have the property that at least
2^{j}
positive integers at most
2^{j}j
can be written as a sum of blue elements of the same hue, then the left hand side of (
8
) is at least
\prod_{j=1}^{3i/4}2^{j}=2^{\binom{3i/4+1}{2}}>2^{i^{2}/4}
, contradicting (
8
). Hence, for at least
i/4
of the colorings
c_{j}
with
j=1,\ldots,i
, we have that there are at most
2^{j}
positive integers at most
2^{j}j
which can be written as a sum of blue elements of the same hue in
A
. Call
j

blue-strong
if in coloring
c_{j}
at least
2^{j}
positive integers at most
2^{j}j
can be written as a sum of blue elements of the same hue in
A
. Call
j

weak
if it is neither blue-strong nor red-strong. Hence, for
i
sufficiently large, there are at least
i-i/8-3i/4=i/8
elements
j\in[i]
which are weak. Thus, there are infinitely many weak
j
and we let
J=\{j_{h}\}_{h\geq 1}
be an infinite sequence of weak
j
so that
j_{h}\geq 2^{rj_{h-1}^{2}}
.

We next define an
r
-coloring
c
of
A
for which there are infinitely many integers which cannot be written as a sum of monochromatic elements from
A
. The coloring
c
is a product coloring of the hue coloring (which uses
r/2
colors) and a red/blue-coloring of
A
. We color an integer in
A
blue if it is in one of the intervals
(2^{j},2^{j}j]
with
j\in J
and red otherwise. We will prove that at least half the elements in
(2^{j-1}(j+2),2^{j}j]
, where
j\in J
is sufficiently large, cannot be written as a monochromatic sum in the coloring
c
.

Suppose now that
N\in(2^{j-1}(j+2),2^{j}j]
is a sum of red elements of the same hue. Since there are no red elements in
(2^{j},2^{j}j]
in the coloring
c
,
N
can also be written as a sum of red elements of the same hue in
c_{j}
, contradicting the assumption that
j
is weak. Hence, no element in
(2^{j-1}(j+2),2^{j}j]
is a sum of red elements of the same hue in the coloring
c
.

As
j=j_{h}
is weak, there are at most
2^{j}
elements at most
2^{j}j
that can be written as a monochromatic sum of blue elements of the same hue in
(2^{j},2^{j}j]
. The number of remaining blue elements in
[2^{j}j]
is at most

A(2^{j_{h-1}}j_{h-1})\leq\frac{r}{140}(\log_{2}(2^{j_{h-1}}j_{h-1}))^{2}\leq\frac{rj_{h-1}^{2}}{2}\leq\frac{\log_{2}j}{2}.

Thus, the number of positive integers at most
2^{j}j
which can be written as a monochromatic sum of blue elements in the coloring
c
is at most
2^{(\log_{2}j)/2}2^{j}<2^{j}j/8
. Hence, as
2^{j}j/8\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(2^{j}j-2^{j-1}(j+2)\right)
,
at least half the elements in
(2^{j-1}(j+2),2^{j}j]
cannot be written as a sum of blue elements of the same hue in the coloring
c
. As there are infinitely many such
j
, there are infinitely many positive integers which are not the monochromatic sum of elements in the coloring
c
. This completes the proof. ∎

Remark.
In the proof above, for
j=1,\ldots,i
, we made use of colorings
C_{j}
which color the positive integers up to
2^{j}
red and all larger integers blue. Alternatively, we could have picked a random coloring
\phi_{x}
which colors all positive integers up to
x
red and all larger integers blue, where
x\in[N]
is chosen with probability
\frac{1}{xH(N)}
with
H(N)=\sum_{x=1}^{N}\frac{1}{x}
. One can then do a similar analysis using elementary probability to get a better constant factor in Theorem
3.5
.

4
Density completeness

In this section, we discuss Theorems
1.3
and
1.4
, our results on density completeness. Since reordering a sequence does not change whether or not it is
\epsilon
-complete, it will suffice to consider monotonically increasing sequences. We will begin with the following simple result, from which the first part of Theorem
1.3
follows.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\epsilon>0
. If
A=(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is
a monotonically increasing sequence of positive integers which is
\epsilon
-complete,
then there is
C
such that

a_{n}\leq\sum_{i\leq\epsilon n+C}a_{i}

holds for all positive integers
n
.

Proof.

Suppose that there is no such
C
. Then there is a function
g:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}

with
\lim_{n\to\infty}g(n)=\infty
such that
a_{n}>\sum_{i\leq\epsilon n+g(n)}a_{i}

holds for infinitely many
n
. Thus, we can pick an infinite sequence
of positive integers
n_{1},n_{2},\dots
such that, for all
j
, we have

a_{n_{j}}>\sum_{i\leq\epsilon n_{j}+g(n_{j})}a_{i}
and
g(n_{j})>n_{j-1}
. Pick a subsequence
A^{\prime}
of
A
by deleting all elements
a_{i}

of
A
whose subscript
i
satisfies
\epsilon n_{j}+g(n_{j})<i\leq n_{j}

for some positive integer
j
.

We first show that
A^{\prime}(x)\geq\epsilon A(x)
holds for all
x
. It
suffices to check this when
x=a_{n_{j}}
for
some positive integer
j
. However, we have
A^{\prime}(a_{n_{j}})\geq\epsilon n_{j}+g(n_{j})-n_{j-1}>\epsilon n_{j}=\epsilon A(a_{n_{j}})
,
as required.

To see that
A^{\prime}
is not complete, we show that
each integer
a_{n_{j}}
is not the sum of elements from
A^{\prime}
. Indeed,
such elements must be at most
a_{n_{j}}
and hence at most
a_{\epsilon n_{j}+g(n_{j})}
.
However,
\sum_{i\leq\epsilon n_{j}+g(n_{j})}a_{i}<a_{n_{j}}
, so

a_{n_{j}}
is not in
\Sigma(A^{\prime})
and
A^{\prime}
is not complete.
∎

This gives a necessary growth condition for
a sequence to be
\epsilon
-complete. Recall that it is also necessary
for an
\epsilon
-complete sequence to satisfy the divisibility
condition that no prime is a factor of more than an
\epsilon
-proportion
of the elements in the sequence. In the proof of Theorem
4.3

below, we show that, apart from some mild additional assumptions, a
random sequence satisfying both the growth condition from Theorem
4.1
and a suitable variant of this divisibility condition
is likely to be
\epsilon
-complete.

Recall that, for a sequence
B=(b_{n})_{n\geq 1}
, the discrete
derivative is defined by
\Delta b_{n}:=b_{n+1}-b_{n}
. Fix
0<\epsilon<1
. A sequence
B=(b_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is called
\epsilon
-friendly
(or
friendly
) if it satisfies the following five growth conditions:

1.

For some constant
C
and all
n
,

b_{n}\leq\sum_{i\leq\epsilon n+C}b_{i}.

2.

\lim_{n\to\infty}\Delta b_{n}=\infty
.

3.

\lim_{i\to\infty}\frac{B(2^{i+1})-B(2^{i})}{i}=\infty
.

4.

There exists
0<c<1
such that

c\Delta b_{i}\leq\Delta b_{j}
for all
i<j
. Moreover, if
b_{j}<2b_{i}
,
then
\Delta b_{j}\leq\frac{1}{c}\Delta b_{i}
.

5.

B
is strictly increasing.

By Theorem
4.1
, condition (i) is necessary for
an increasing sequence of positive integers to be
\epsilon
-complete. The other growth conditions
are mild assumptions that will be helpful in proving the existence
of an
\epsilon
-complete sequence
A=(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}
which interlaces

B
, that is, for which
b_{n}\leq a_{n}\leq b_{n+1}
for all
n
.

Let
b_{1},b_{2},\ldots,b_{t}
be any finite strictly increasing
sequence of positive integers. Let
\{x\}=x-\lfloor x\rfloor
denote
the fractional part of
x
. If we define
B=(b_{n})_{n\geq 1}
recursively
by

b_{n}=\lfloor\{\epsilon n\}b_{\lceil\epsilon n\rceil}\rfloor+\sum_{i\leq\epsilon n}b_{i}

for
n>t
, then it is easy to check that such a sequence is friendly
and satisfies
b_{n}=\Theta(f_{n})
, where, following the introduction,
F=(f_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is any sequence
of positive integers for which
f_{n}=\sum_{i\leq\epsilon n}f_{i}

for all sufficiently large
n
. We note that the term
\lfloor\{\epsilon n\}b_{\lceil\epsilon n\rceil}\rfloor
is added as a “discrete interpolation” factor to guarantee conditions (ii) and (iv) of friendly sequences.

Claim 4.2
.

If
B=(b_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a friendly sequence
and
n
is a sufficiently large positive integer, then
b_{2n/c+1}\geq 2b_{n+1}
,
where
c
is the constant in condition (iv) of friendly sequences.

Proof.

We have

b_{n+1}=b_{1}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Delta b_{i}\leq b_{1}+n\max_{i\leq n}\Delta b_{i}

(9)

and

\displaystyle b_{2n/c+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b_{n+1}+\sum_{i=n+1}^{2n/c}\Delta b_{i}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle b_{n+1}+(2n/c-n)\min_{j\geq n}\Delta b_{j}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle b_{n+1}+(n/c)\cdot c\max_{i\leq n}\Delta b_{i}+(n/c-n)\min_{j\geq n}\Delta b_{j}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle b_{n+1}+(n/c)\cdot c\max_{i\leq n}\Delta b_{i}+b_{1}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 2b_{n+1}.

Here we used condition (iv) of friendly sequences to deduce the second inequality, the third inequality
follows from
(n/c-n)\min_{j\geq n}\Delta b_{j}>b_{1}
for
n
sufficiently large and the last inequality
is by (
9
).
∎

The next theorem is our main result on
\epsilon
-complete sequences
and completes the proof of Theorem
1.3
. We remark that since condition (i) of friendly sequences only
gives an upper bound on
b_{n}
, this result also allows us to find sequences that are considerably denser than

(f_{n})_{n\geq 1}
that are
\epsilon
-complete.

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
0<\epsilon<1
and
B=(b_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a friendly sequence
as defined above. Then there is a sequence
A=(a_{n})_{n\geq 1}
of positive
integers that interlaces
B
, i.e.,
b_{n}\leq a_{n}<b_{n+1}
for
all
n
, which is
\epsilon
-complete.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon_{0}>0
be sufficiently small. We pick the sequence
A
by taking, for
j
sufficiently large,

a_{j}
to be a uniform random integer in
[b_{j},b_{j+1})
which has
no prime factor at most
(\max(1/\epsilon,1/\epsilon_{0}))^{4000}
. For small
j
this might not be possible,
as the interval
[b_{j},b_{j+1})
might not contain any integer with no prime factor
at most
(\max(1/\epsilon,1/\epsilon_{0}))^{4000}
, so we let
a_{j}
be any integer in
[b_{j},b_{j+1})

in this case. This guarantees that
A
interlaces
B
.

For a positive integer
i
, let
h(i)
be the smallest integer for
which
b_{h(i)}\geq 2^{i}
. Note that
b_{h(i+1)-1}
is the largest
element of
B
which is less than
2^{i+1}
. Let
A_{i}:=A\cap[b_{h(i)},b_{h(i+1)-1})
,
so
A_{i}
consists of all but at most two elements of
A\cap[2^{i},2^{i+1})
. By condition (iii) of friendly sequences, for any
C
and for
i
sufficiently large depending on
C
,
|A_{i}|\geq C\max(1/\epsilon,1/\epsilon_{0})i
.
The following lemma is a close relative of Lemma
2.8
. The proof of the lemma, which is an appropriate modification of the proof of Lemma
2.8
, is deferred to Appendix
A.2
.

Lemma 4.4
.

There exist positive constants
\epsilon_{0}
,
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
such that the following holds. For
i
sufficiently large, with positive probability,
the set
A_{i}
has the property that, for any subset
A^{\prime}_{i}\subset A_{i}

with
|A^{\prime}_{i}|\geq(\min(\epsilon,\epsilon_{0})/4)|A_{i}|
,
A^{\prime}_{i}
contains
a subset
A^{\prime\prime}_{i}
with
|A^{\prime\prime}_{i}|\leq C_{1}i
such that
\Sigma(A^{\prime\prime}_{i})

contains every integer in the interval
[y,2y]
, where
y=C_{2}2^{i}i
.

Since the choices of
A_{i}
for different
i
are
mutually independent, we can guarantee and will assume that
A_{i}
satisfies the conclusion of Lemma
4.4
for
each
sufficiently large
i
.

Our goal now is to show that if
A^{\prime}
is any subsequence of
A

with
|A^{\prime}(n)|\geq\epsilon|A(n)|
for all sufficiently large
n
,
then
A^{\prime}
is complete. We first show that for each
n_{0}
there
is
n\geq n_{0}
such that
A^{\prime}
is complete or
A^{\prime}
contains only
roughly
\epsilon n
elements among the first
n
elements of
A
.
We then go through a very similar argument using this additional structure
to conclude that
A^{\prime}
is complete.

Let
i_{0}
be a sufficiently large positive integer and
m=A(2^{i_{0}})
.
The number of elements in
A
which are at most
2^{i_{0}}
and not
in any
A_{i}
is at most
2i_{0}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4}m
. Let
A^{\prime}_{i}=A_{i}\cap A^{\prime}
. So the
set
\bigcup_{i\leq i_{0}}A^{\prime}_{i}
of elements in
A^{\prime}
which are
at most
2^{i_{0}}
and in some
A_{i}
has size at least
\epsilon A(2^{i_{0}})-\frac{\epsilon}{4}m\geq\frac{3\epsilon}{4}m
.
Let
i_{1}\leq i_{0}
be the largest positive integer for which
|A^{\prime}_{i_{1}}|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{4}|A_{i_{1}}|
, which exists by the observation just made.
The set
\bigcup_{i_{1}<i\leq i_{0}}A^{\prime}_{i}
has cardinality at most

\frac{\epsilon}{4}|\bigcup_{i_{1}<i\leq i_{0}}A_{i}|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4}m
,
so there are at least
\frac{3\epsilon}{4}m-\frac{\epsilon}{4}m=\frac{\epsilon}{2}m

elements in
\bigcup_{i\leq i_{1}}A^{\prime}_{i}
. In particular,
A(2^{i_{1}+1})\geq A^{\prime}(2^{i_{1}+1})\geq\frac{\epsilon}{2}m
.

Since
A_{i_{1}}
satisfies the conclusion of Lemma
4.4
and
|A^{\prime}_{i_{1}}|\geq(\epsilon/4)|A_{i_{1}}|\geq(\min(\epsilon,\epsilon_{0})/4)|A_{i_{1}}|
, there is
A^{\prime\prime}_{i_{1}}\subset A^{\prime}_{i_{1}}
with

|A^{\prime\prime}_{i_{1}}|\leq C_{1}i_{1}
such that
\Sigma(A^{\prime\prime}_{i_{1}})
contains
every integer in the interval
[y,2y]
where
y=C_{2}2^{i_{1}}i_{1}
.
Label the elements in
A^{\prime}\setminus A^{\prime\prime}_{i_{1}}
in increasing order
as
a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2},\ldots
.

By Lemma
2.1
, if, for each
j
, we have
a^{\prime}_{j}\leq 2y-y+a^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+a^{\prime}_{j-1}
,
then
\Sigma(A^{\prime}\setminus A^{\prime\prime}_{i_{1}})+[y,2y)
contains all integers
at least
y
and, as
\Sigma(A^{\prime})
is a superset of
\Sigma(A^{\prime}\setminus A^{\prime\prime}_{i_{1}})+[y,2y)
,

A^{\prime}
would be complete. So we may assume that there is some
j
for
which

a^{\prime}_{j}>y+a^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+a^{\prime}_{j-1}.

(10)

In particular,
a^{\prime}_{j}\geq 2^{i_{1}+1}\geq a_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}m}\geq\frac{\epsilon}{2}m
, so that
j
can be made sufficiently large by taking
i_{0}
and, hence,
m
sufficiently large.

As
a^{\prime}_{j}\in A^{\prime}\setminus A^{\prime\prime}_{i_{1}}\subset A
, there is a positive integer
n
for
which
a^{\prime}_{j}=a_{n}
, where, again,
n
can be made sufficiently large by taking
i_{0}
sufficiently large. We have

\sum_{i\leq j-1}a^{\prime}_{i}<a_{n}<b_{n+1}\leq\sum_{i\leq\epsilon(n+1)+C}b_{i}\leq\sum_{i\leq\epsilon(n+1)+C}a_{i},

where the first inequality follows from (
10
), the second
and fourth inequalities are by the fact that
A
interlaces
B
and the third
inequality follows from condition (i) of friendly sequences.
This implies that
j-1\leq\epsilon(n+1)+C
, so

A^{\prime}(a_{n}-1)\leq|A^{\prime\prime}_{i_{1}}|+j-1\leq C_{1}i_{1}+\epsilon(n+1)+C<C_{1}i_{0}+\epsilon n+C+1.

(11)

That is, the number of elements of
A^{\prime}
amongst the first
n
elements of
A
is roughly
\epsilon n
.

We next give a similar argument, but using the extra information that
there are many elements
a_{s}\in A\setminus A^{\prime}
with
s\leq n

in order to conclude that
A^{\prime}
is complete. Let
N=8(n+1)/(\epsilon^{2}c)
.
Let
i_{2}
be the least positive integer such that
2^{i_{2}}\geq a_{N}
and
let
m^{\prime}=A(2^{i_{2}})
, so
m^{\prime}\geq N
.
As
a_{2N/c+1}\geq b_{2N/c+1}\geq 2b_{N+1}>2a_{N}
, where the middle
inequality follows from Claim
4.2
, there is a perfect
power of two which is at least
a_{N}
and less than
a_{2N/c+1}
,
so
m^{\prime}=A(2^{i_{2}})\leq 2N/c
. Furthermore, since
a_{n}=a_{j}^{\prime}\geq a_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}m}
, we have that
n\geq\frac{\epsilon}{2}m
. Thus,
N=8(n+1)/(\epsilon^{2}c)>m
, so
A(2^{i_{2}})\geq N>m=A(2^{i_{0}})
. In particular, we obtain that
i_{2}\geq i_{0}
. Hence,
N
,
i_{2}
and
m^{\prime}
may be made sufficiently large by taking
i_{0}
and, hence,
n
sufficiently large.

We also have
A^{\prime}(2^{i_{2}})\geq\epsilon A(2^{i_{2}})=\epsilon m^{\prime}
,
so
A^{\prime}
contains at least
\epsilon m^{\prime}
elements
a_{s}\leq 2^{i_{2}}
.
Let
i_{3}\leq i_{2}
be the largest positive integer such
that
|A^{\prime}_{i_{3}}|\geq\frac{\epsilon}{4}|A_{i_{3}}|
. Recall that,
for each positive integer
i
, the number of elements of
A
in

[2^{i},2^{i+1})\setminus[b_{h(i)},b_{h(i+1)-1})
is at most two.
It follows that
A^{\prime}\cap[2^{i_{2}}]\setminus\bigcup_{i<i_{2}}[b_{h(i)},b_{h(i+1)-1})

has cardinality at most
2i_{2}\leq\frac{\epsilon m^{\prime}}{4}
, where
the last inequality follows from condition (iii) of friendly sequences and the fact that
i_{2}
is sufficiently large. Hence, at
least a fraction
\epsilon-\frac{\epsilon}{4}-\frac{\epsilon}{4}=\frac{\epsilon}{2}

of the elements of
A
up to
2^{i_{2}}
are greater than
a_{\epsilon N/4}

and in
A^{\prime}\cap\bigcup_{i<i_{2}}[b_{h(i)},b_{h(i+1)-1})
and, therefore,

i_{3}
satisfies
2^{i_{3}+1}>a_{\epsilon N/4}
.

Since
A_{i_{3}}
satisfies the conclusion of Lemma
4.4
and
|A^{\prime}_{i_{3}}|\geq(\min(\epsilon,\epsilon_{0})/4)|A_{i_{3}}|
, there is
A_{i_{3}}^{\prime\prime}\subset A^{\prime}_{i_{3}}
with

|A_{i_{3}}^{\prime\prime}|\leq C_{1}i_{3}
such that
\Sigma(A_{i_{3}}^{\prime\prime})
contains
every integer in the interval
[y^{\prime},2y^{\prime}]
where
y^{\prime}=C_{2}2^{i_{3}}i_{3}
.
Label the elements in
A^{\prime}\setminus A_{i_{3}}^{\prime\prime}
in increasing order
as
a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2},\ldots
, noting that we have relabeled most of the elements
in
A^{\prime}
.

Again, by Lemma
2.1
, if, for each
j
,
we have
a^{\prime}_{j}\leq 2y^{\prime}-y^{\prime}+a^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+a^{\prime}_{j-1}
, then
\Sigma(A^{\prime}\setminus A_{i_{3}}^{\prime\prime})+[y^{\prime},2y^{\prime})

contains all integers at least
y^{\prime}
and, as
\Sigma(A^{\prime})
is a superset
of
\Sigma(A^{\prime}\setminus A_{i_{3}}^{\prime\prime})+[y^{\prime},2y^{\prime})
,
A^{\prime}
would be
complete. So we may assume that there is some
j^{\prime}
for which

a^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}>y^{\prime}+a^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+a^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}-1}.

Note in particular that
a^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}\geq y^{\prime}\geq 2^{i_{3}+1}\geq a_{\epsilon N/4}
.
Let
n^{\prime}
be such that
a_{n^{\prime}}=a^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}
, so

n^{\prime}\geq\epsilon N/4.

(12)

By condition (i) of friendly sequences, we have

a^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}=a_{n^{\prime}}<b_{n^{\prime}+1}\leq\sum_{i\leq\epsilon(n^{\prime}+1)+C}b_{i}\leq\sum_{i\leq\epsilon(n^{\prime}+1)+C}a_{i}.

Note also that, for
i\geq\epsilon n^{\prime}
, we have

a_{i}\geq a_{\epsilon n^{\prime}}\geq b_{\epsilon n^{\prime}}\geq 2b_{c\epsilon n^{\prime}/2}>2a_{c\epsilon n^{\prime}/2-1}\geq 2a_{c\epsilon^{2}N/8-1}\geq 2a_{n}.

(13)

Here the first inequality follows from
A
being increasing, the second
and fourth inequalities follow from the fact that
A
interlaces
B
, the third
inequality follows from Claim
4.2
, the fifth inequality
follows from (
12
) and the last inequality follows from
the choice of
N
and the fact that
A
is increasing.

Since
A^{\prime}
has at least
\epsilon n^{\prime}
elements up to
a_{n^{\prime}}
,
we have
j^{\prime}\geq\epsilon n^{\prime}-|A^{\prime\prime}_{i_{3}}|
. It follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{k<j^{\prime}}a^{\prime}_{k}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-\sum_{a\in[a_{n}]\cap(A\setminus A^{\prime})}a+\sum_{k\leq\epsilon n^{\prime}-|A_{i_{3}}^{\prime\prime}|-1+n-A^{\prime}(a_{n})}a_{k}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-a_{n}(n-A^{\prime}(a_{n}))+\sum_{k\leq\epsilon n^{\prime}-|A_{i_{3}}^{\prime\prime}|-1+n-A^{\prime}(a_{n})}a_{k}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}a_{\epsilon n^{\prime}}(n-A^{\prime}(a_{n}))+\sum_{k\leq\epsilon n^{\prime}-|A_{i_{3}}^{\prime\prime}|-1+n-A^{\prime}(a_{n})}a_{k}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{k\leq\epsilon n^{\prime}-|A_{i_{3}}^{\prime\prime}|-1+\frac{1}{2}\left(n-A^{\prime}(a_{n})\right)}a_{k},

where the first inequality uses that
A
is an increasing sequence and
\sum_{k<j^{\prime}}a^{\prime}_{k}+\sum_{a\in[a_{n}]\cap(A\setminus A^{\prime})}a
is a sum of at least
\epsilon n^{\prime}-|A^{\prime\prime}_{i_{3}}|-1+n-A^{\prime}(a_{n})
distinct terms of
A
, which is at least the sum of the first
\epsilon n^{\prime}-|A^{\prime\prime}_{i_{3}}|-1+n-A^{\prime}(a_{n})
terms in
A
.
The second inequality follows from
A
being increasing,
the third inequality follows from using (
13
) and the
last inequality follows from
A
being increasing and the following estimate showing that
\frac{1}{2}(n-A^{\prime}(a_{n}))-|A^{\prime\prime}_{i_{3}}|-1>0
. We have

-|A_{i_{3}}^{\prime\prime}|-1+\frac{1}{2}\left(n-A^{\prime}(a_{n})\right)\geq-C_{1}i_{3}-1+\frac{1}{2}\left(n-\left(C_{1}i_{0}+\epsilon n+C+2\right)\right)\geq-2C_{1}i_{2}+\frac{1}{2}(1-\epsilon)n-\frac{C+2}{2}>C+1,

where the first inequality is by (
11
), the second
inequality uses
i_{0},i_{3}\leq i_{2}
and
i_{2}
is sufficiently
large, while the last inequality uses
n\geq\epsilon^{2}cN/10
,

N\geq cm^{\prime}/2
, condition (i) of friendly sequences, the fact that
A

interlaces
B
,
m^{\prime}=A(2^{i_{2}})
,
i_{2}
is sufficiently
large and
m^{\prime}\geq 2^{\sqrt{\left(2\log_{2}(1/\epsilon)+o(1)\right)i_{2}}}
from Appendix
A.1
, from all of which it follows that
i_{2}\ll n
.
However, this implies that

b_{n^{\prime}+1}>a_{n^{\prime}}=a^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}>\sum_{k<j^{\prime}}a^{\prime}_{k}>\sum_{k\leq\epsilon n^{\prime}+C+1}a_{k}\geq\sum_{k\leq\epsilon n^{\prime}+C+1}b_{k}\geq\sum_{k\leq\epsilon(n^{\prime}+1)+C}b_{k},

contradicting condition (i) of friendly sequences.
∎

Theorem
1.4
is obtained similarly, by replacing Lemma
4.4
in the above proof by an appropriate analogue of Lemma
2.9
. As indicated in the introduction, we omit the details.

5
Monochromatic subset sums

5.1
Proof of the lower bound in Theorem
1.6

Throughout this section, we use the convention that products and sums indexed by
p
run over primes. Recall that
p_{i}
is the
i^{\textrm{th}}
prime,
W(\rho)=\prod_{i=1}^{\rho}p_{i}
and
\tau(\rho,m)=\phi(W(\rho)m)/(W(\rho)m)=\prod_{p|W(\rho)m}(1-1/p)
. We recall from the introduction that, for positive integers
n
and
m
with
m\in[n,\binom{n}{2}]
, we define
\rho(n,m)
to be the smallest positive integer
\rho
such that
\rho/\tau(\rho,m)\geq n^{2}/\phi(m)
. Let
\psi(n,m)=\frac{m^{1/3}(m/\phi(m))}{(\log n)^{1/3}(\log\log n)^{2/3}}
and
{\cal R}(n,m)=\min\left(\psi(n,m),\rho(n,m)\right)
. By Claim
B.3
in Appendix
B.2
, we note that
{\cal R}(n,m)=\Theta\left(\psi(n,m)\right)
when
m=O\left(\frac{n^{3/2}(\log\log n)^{1/2}}{(\log n)^{1/2}}\right)
and
{\cal R}(m,n)=\Theta(\rho(n,m))
otherwise.

We aim to prove that
f(n,m)
, the minimum
r
such that there exists an
r
-coloring of
[n-1]
where
m
cannot be written as a sum of distinct monochromatic elements, is bounded below by
{\cal R}(n,m)
up to a constant factor, giving the lower bound in Theorem
1.6
. The main result of this subsection is the following lemma, from which the required lower bound easily follows.

Lemma 5.1
.

There exist positive constants
c
and
C

such that the following holds. Let
n
be sufficiently large and
m\in[n,\binom{n}{2}]
be such that
r=c{\cal R}(n,m)
is at least
C
.
Let
y<n/2
be such that

m\in\left[\frac{y^{2}(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)}{25r},\frac{y^{2}(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)}{15r}\right]

and let
Y
be the set of integers in
[y,2y)
of the form
qu
, where
u|m
,

u\leq y^{1/16}
and
q
is coprime to
W(r)m
.
Then, in any
r
-coloring of
Y
, there exists a monochromatic subset sum which equals
m
.

By Claim
B.4
in Appendix
B.2
, for any
m\in[n,\binom{n}{2}]
, there exists a choice of
y\in[\max(r^{2},n^{3/5}),n/2)
satisfying the required condition. We may therefore apply the lemma to conclude that if
{\cal R}(n,m)\geq C/c
, then
f(n,m)\geq c{\cal R}(n,m)
. That is, the lower bound in Theorem
1.6
holds in this case. On the other hand, if
{\cal R}(n,m)<C/c
, we have the trivial bound
f(n,m)\geq 1\geq C^{-1}c{\cal R}(n,m)
, so the lower bound in Theorem
1.6
also holds in this case. For the same reason, we can and will assume throughout that
r
is sufficiently large.

We will build towards the proof of Lemma
5.1
through a series of reductions and intermediate results. For convenience, we will often use objects and notation in the lemma statements without repeating their definitions from earlier. We begin with the following number-theoretic estimate, whose proof may be found in Appendix
B.1
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
r
,
n
and
m
be positive integers such that
m\in[n,\binom{n}{2}]
,
r\leq n
and
r
is sufficiently large. For any interval
I=[x,2x)
with
x\geq n^{1/4}
, there are at most
8(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)x
integers in
I
of the form
qu
, where
u|m
,
u\leq x^{1/16}
and
q
is coprime to
W(r)m
. If also
x\geq r^{2}
, then there are at least
\frac{1}{8}(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)x
integers in
I
of this form.

By Lemma
5.2
, the set
Y
defined in Lemma
5.1
satisfies

|Y|\geq\frac{1}{8}(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)y.

By the pigeonhole principle, in any
r
-coloring of
Y
, there is
one color class whose size is at least
\frac{1}{8}(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)\frac{y}{r}
. Let
Q_{0}
be the elements of
Y
in this color class. We will prove that
m\in\Sigma(Q_{0})
.

Call a set
X
of integers
k
-diverse
if, for each
v\geq 2
,
there are at least
k
elements of
X
which are not divisible by

v
. If
Q_{0}
is not
y^{1/4}
-diverse, there exists
v_{0}\geq 2
such that at most
y^{1/4}
elements of
Q_{0}
are not divisible by
v_{0}
. We replace
Q_{0}
by

Q_{1}=\{a/v_{0}:a\in Q_{0},v_{0}|a\}\subseteq[y/v_{0},2y/v_{0})
. We then iterate this process. For
i\geq 1
, if
Q_{i}
is not
y^{1/4}
-diverse, we can remove at most
y^{1/4}
elements of
Q_{i}
so that the remaining elements are divisible by some
v_{i}\geq 2
. We then let
Q_{i+1}=\{x/v_{i}:x\in Q_{i},v_{i}|x\}
. We stop the process once we reach a set
Q_{s}
which is
y^{1/4}
-diverse. Note that there can be at most
\log_{2}n
iterations, so there must be at least
\frac{1}{8}(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)\frac{y}{r}-y^{1/4}\log_{2}n

elements in
Q_{s}
.

By the process defining
Q_{s}
, there exists
v
such that
Q_{s}=\{x/v:x\in Q_{0},v|x\}
.
Let
Q=Q_{s}
. Then
Q
is a subset of
[y/v,2y/v)
of
size at least
\frac{1}{8}(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)\frac{y}{r}-y^{1/4}\log_{2}n
which is
y^{1/4}
-diverse.
Let
z=\frac{1}{8}(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)\frac{y}{r}-y^{1/4}\log_{2}n
. Note that

\frac{1}{8}(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)\frac{y}{r}\geq z\geq\frac{1}{10}(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)\frac{y}{r}\geq\frac{1}{10}\tau(r,m)\sqrt{y}>y^{1/3},

(14)

where we used that
y\geq\max(r^{2},n^{3/5})
, which is inequality (
35
) of Claim
B.4
in Appendix
B.2
, and
\tau(r,m)\geq 1/(8\log n\log\log n)
by inequality (
33
) in Appendix
B.2
. In particular, for
r
sufficiently large,

|Q|=z\geq\frac{1}{10}(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)\frac{y}{r}>64(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)\frac{y}{r\log r}.

The next lemma shows that
v|m
.

Lemma 5.3
.

If there exist at least
64(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)\frac{y}{r\log r}

elements in
Y
which are divisible by
v
, then
v|m
and
v\leq y^{1/16}
. Furthermore, all elements of
Y
which are divisible by
v
have the form
qvu
, where
(vu)|m
,
vu\leq y^{1/16}
and
\gcd(q,W(r)m)=1
.

Proof.

Note that if
\gcd(q,W(r)m)=1
and
q\neq 1
, then any prime factor of
q
is at least
p_{r}>r\log r/8
for sufficiently large
r
. Recall that elements of
Y
have the form
qu
, where
u|m
,
u\leq y^{1/16}
and
\gcd(q,W(r)m)=1
. Assume that there exists
v
such that either
v\nmid m
or
v>y^{1/16}
and at least
64(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)\frac{y}{r\log r}
elements in
Y
are divisible by
v
. We claim that
v
must have a prime factor
p
which is coprime to
W(r)m
. Indeed, if this were not the case, then
v
only has prime factors which are divisors of
W(r)m
, so
\gcd(v,q)=1
for any
q
coprime to
W(r)m
. Thus, if an element of the form
qu
with
u|m
,
u\leq y^{1/16}
and
\gcd(q,W(r)m)=1
is divisible by
v
, then
v|u
, so
v|m
and
v\leq y^{1/16}
, contradicting our assumption. Thus,
v
has a prime factor
p
which is coprime to
W(r)m
. In particular,
p\geq p_{r}>r(\log r)/8
.

We have that at least
64(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)\frac{y}{r\log r}
elements
of
Y
are divisible by
p
. For each element
qu
of
Y
which is divisible by
p
, since
p
is coprime to
W(r)m
, we must have
p|q
, so
q=q^{\prime}p
for
q^{\prime}
coprime to
W(r)m
. Hence, elements of
Y
which are divisible by
p
have the form
pq^{\prime}u
where
u|m
,
u\leq y^{1/16}
and
\gcd(q^{\prime},W(r)m)=1
. If
y/p\geq n^{1/4}
, Lemma
5.2
implies that the number of such elements is at most
8(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)\frac{y}{p}<64(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)\frac{y}{r\log r}
. If
y/p<n^{1/4}
, then the number of such elements is at most
y/p<n^{1/4}<64(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)\frac{y}{r\log r}
, where the second inequality is verified as inequality (
36
) of Claim
B.4
in Appendix
B.2
. In either case, we have a contradiction, so we must have that
v|m
and
v\leq y^{1/16}
.

Since
v|m
, we have
\gcd(v,W(r)m)=v
, so each element of the form
qu
where
u|m
,
u\leq y^{1/16}
and
\gcd(q,W(r)m)=1
which is divisible by
v
must have
v|u
. Hence,
qu=qvu^{\prime}
where
(vu^{\prime})|m
,
vu^{\prime}\leq y^{1/16}
and
\gcd(q,W(r)m)=1
, establishing the second claim in the lemma.
∎

Since
|Q|>64(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)\frac{y}{r\log r}
and
\{vx:x\in Q\}
is a subset of
Y
, Lemma
5.3
implies that each element of
Q
can be written in the form
qu
, where
(vu)|m
,
vu\leq y^{1/16}
and
\gcd(q,W(r)m)=1
. Let

Y_{v}=\{t\in[y/v,2y/v):t=qu,u|(m/v),u\leq y^{1/16}/v,\gcd(q,W(r)m)=1\}.

We have that
vt\in Y
for all
t\in Y_{v}
and
Q\subseteq Y_{v}
.

Let
V
be a random subset of
Q
of size
z/8
. The next lemma implies that
V
is
y^{1/4}/16
-diverse with probability at least
1/2
. From now, we fix
V
to be a subset of
Q
of size
z/8
which is
y^{1/4}/16
-diverse.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
k
and
h
be positive integers with
h\neq 1
and
N=\exp(k/16h)
.
Let
A
be a set of
t
integers in
[N]
which is
k
-diverse. Let
B
be a uniformly random subset of
A
of
size
t/h
. Then
B
is
k/(2h)
-diverse with probability at least
1-1/N
.

Proof.

For each
d\in[N]
with
d>1
, let
X_{d}
be the set of elements in
A
which are not divisible by
d
. By our assumption,
|X_{d}|\geq k
for each
d
. The number of elements in
B\cap X_{d}
follows a hypergeometric distribution. As the hypergeometric distribution is at least as concentrated as the corresponding binomial distribution (for a proof, see Section 6 of
[
29
]
), we can apply the Chernoff bound to obtain that the probability that
|B\cap X_{d}|<|X_{d}|/(2h)
is at most
\exp(-|X_{d}|/8h)\leq\exp(-k/8h)=N^{-2}
. By taking a union bound over all
d\in[N]
with
d>1
, we conclude that the probability
B
is not
k/(2h)
-diverse is at most
N\cdot N^{-2}=N^{-1}
.
∎

The following lemma is the key to proving Lemma
5.1
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\ell=\lceil 32/\xi\rceil
, where
\xi
is the constant in Lemma
2.4
. Let
A
be a subset of
Y_{v}
of
size
z/(8\ell)
which is
y^{1/4}/(32\ell)
-diverse. Then
|\Sigma(A)|\geq yz/(\ell^{2}v)

and
\Sigma(A)
is not a subset of an arithmetic progression with
common difference greater than
1
.

Before moving on to the proof of Lemma
5.5
, we show how Lemma
5.1
follows from it.

Proof of Lemma
5.1
assuming Lemma
5.5
.

Recall that we have fixed a subset
V
of
Q
of size
z/8
which is
y^{1/4}/16
-diverse. We will prove that
\Sigma(V)
contains an interval
J=[a,b]
of length
at least
2y/v
. To see why this suffices, first note that

vb\leq v\cdot\max\Sigma(V)<2y\cdot\frac{z}{8}\leq 2y\cdot\frac{(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)y}{8\cdot 8r}<m,

where the third inequality follows from (
14
) and the last inequality follows since
y^{2}\leq\frac{25rm}{(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)}
by the choice of
y
.
If now we can find the required interval
J
, Lemma
5.1
follows
since each element of
Q
is at most
2y/v
and, hence, by Lemma
2.1
,
\Sigma(Q)=\Sigma(V\cup(Q\setminus V))

contains an interval whose smallest element is
a<m/v
by the inequality above and whose largest element is

b+\sum_{i\in Q\setminus V}i>\frac{y}{v}\cdot z(1-1/8)\geq\frac{y}{v}\cdot\frac{7(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)y}{8\cdot 10r}>\frac{m}{v},

where the last inequality follows since
y^{2}\geq\frac{15rm}{(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)}
by the choice of
y
.
Hence,
\Sigma(Q_{0})
contains the progression
\{va,v(a+1),\dots,v(m/v)\}
, which contains
m
.

We partition
V
randomly into
\ell
sets
V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{\ell}
of size
z/(8\ell)
. By Lemma
5.4
and the union
bound, the probability that
V_{i}
is
y^{1/4}/(32\ell)
-diverse for all
i\in[\ell]
is at least
1/2
. Hence, we can fix
a partition of
V
into
\ell
sets
V_{1},V_{2},\dots,V_{\ell}
of size
z/(8\ell)
, where
V_{i}
is
y^{1/4}/(32\ell)
-diverse for each
i\in[\ell]
.

For each
i\in[\ell]
,
\Sigma(V_{i})
is a subset of the interval

[0,z/(8\ell)\cdot 2y/v]=[0,yz/(4\ell v)]
. By Lemma
5.5
,

|\Sigma(V_{i})|\geq yz/(\ell^{2}v)

and
\Sigma(V_{i})
is not a subset of an arithmetic progression
with common difference greater than
1
. Therefore, by Lemma
2.2
,

\Sigma(V_{1})+\cdots+\Sigma(V_{\ell})
contains an interval of length
at least
yz/(2\ell v)>2y/v
for
n
sufficiently large, as required.
∎

We have therefore reduced the task of proving the lower bound in Theorem
1.6
to Lemma
5.5
. The strategy for proving Lemma
5.5

is now as follows. We partition
A
into two subsets
A_{1}
and
A_{2}

of size
z/(16\ell)
, observing that we can choose
A_{1}
and
A_{2}

to be
y^{1/4}/(128\ell)
-diverse by Lemma
5.4
.
We then show that
\Sigma(A_{1})
contains elements
in many different congruence classes modulo
t
for all
t
in
A_{2}
, allowing
us to apply Lemma
2.5
repeatedly (as in the proofs of our results on completeness)
to conclude that each element of
A_{2}
introduces many new elements to the set of subset sums.

The next lemma is the main step in the proof of
Lemma
5.5
. Recall that

\Sigma_{t}(A)=\left\{\sum_{x\in S}x\bmod{t}:S\subseteq A\right\}

and
\xi
is the absolute constant defined in Lemma
2.4
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
t\in[y/v,2y/v)
. Let
A
be a subset of

Y_{v}
of size
z/(16\ell)
which is
y^{1/4}/(128\ell)
-diverse. Then

|\Sigma_{t}(A)|\geq\min(\xi,32/\ell)t
.

To show that the set of mod
t

subset sums is large, we prove the following structural lemma, stating that the set of
elements whose inclusion does not expand the set of mod
t
subset
sums must either be small or additively structured.
We will then use this additive structure
to show that the corresponding set in
\mathbb{Z}
must contain a
small number of integers of the form
qu
, which we will see is impossible.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
t
be an integer. Let
A\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{t}

be such that
8d<|A|<\xi t
. Let
G_{d}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{t}

be the set of
x
such that
|(A+x)\cup A|\leq|A|+d
. Then either

G_{d}
is contained in a proper subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{t}
,
|G_{d}|\leq\frac{20|A|}{(|A|/2d)^{1.02}}

or there is a subgroup
H
of
\mathbb{Z}_{t}
such that
G_{d}

is contained in a set of size at most
128d
which is an arithmetic progression of
H
-cosets.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.7
,
kG_{d}\subseteq G_{kd}
, where
kG_{d}=\{x_{1}+x_{2}+\cdots+x_{k}:x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{k}\in G_{d}\}
. Let
i=\lfloor\log_{2}(|A|/2d)\rfloor
and let
k=2^{i}
, noting that
kd\leq|A|/2
. Therefore, applying
Lemma
2.6
to
G_{kd}
, we get

|kG_{d}|\leq|G_{kd}|\leq\frac{|A|^{2}}{|A|-kd}\leq 2|A|.

(15)

Assume that
G_{d}
is not contained in a proper subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{t}
. Let
j
be such that
0\leq j<i
. Since
0\in G_{d}
by definition, we have
G_{d}\subset 2^{j}G_{d}
, so
0\in 2^{j}G_{d}
and
2^{j}G_{d}
is not contained in a proper subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{t}
. Thus,
2^{j}G_{d}
is not contained in a coset of a proper subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{t}
. By Lemma
2.3
,

|2^{i}G_{d}|\geq\min\{t,2^{i-j-1}|2^{j}G_{d}|\}=2^{i-j-1}|2^{j}G_{d}|
,
where we used that
|2^{i}G_{d}|\leq 2|A|<2\xi t<t
from (
15
).
Thus,

|2^{j}G_{d}|\leq 2^{1-i+j}|2^{i}G_{d}|<2\xi t.

(16)

Assume now that
|2^{j+1}G_{d}|=|2^{j}G_{d}+2^{j}G_{d}|\leq 2.04|2^{j}G_{d}|

for some
2\leq j<i
. By Lemma
2.4
, there exists a proper subgroup
H
of
\mathbb{Z}_{t}
such that one of the following holds:

1.

2^{j}G_{d}
is contained in a set of size at most
\frac{\ell}{\ell-1}\cdot 1.04|2^{j}G_{d}|
which is an arithmetic progression of
H
-cosets of length
\ell\geq 2
,

2.

2^{j}G_{d}
meets exactly three
H
-cosets which are terms of an arithmetic progression of
H
-cosets of length
\ell
and
(\min(\ell,4)-1)|H|\leq 1.04|2^{j}G_{d}|
or

3.

2^{j}G_{d}
is contained in one
H
-coset.

We have already seen that the third case cannot happen, that is, that
2^{j}G_{d}
is not contained in a coset of a proper subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{t}
.

Suppose that we are in the second case. Then
2^{j}G_{d}
is contained in a union of three
H
-cosets, so
4G_{d}
is contained in a union of three
H
-cosets. Since
0\in G_{d}
and
G_{d}
is not contained in an
H
-coset, the image of
G_{d}
in
\mathbb{Z}_{t}/H
is a subset
S
of
\mathbb{Z}_{t}/H
of size at least
2
such that
0\in S
and
4S
has size at most
3
. This can only happen if
S
is contained in a subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{t}/H
of size at most
3
. In this case,
G_{d}
is contained in a subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{t}
of size at most
3|H|
. Since
|H|\leq(\min(\ell,4)-1)|H|\leq 1.04|2^{j}G_{d}|
, we have
3|H|\leq 3.12|2^{j}G_{d}|<6.24\xi t<t
, so
G_{d}
is contained in a proper subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{t}
, a contradiction. Thus, the second case cannot happen.

We now consider the first case, where
2^{j}G_{d}

is contained in a set of size at most
\frac{\ell}{\ell-1}\cdot 1.04|2^{j}G_{d}|\leq 2.08|2^{j}G_{d}|
which is
an arithmetic progression of
H
-cosets of length
\ell
. As
0\in G_{d}
,
this progression of
H
-cosets contains
0
. Let
m
be such that

H=\{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{t}:m|x\}
. Then the
H
-cosets can be identified
with elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
. The common difference of the progression
of
H
-cosets must be coprime to
m
, as otherwise
2^{j}G_{d}

would be contained in a proper subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{t}
. Thus, by rescaling if necessary,
we may assume that the common difference
of the progression of
H
-cosets is
1
. Let
P_{j}
be the interval in
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
which corresponds
to the
H
-cosets in the progression containing
2^{j}G_{d}
. Note
that
0\in G_{d}
, so that
2^{h-1}G_{d}\subseteq 2^{h}G_{d}
for all
h
.
Hence, for each
h\leq j
, we can choose intervals
P_{h}
around
0
in
\mathbb{Z}_{m}

such that
2^{h}(G_{d}/H)\subseteq P_{h}
,
P_{h}\subseteq P_{j}
and
2^{j-h}P_{h}\subseteq P_{j}
.
The length
\ell
of
P_{j}
is at most
m/2
, since otherwise
|2^{j}G_{d}|\geq\frac{t/2}{2.08}
, contradicting (
16
).
We can thus deduce that, for all
h\leq j
,
P_{h}
is an interval of length at most
1+2^{h-j}(|P_{j}|-1)
around
0
in
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
, since, for two intervals
I
,
I^{\prime}
around 0 of length
|I|\leq|I^{\prime}|\leq m/2
with
2I\subseteq I^{\prime}
, we have
|I|\leq(|I^{\prime}|+1)/2
.
Hence,
G_{d}/H
is a subset of
an interval of length at most
1+(|P_{j}|-1)/2^{j}
. Since
G_{d}
is not contained in a proper subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{t}
,
(|P_{j}|-1)/2^{j}\geq 1
. Thus, we have
1+(|P_{j}|-1)/2^{j}\leq|P_{j}|/2^{j-1}
. Therefore,
G_{d}
is contained in a union of
H
-cosets of size at most

|H||P_{j}|/2^{j-1}\leq\frac{\ell}{\ell-1}\cdot 1.04|2^{j}G_{d}|\cdot 2^{1-j}\leq\frac{\ell}{\ell-1}\cdot 1.04\cdot 2^{3-i}|A|\leq 128d,

where, in the second inequality, we used (
15
) and (
16
) and, in the final inequality, we used that
i=\lfloor\log_{2}(|A|/2d)\rfloor
and
\ell\geq 2
.

If there does not exist
j\in[2,i)
such that
|2^{j+1}G_{d}|\leq 2.04|2^{j}G_{d}|
, then

|2^{i}G_{d}|\geq 2.04^{i-2}|G_{d}|\geq(|A|/2d)^{1.02}|G_{d}|/10.

Combining this with (
15
), we deduce that
|G_{d}|\leq\frac{20|A|}{(|A|/2d)^{1.02}}
.
∎

Besides Lemma
5.7
, we need several other ingredients for the proof of Lemma
5.6
. We begin with the following result, which will also be useful to us in subsequent sections. For this section, the key corollary is that if
A
is
k
-diverse for
k\geq d-1
, then
\Sigma_{d}(A)=\mathbb{Z}_{d}
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
d
be a positive integer. Let
A
be a set of integers such that, for each
d^{\prime}|d
, at least
d^{\prime}-1
elements of
A
are not divisible by
d^{\prime}
. Then
\Sigma_{d}(A)=\mathbb{Z}_{d}
. Furthermore, if
A
contains at least
d-1
elements which are not divisible by
d
, then
\Sigma_{d}(A)
contains a non-zero subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
.

Proof.

We will use the following simple claim.

Claim.
If
S
is a subset of
\mathbb{Z}_{t}
and
X\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{t}
is such that
|(S+x)\cup S|=|S|
for all
x\in X
, then
S
is a union of cosets of the subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{t}
spanned by
X
.

Proof.
If
|(S+x)\cup S|=|S|
, then
S+x=S
. Thus, by induction, we have that
S+x_{1}+\dots+x_{k}=S
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in X
. In particular, we have
S+\langle X\rangle=S
, where
\langle X\rangle
is the subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{t}
spanned by
X
. Since
S+\langle X\rangle
is a union of cosets of
\langle X\rangle
, we obtain the desired conclusion. ∎

Note that
\Sigma_{t}(S\cup\{x\})=\Sigma_{t}(S)\cup(\Sigma_{t}(S)+x)
. From the claim, if
S
is a multiset in
\mathbb{Z}_{t}
and
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{t}
is coprime to
t
, then we have
|\Sigma_{t}(S\cup\{x\})|\geq\min(|\Sigma_{t}(S)|+1,t)
, as either
|\Sigma_{t}(S\cup\{x\})|\geq|\Sigma_{t}(S)|+1
or
\Sigma_{t}(S)
is a union of cosets of
x\mathbb{Z}_{t}=\mathbb{Z}_{t}
, so
\Sigma_{t}(S)=\mathbb{Z}_{t}
. Thus, if
B=\{b_{1},\dots,b_{t-1}\}
is a multiset of size
t-1
consisting
of elements in
\mathbb{Z}_{t}
coprime to
t
, then
\Sigma_{t}(B)=\mathbb{Z}_{t}
.
Indeed, this follows easily from the fact that
\Sigma_{t}(\emptyset)=\{0\}
and, for each
i\geq 1
,
|\Sigma_{t}(\{b_{1},\dots,b_{i}\})|\geq\min(|\Sigma_{t}(\{b_{1},\dots,b_{i-1}\})|+1,t)
.

Suppose now that
A
is a set of integers such that, for each
d^{\prime}|d
, at least
d^{\prime}-1
elements of
A
are not divisible by
d^{\prime}
.
We will prove that
\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(A)=\mathbb{Z}_{d^{\prime}}
for all
d^{\prime}|d
by induction on the number of prime factors (counted
with repeats) of
d^{\prime}
. When
d^{\prime}
is a prime, the conclusion follows
from the observation above. Assume now that the conclusion holds whenever
d^{\prime}
has at most
j
prime factors, for some
j\geq 1
.

Let
d^{\prime}
be a divisor of
d
with
j+1
prime factors. Let
A_{0}
be the multiset of elements in
A
not divisible by
d^{\prime}
, considered modulo
d^{\prime}
. By our assumption,
A_{0}
has size at least
d^{\prime}-1
. Observe that
\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(A)=\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(A_{0})
. Assume that
|\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(A_{0})|<d^{\prime}
. Let
\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(0)=\{0\}
. We consider the following iterative process. At step
i\geq 1
, we choose
a_{i}\in A_{i-1}

so that
|(\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(i-1)+a_{i})\setminus\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(i-1)|
is maximized and
let
\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(i)=\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(i-1)\cup(\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(i-1)+a_{i})
and
A_{i}=A_{i-1}\setminus\{a_{i}\}
. Note that we consider the
A_{i}
as a multiset of elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{d^{\prime}}
and the
\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(i)
as subsets of
\mathbb{Z}_{d^{\prime}}
.

Let
i\leq|A_{0}|
be the first
step where
|\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(i)|\leq|\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(i-1)|
. Note that
i
must exist since, otherwise,
|\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(|A_{0}|)|\geq|\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(0)|+|A_{0}|=1+|A_{0}|\geq d^{\prime}
, contradicting our assumption that
|\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(|A_{0}|)|=|\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(A_{0})|<d^{\prime}
.
Since
i
is the first step with
|\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(i)|\leq|\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(i-1)|
, we must have that
|\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(j)|\geq|\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(j-1)|+1
for all
j<i
. Thus,
|\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(i)|\geq 1+i-1=i
. In step
i
, we have
|(\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(i-1)+a)\cup\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(i-1)|=|\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(i-1)|
for all
a\in A_{i-1}
, so, by the claim,
\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(i-1)
is a union of cosets of the subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{d^{\prime}}
spanned by
A_{i-1}
. Let
d^{\prime\prime}
be the largest divisor of
d^{\prime}
which divides all elements in
A_{i-1}
. Then the subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{d^{\prime}}
spanned by
A_{i-1}
is
d^{\prime\prime}\mathbb{Z}_{d^{\prime}}
and we have that
\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(i-1)
is a union of
d^{\prime\prime}\mathbb{Z}_{d^{\prime}}
-cosets.
Note that
d^{\prime\prime}\neq d^{\prime}
, since the elements of
A_{0}
are not divisible by
d^{\prime}
and
A_{i-1}
contains at least one element in
A_{0}
. Thus,
d^{\prime\prime}<d^{\prime}
and, hence,
d^{\prime\prime}
has at most
j
prime factors. By the induction hypothesis,
\Sigma_{d^{\prime\prime}}(A_{0})=\mathbb{Z}_{d^{\prime\prime}}
.
Note that
\Sigma_{d^{\prime\prime}}(A_{0})=\Sigma_{d^{\prime\prime}}(\{a_{1},\dots,a_{i-1}\})
, since
all remaining elements of
A_{0}
are divisible by
d^{\prime\prime}
. Thus,
\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(i-1)
contains an element in each
d^{\prime\prime}\mathbb{Z}_{d^{\prime}}
-coset of
\mathbb{Z}_{d^{\prime}}
. Since
\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(i-1)
is a union of
d^{\prime\prime}\mathbb{Z}_{d^{\prime}}
-cosets and
contains an element in each
d^{\prime\prime}\mathbb{Z}_{d^{\prime}}
-coset of
\mathbb{Z}_{d^{\prime}}
,

\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(i-1)
contains all elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{d^{\prime}}
. Thus,
\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(A)=\Sigma_{d^{\prime}}(i-1)=\mathbb{Z}_{d^{\prime}}
, completing the induction.

For the second statement, observe, by the claim, that if
|\Sigma_{d}(S\cup\{x\})|=|\Sigma_{d}(S)|
, then
\Sigma_{d}(S)
is a union of cosets of
x\mathbb{Z}_{d}
and, as
0\in\Sigma_{d}(S)
, we have that
\Sigma_{d}(S)
contains the subgroup
x\mathbb{Z}_{d}
of
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
. Thus, if
A
contains at least
d-1
integers
a_{1},\dots,a_{d-1}
not divisible by
d
and
\Sigma_{d}(A)
does not contain a non-zero subgroup of

\mathbb{Z}_{d}
, then we must have
|\Sigma_{d}(\{a_{1},\dots,a_{i}\})|\geq|\Sigma_{d}(\{a_{1},\dots,a_{i-1}\})|+1
for all
i\geq 1
. But then
|\Sigma_{d}(A)|\geq 1+(d-1)=d
, which means that
\Sigma_{d}(A)
equals
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
.
∎

We remark that the condition in the above lemma is tight, since if
d
is prime and
A
consists of
d-2
elements congruent to
1
modulo
d
, then
\Sigma_{d}(A)
does not contain any non-zero subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
.

The next lemma gives an upper bound on the number of integers coprime to
W(r)/\gcd(W(r),m)
in an arithmetic progression. Note that all integers of the form
qu
where
u|m
and
\gcd(q,W(r)m)=1
are coprime to
W(r)/\gcd(W(r),m)
. The proof of this lemma uses the Selberg sieve and may be found in Appendix
B.1
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
r
and
n
be sufficiently large positive integers and
m\in[n,\binom{n}{2}]
. Let
X
be an arithmetic progression of size

|X|\geq r^{1/16}
with common difference
b\leq n
. Then the number of elements
of
X
which are coprime to
W(r)/\gcd(W(r),m)
is at most

\frac{256|X|\log\log n}{\log r}.

Furthermore, when
b=1
, the number of elements
of
X
which are coprime to
W(r)/\gcd(W(r),m)
is at most

256|X|\prod_{p|W(r),p\nmid m}(1-1/p).

Given a cyclic group
\mathbb{Z}_{t}
and an interval of integers
[x,x+t)
, we have a natural identification
\psi_{t}:\mathbb{Z}_{t}\to[x,x+t)
, where
\psi_{t}(u)
is the unique integer in
[x,x+t)
which is congruent to
u
modulo
t
. The next lemma shows that under this identification, for a subgroup
H
of
\mathbb{Z}_{t}
, a progression of
H
-cosets is identified with a large subset of a union of long arithmetic progressions of integers. A variant of this lemma goes back at least to the proof of Roth’s Theorem
[
33
]
.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
H
be a subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{t}
and let
R
be an arithmetic progression of
H
-cosets. Consider the image
\psi_{t}(R)
of
R
under the identification
\psi_{t}:\mathbb{Z}_{t}\to[x,x+t)
. Then
\psi_{t}(R)
is contained in a set of size at most
3|R|
which is a union of arithmetic progressions of integers, each of length at least
|R|^{1/3}
.

Proof.

First observe that the image under
\psi_{t}
of each
H
-coset is an arithmetic progression. Thus, if
|H|\geq|R|^{1/3}
, then
\psi_{t}(R)
is a union of arithmetic progressions, each of length at least
|R|^{1/3}
.

Assume now that
|H|<|R|^{1/3}
. Let
H=d\mathbb{Z}_{t}
for some divisor
d
of
t
. Let
\ell=|R|/|H|\geq|R|^{2/3}
. By definition, we can write
R=\bigcup_{i\in[\ell]}(x+iy+H)
for some
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}_{t}
and
y\notin H
. For each
H
-coset
x+iy+H
, we can choose a representative
z_{i}
for the coset in
[x,x+d)
. Let
P=(z_{1},z_{2},\dots,z_{\ell})
. We have that
P\pmod{d}
forms a progression of common difference
u
in
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
. We show that
P
is contained in a set of size at most
3\ell
which is a union of progressions of integers, each of length at least
\sqrt{\ell}=\sqrt{|R|/|H|}\geq|R|^{1/3}
. From this claim, the conclusion of the lemma easily follows.

We claim that there exists
s\in[1,\lfloor\sqrt{\ell}\rfloor]
such that
su
is congruent to an integer in
[-d/\lceil\sqrt{\ell}\rceil,d/\lceil\sqrt{\ell}\rceil]
modulo
d
. Partition
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
into a union of intervals
[kd/\lceil\sqrt{\ell}\rceil,(k+1)d/\lceil\sqrt{\ell}\rceil)
for
k=0,1,\dots,\lceil\sqrt{\ell}\rceil-1
.
Suppose that there does not exist
s\in[1,\lfloor\sqrt{\ell}\rfloor]
such that
su\pmod{d}\in[-d/\lceil\sqrt{\ell}\rceil,d/\lceil\sqrt{\ell}\rceil]
. Then
u,2u,\dots,\lfloor\sqrt{\ell}\rfloor u\pmod{d}
must be contained in the intervals
[kd/\lceil\sqrt{\ell}\rceil,(k+1)d/\lceil\sqrt{\ell}\rceil)
for
k=1,\dots,\lceil\sqrt{\ell}\rceil-2
. Since
\lfloor\sqrt{\ell}\rfloor\geq\lceil\sqrt{\ell}\rceil-1
, the pigeonhole principle implies that there are
1\leq s^{\prime}<s^{\prime\prime}\leq\lfloor\sqrt{\ell}\rfloor
such that
s^{\prime}u\pmod{d}
and
s^{\prime\prime}u\pmod{d}
are in the same interval
[kd/\lceil\sqrt{\ell}\rceil,(k+1)d/\lceil\sqrt{\ell}\rceil)
. Then
s^{\prime\prime}-s^{\prime}\in[1,\lfloor\sqrt{\ell}\rfloor]
and
(s^{\prime\prime}-s^{\prime})u
is congruent to an integer
v
in
[-d/\lceil\sqrt{\ell}\rceil,d/\lceil\sqrt{\ell}\rceil]
modulo
d
, contradicting our assumption.

Suppose now that
s\in[1,\lfloor\sqrt{\ell}\rfloor]
is such that
su
is congruent to an integer in
[-d/\lceil\sqrt{\ell}\rceil,d/\lceil\sqrt{\ell}\rceil]
modulo
d
. Since
P\pmod{d}
forms a progression of common difference
u
in
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
,
we can partition
P
into
s
subsets
P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{s}
such that
P_{i}\pmod{d}
is a progression with common difference
su
in
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
.
Each set
P_{i}
can be greedily partitioned into progressions of integers with common difference
v
such that all of the progressions in the partition, except the first and last ones, have length at least
\lceil\sqrt{\ell}\rceil
.
By extending arbitrarily the progressions with length less than
\lceil\sqrt{\ell}\rceil
, we obtain that
P
is contained in a union of arithmetic progressions of integers, each of length at least
\lceil\sqrt{\ell}\rceil
, where the size of the union is at most
(\lceil\sqrt{\ell}\rceil-1)\cdot 2s+|P|\leq 3|P|
. This verifies the desired claim.
∎

We will also need the following simple lemma in the proof of Lemma
5.6
.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
A
be a multiset of elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{t}
and let
d
be a divisor of
t
. Then, for any
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{t}/d\mathbb{Z}_{t}
such that
\Sigma_{t}(A)\cap(u+d\mathbb{Z}_{t})\neq\emptyset
,

|\Sigma_{t}(A)\cap(u+d\mathbb{Z}_{t})|\geq|\Sigma_{t}(A\cap d\mathbb{Z}_{t})|.

Proof.

Let
S_{u}=\Sigma_{t}(A)\cap(u+d\mathbb{Z}_{t})
. For all non-zero
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{t}/d\mathbb{Z}_{t}
, if
S_{u}\neq\emptyset
, then we can find an element
x
in
S_{u}
which is a sum of distinct elements of
A
which are not in
d\mathbb{Z}_{t}
. Thus, each element of
x+\Sigma_{t}(A\cap d\mathbb{Z}_{t})
can be written a sum of distinct elements in
A
, so
x+\Sigma_{t}(A\cap d\mathbb{Z}_{t})\subseteq\Sigma_{t}(A)
. It is also clear that
x+\Sigma_{t}(A\cap d\mathbb{Z}_{t})\subseteq u+d\mathbb{Z}_{t}
, so
x+\Sigma_{t}(A\cap d\mathbb{Z}_{t})\subseteq S_{u}
. If
u=0\in\mathbb{Z}_{t}/d\mathbb{Z}_{t}
, then letting
x=0
, we have
x+\Sigma_{t}(A\cap d\mathbb{Z}_{t})=\Sigma_{t}(A\cap d\mathbb{Z}_{t})\subseteq S_{u}
. Thus, if
S_{u}
is non-empty, then
|S_{u}|\geq|\Sigma_{t}(A\cap d\mathbb{Z}_{t})|
.
∎

We can now prove Lemma
5.6
. We recall the statement, that if
t\in[y/v,2y/v)
and
A
is a subset of

Y_{v}
of size
z/(16\ell)
which is
y^{1/4}/(128\ell)
-diverse, then
|\Sigma_{t}(A)|\geq\min(\xi,32/\ell)t
.

Proof of Lemma
5.6
.

We consider the following iterative process. Let
\Sigma_{t}(0)=\{0\}
and
A_{0}=A
. At each step
i\geq 1
,
we pick an element
a_{i}
in
A_{i-1}
and let
\Sigma_{t}(i)=\Sigma_{t}(i-1)\cup(\Sigma_{t}(i-1)+a_{i})

and
A_{i}=A_{i-1}\setminus\{a_{i}\}
. In particular,
\Sigma_{t}(i)=\Sigma_{t}(\{a_{1},\dots,a_{i}\})\subseteq\Sigma_{t}(A)
for all
i\leq|A|
. Let
d_{i}=\gcd(A_{i-1})
. For
i\leq|A|/2
,
A_{i-1}
is a subset of
Y_{v}
of size at least
\frac{|A|}{2}\geq\frac{z}{32\ell}>64(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)\frac{y}{r\log r}
, where we used (
14
) and assumed that
r
is sufficiently large in terms of
\ell
. Thus,
\{vx:x\in A_{i-1}\}
is a subset of
Y
of size larger than
64(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)\frac{y}{r\log r}
whose elements are divisible by
vd_{i}
. By Lemma
5.3
, we obtain
vd_{i}\leq y^{1/16}
and all elements of
A_{i-1}
have the form
qu
where
u\mid(m/v)
,
u\leq y^{1/16}/v\leq y^{1/16}
,
d_{i}|u
and
\gcd(q,W(r)m)=1
. We will run the above process for at most
|A|/2
steps, so we may assume that
i\leq|A|/2
and these conclusions hold throughout.

For each
i
, we say that step
i
is either a
growth phase
, an
unsaturated phase
or a
saturated phase
. Note that the cosets of
d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{t}
can be indexed by elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{d_{i}}
. For each
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{d_{i}}
, let
S_{u}=\Sigma_{t}(i-1)\cap(u+d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{t})
. We say that
i
is
a growth phase
if there exists
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{d_{i}}
such that
S_{u}
is non-empty and has size at most
y^{3/4}
. We say that
i
is
an unsaturated phase
if it is not a growth phase and there exists
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{d_{i}}
such that
y^{3/4}<|S_{u}|<\frac{\xi t}{d_{i}}
. Finally, if step
i
is neither a growth phase nor an unsaturated phase, then it is a
saturated phase
.

Next we describe how
a_{i}
is chosen. Let
\Sigma(d,i-1)=\{\sum_{j\in S}a_{j}\pmod{t}:S\subseteq[i-1]\cap\{j:d|a_{j}\}\}
. Then
\Sigma(d,i-1)
is a subset of the subgroup
d\mathbb{Z}_{t}
of
\mathbb{Z}_{t}
, which can be identified with
\mathbb{Z}_{t/d}
. We identify
\Sigma(d,i-1)
with a subset of
\mathbb{Z}_{t/d}
. Similarly, we can identify
A_{i-1}
with a subset of
\mathbb{Z}_{t/d_{i}}
.
If
i
is a growth phase, we pick
a_{i}
such that
|\Sigma(d_{i},i)|-|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|

is maximized. Otherwise, if
i
is not a growth phase, we pick

a_{i}
such that
|\Sigma_{t}(i)|-|\Sigma_{t}(i-1)|
is maximized.

The following claims capture the key steps in the proof.

Claim 1.
The number of growth phases among the first
|A|/2
steps is at most
(256\ell y^{3/4}/z+\log_{3/2}t+2)(\log_{2}y^{1/16}+1)
.

Claim 2.
If
i\leq|A|/2
is an unsaturated phase, then
|\Sigma_{t}(i)|-|\Sigma_{t}(i-1)|>2^{12}y/z
.

Claim 2 is the most important step in the proof and will take up most of our time. However, before proving these claims, let us see how Lemma
5.6
follows from combining them.

First, suppose that there exists
i\leq|A|/2
such that
i
is a saturated phase. By Lemma
5.8
, since
A
is
y^{1/4}/(128\ell)
-diverse and
d_{i}<y^{1/4}/(128\ell)
,
\Sigma_{d_{i}}(\{a_{1},\dots,a_{i-1}\})=\Sigma_{d_{i}}(A)=\mathbb{Z}_{d_{i}}
. Hence,
S_{u}
is non-empty for all
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{d_{i}}
. Since
i
is a saturated phase, we have that
|S_{u}|\geq\frac{\xi t}{d_{i}}
for all
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{d_{i}}
, so
|\Sigma_{t}(i-1)|=\sum_{u\in\mathbb{Z}_{d_{i}}}|S_{u}|\geq\xi t
. Therefore,
|\Sigma_{t}(A)|\geq|\Sigma_{t}(i-1)|\geq\xi t
, as desired.

Next, suppose that no
i\leq|A|/2
is a saturated phase. In this case, if
i\leq|A|/2
is not a growth phase, it must be an unsaturated phase and, by Claim 2, we have
|\Sigma_{t}(i)|>|\Sigma_{t}(i-1)|+2^{12}y/z
. Since Claim 1 implies that there are at least
|A|/2-(256\ell y^{3/4}/z+\log_{3/2}t+2)(\log_{2}y^{1/16}+1)
unsaturated phases in the first
|A|/2
steps and
|A|=z/(16\ell)
, we have

|\Sigma_{t}(A)|\geq|\Sigma_{t}(|A|/2)|\geq\frac{2^{12}y}{z}\cdot\left(\frac{z}{32\ell}-(256\ell y^{3/4}/z+\log_{3/2}t+2)(\log_{2}y^{1/16}+1)\right)\geq\frac{2^{12}y}{64\ell}\geq\frac{32t}{\ell},

as required.
∎

We next give the proofs of Claims 1 and 2, beginning with the simpler of the two.

Proof of Claim 1.

First, we show that in each step
i\leq|A|/2
, if
|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|<|A_{i-1}|/2
, then

|\Sigma(d_{i},i)|-|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|\geq\max_{a\in A_{i-1}}|(\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)+a)\setminus\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|\geq|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|/2,

(17)

while if
|A_{i-1}|/2\leq|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|\leq y^{3/4}
, then

|\Sigma(d_{i},i)|-|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|\geq\max_{a\in A_{i-1}}|(\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)+a)\setminus\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|\geq|A_{i-1}|/8.

(18)

The first bound (
17
