) follows directly from Lemma
2.6
, since the set of elements
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{t/d_{i}}
for which

|(\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)+a)\setminus\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|<|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|/2

has size at most
2|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|<|A_{i-1}|
.

For the second bound (
18
), assume, for the sake of contradiction, that for some step
i\leq|A|/2
where
|A_{i-1}|/2\leq|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|\leq y^{3/4}
,
|\Sigma(d_{i},i)|-|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|<|A_{i-1}|/8
. Then, for all
a\in A_{i-1}
, we have
|(\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)+a)\setminus\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|<|A_{i-1}|/8
. Let
k=\left\lfloor\frac{4|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|}{|A_{i-1}|}\right\rfloor
and let

T=\{a\in\mathbb{Z}_{t/d_{i}}:|(\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)+a)\setminus\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|<|A_{i-1}|/8\}.

We have
A_{i-1}\subseteq T
and
0\in T
. By Lemma
2.7
, for any
a\in kT
,

|(\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)+a)\setminus\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|<|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|/2.

Hence, by Lemma
2.6
, we have
|k(A_{i-1}\cup\{0\})|\leq|kT|\leq 2|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|
. Using that
vd_{i}\leq y^{1/16}
and
t\geq y/v
, we have
|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|\leq y^{3/4}<t/(2d_{i})
, so
|k(A_{i-1}\cup\{0\})|<t/d_{i}
. Identified as a subset of
\mathbb{Z}_{t/d_{i}}
,
A_{i-1}
is not a subset of any proper subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{t/d_{i}}
by the definition of
d_{i}
, so
A_{i-1}\cup\{0\}
is not contained in any coset of a proper subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{t/d_{i}}
. Therefore, by Lemma
2.3
, we have

|A_{i-1}\cup\{0\}|\leq\frac{2}{k+1}|k(A_{i-1}\cup\{0\})|\leq\frac{4|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|}{k+1}<\frac{4|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|}{4|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|/|A_{i-1}|}=|A_{i-1}|,

a contradiction.

Using (
17
) and (
18
), we may quickly complete the proof of Claim 1. Note that, by Lemma
5.11
, for any
u
such that
S_{u}
is non-empty,
|S_{u}|\geq|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|
. Thus, if
i
is a growth phase, then
|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|\leq y^{3/4}
.
Note that
d_{i}|d_{i+1}
, so either
d_{i+1}=d_{i}
or
d_{i+1}\geq 2d_{i}
.
As
d_{i}\leq y^{1/16}
for
i\leq|A|/2
,
d_{i}
can change at most
1+\log_{2}y^{1/16}

times in the first
|A|/2
steps.
By (
17
), if
|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|<|A_{i-1}|/2
, then
|\Sigma(d_{i},i)|-|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|\geq|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|/2
. Thus, for each period among the first

|A|/2
steps where
d_{i}
remains constant, the number of steps
where
|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|<|A_{i-1}|/2
is at most
1+\log_{3/2}t
,
since, in each such step,
|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|
grows by a factor of at least
3/2
. For the remaining steps in this period, where
|A_{i-1}|/2\leq|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|\leq y^{3/4}
, (
18
) implies that
|\Sigma(d_{i},i)|-|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|\geq|A_{i-1}|/8\geq z/(256\ell)
in each step, so there are at most

1+256\ell y^{3/4}/z
more growth phases where
d_{i}

stays constant. Thus, the number of growth phases among the first
|A|/2

steps in each period where
d_{i}
stays constant is at most
256\ell y^{3/4}/z+\log_{3/2}t+2
.
Since
d_{i}
can change at most
1+\log_{2}y^{1/16}
times in the first
|A|/2
steps, there are
at most
(256\ell y^{3/4}/z+\log_{3/2}t+2)(\log_{2}y^{1/16}+1)
growth phases in the
first
|A|/2
steps. ∎

Finally, we give the proof of Claim 2, thereby completing the proof of Lemma
5.6
.

Proof of Claim 2.

Let
i
be an unsaturated phase with
i\leq|A|/2
. Assume, for the sake of contradiction, that
|\Sigma_{t}(i)|-|\Sigma_{t}(i-1)|\leq 2^{12}y/z
. Since
i
is not a growth phase and not a saturated phase, there exists
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{d_{i}}
such that
y^{3/4}<|S_{u}|<\frac{\xi t}{d_{i}}
.

We now view
S_{u}
and
A_{i-1}
as subsets of
\mathbb{Z}_{t/d_{i}}
. Note that, by the definition of
d_{i}
,
A_{i-1}
is not a subset of any proper subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{t/d_{i}}
. Let
B
be the set of elements
a
of
\mathbb{Z}_{t/d_{i}}
such that
|(S_{u}+a)\setminus S_{u}|\leq 2^{12}y/z
. By our choice of
a_{i}
and our assumption that
|\Sigma_{t}(i)|-|\Sigma_{t}(i-1)|\leq 2^{12}y/z
, we have
A_{i-1}\subseteq B
. Since
|S_{u}|<\frac{\xi t}{d_{i}}
and
|S_{u}|>y^{3/4}>8\cdot 2^{12}y/z
by (
14
), we can apply Lemma
5.7
to conclude that either the set
B
is contained in a proper subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{t/d_{i}}
,
B
has size at most
\frac{20(2^{13}y/z)^{1.02}}{|S_{u}|^{0.02}}
or there is a subgroup
H
of
\mathbb{Z}_{t/d_{i}}
such that
B
is contained in a set of size at most
2^{20}y/z
which is an arithmetic progression of
H
-cosets.
The first possibility cannot hold, since
B
contains
A_{i-1}
which is not a subset of any proper subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{t/d_{i}}
. The second possibility also cannot hold, since

\frac{20(2^{13}y/z)^{1.02}}{|S_{u}|^{0.02}}\leq\frac{2^{5+13\cdot 1.02}y^{1.005}}{z^{1.02}}<|A|/2\leq|A_{i-1}|\leq|B|,

where we used the bound
|S_{u}|>y^{3/4}
.
Therefore, there is a subgroup
H
of
\mathbb{Z}_{t/d_{i}}
such that

A_{i-1}
, identified as a subset of
\mathbb{Z}_{t/d_{i}}
, is contained in a set
R
of size at most
2^{20}y/z
which is an arithmetic progression of
H
-cosets. We can identify the elements
of
\mathbb{Z}_{t/d_{i}}
with elements in
[y/v,y/v+t)\supseteq[y/v,2y/v)

which are divisible by
d_{i}
. Under this identification,
R
is identified with a set of integers which contains
A_{i-1}
.

By Lemma
5.10
, under the above identification, the image of
R
is contained in a set of integers of size at most
3|R|
which is a union of arithmetic progressions
P_{s}
,
s\in\mathcal{S}
, of integers, each of length at least
|R|^{1/3}
. We have
|R|\geq|A_{i-1}|\geq z/(32\ell)
. Thus,
A_{i-1}
is contained in a set of size at most
2^{22}y/z
which is a union of arithmetic progressions
P_{s}
of integers, each of length at least
(z/(32\ell))^{1/3}\geq r^{1/16}
, by (
14
) and (
35
) from Appendix
B.2
.
Recall that
A_{i-1}
is a subset of
[y/v,2y/v)
consisting of elements of the form
qu
, where
u|m
,
u\leq y^{1/16}
,

d_{i}|u
and
\gcd(q,W(r)m)=1
. Note that each element of the form
qu
where
u|m
and
\gcd(q,W(r)m)=1
is coprime to
W(r)/\gcd(W(r),m)
. Since each arithmetic progression
P_{s}
has length at least
r^{1/16}
and common difference at most
n
, Lemma
5.9
implies that the number of elements in
P_{s}
of the form
qu
, where
u|m
,
u\leq y^{1/16}
and
\gcd(q,W(r)m)=1
, is at most

256|P_{s}|\cdot\frac{\log\log n}{\log r}.

Thus, the number of elements of
R
(identified with a subset of
[y/v,y/v+t)
) of the form
qu
, where
u|m
,
u\leq y^{1/16}
and
\gcd(q,W(r)m)=1
, is at most

256(\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}|P_{s}|)\cdot\frac{\log\log n}{\log r}\leq 256\cdot\frac{2^{22}y}{z}\cdot\frac{\log\log n}{\log r}.

We claim that

256\cdot\frac{2^{22}y}{z}\cdot\frac{\log\log n}{\log r}<\frac{z}{32l}.

This holds if
r\geq 10\log m/\log\log m
, since then, from (
34
) in Appendix
B.2
,
\tau(r,m)\geq 1/(4\log r)
and

\displaystyle\frac{\ell y\log\log n}{z^{2}\log r}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{100\ell yr^{2}(\log\log n)}{(\log r)(m/\phi(m))^{2}y^{2}\tau(r,m)^{2}}\leq\frac{100\ell r^{2}(\log\log n)}{(\log r)\tau(r,m)^{2}(m/\phi(m))^{2}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\tau(r,m)(m/\phi(m))}{15mr}}

\displaystyle\leq 100\ell\sqrt{\frac{64r^{3}(\log r)(\log\log n)^{2}}{m(m/\phi(m))^{3}}}\leq 100\ell\sqrt{64c^{3}}\leq 2^{-40},

where we used (
14
) in the first inequality, the definition of
y
in the second inequality, the bound
\tau(r,m)\geq 1/(4\log r)
in the third inequality, the bound
r\leq c\psi(n,m)=c\frac{m^{1/3}(m/\phi(m))}{(\log n)^{1/3}(\log\log n)^{2/3}}
in the fourth inequality and, in the last inequality, we assumed a sufficiently small choice of
c
(depending on
\ell
). Next, assume that
r<10\log m/\log\log m
. In this case, we have that
r=c{\cal R}(n,m)=c\rho(n,m)
. Furthermore,
m\geq\frac{n^{2}}{(\log n)^{2}}
as, for
\frac{n^{3/2}(\log\log n)^{1/2}}{(\log n)^{1/2}}\leq m\leq\frac{n^{2}}{(\log n)^{2}}
,
r=c\rho(n,m)=\Theta\left(\frac{n^{2}/\phi(m)}{\log(n^{2}/\phi(m))}\right)
by Claim
B.3
, so
r>10\log m/\log\log m
for sufficiently large
n
, contradicting our assumption. We also have that
\tau(r,m)\geq(\phi(m)/m)/(2\log r)
by (
32
) in Appendix
B.2
, so

\displaystyle\frac{\ell y\log\log n}{z^{2}\log r}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{100\ell r^{2}(\log\log n)}{(\log r)\tau(r,m)^{2}(m/\phi(m))^{2}}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\tau(r,m)(m/\phi(m))}{15mr}}

\displaystyle\leq 100\ell\sqrt{\frac{8r^{3}(\log r)(\log\log n)^{2}}{m}}\leq 100\ell\sqrt{\frac{10^{5}(\log n)^{3}}{n^{2}/(\log n)^{2}}}\leq 2^{-40},

assuming that
n
is sufficiently large, where in the third inequality we used
r<10\log m/\log\log m<20\log n/\log\log n
.
Thus, in both cases,

256\cdot\frac{2^{22}y}{z}\cdot\frac{\log\log n}{\log r}<\frac{z}{32\ell}.

This is a contradiction since there are at least
\frac{z}{32\ell}
integers of the form

qu
where
u|m,u\leq y^{1/16}
and
\gcd(q,W(r)m)=1

contained in
R
. Hence, in each step
i\leq|A|/2
which is an unsaturated phase,
|\Sigma_{t}(i)|
grows by at least
2^{12}y/z
.
∎

Using Lemma
5.6
, we can now give the proof of Lemma

5.5
, thus completing our proof of the lower bound in Theorem
1.6
. Again we recall the statement, that if
\ell=\lceil 32/\xi\rceil

and
A
is a subset of
Y_{v}
of size
z/(8\ell)
which is
y^{1/4}/(32\ell)
-diverse, then
|\Sigma(A)|\geq 3yz/(4\ell^{2}v)

and
\Sigma(A)
is not a subset of an arithmetic progression with common difference greater than
1
.

Proof of Lemma
5.5
.

Using Lemma
5.4
, we can partition
A
into two sets
A_{1},A_{2}
such that
|A_{1}|=|A_{2}|=z/(16\ell)

and
A_{1}
is
y^{1/4}/(128\ell)
-diverse.
By Lemma
5.6
, for each
t
in
A_{2}
,
|\Sigma_{t}(A_{1})|\geq\min(\xi,32/\ell)t
.
Recall that we chose
\ell=\lceil 32/\xi\rceil
, so

|\Sigma_{t}(A_{1})|\geq 32t/\ell
. Therefore, by repeated applications of Lemma
2.5
,

|\Sigma(A)|=|\Sigma(A_{1}\cup A_{2})|\geq\sum_{t\in A_{2}}\frac{32t}{\ell}\geq|A_{2}|\cdot\frac{32y}{\ell v}>\frac{yz}{\ell^{2}v}.

Finally, we prove that
\Sigma(A)
is not contained in an arithmetic
progression with common difference
d
larger than
1
. Indeed,
if
\Sigma(A)
were contained in an arithmetic progression with common
difference
d
larger than
1
, then all elements of
\Sigma(A)

would be in the same congruence class modulo
d
, which in turn implies that all elements of

A
would be divisible by
d
.
But this is impossible since
A
is
y^{1/4}/(32\ell)
-diverse.
∎

5.2
Proof of the upper bound in Theorem
1.6

In this section, we show how to improve on the construction of Alon and Erdős
[
2
]
described in the introduction. We begin with the following simple claim.

Claim 5.12
.

There exists a positive constant
\kappa

such that the following holds. For each positive integer
m
, let
d_{m}
be the product of all the primes at most
(\log m)/64
which are not prime divisors of
m
, where
d_{m}=1
if the product is over an empty set. Then, for
m
sufficiently large,
d_{m}\leq m^{1/32}
,
\gcd(d_{m},m)=1
and
\frac{m}{\phi(m)}\cdot\frac{d_{m}}{\phi(d_{m})}\geq\kappa\log\log m
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that
d_{m}\leq m^{1/32}
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\frac{m}{\phi(m)}\cdot\frac{d_{m}}{\phi(d_{m})}

\displaystyle\geq\prod_{p\leq(\log m)/64}\frac{p}{p-1}\geq\exp\left(\sum_{p\leq(\log m)/64}\frac{1}{p}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\exp(\log\log((\log m)/64)-\kappa^{\prime})\geq\kappa\log\log m,

for some absolute constants
\kappa^{\prime},\kappa
.
∎

We are now ready to prove the upper bound in Theorem
1.6
. For the sake of easy reference, we recall the statement, that, for all
n
sufficiently large and
m\in[n,\binom{n}{2}]
,

f(n,m)=O\left({\cal R}(n,m)\right),

where
{\cal R}(n,m)=\min\left(\psi(n,m),\rho(n,m)\right)
. Here
\psi(n,m)=\frac{m^{1/3}(m/\phi(m))}{(\log n)^{1/3}(\log\log n)^{2/3}}
and
\rho(n,m)
is the smallest positive integer
\rho
such that
\rho\prod_{p|W(\rho)m}(1-1/p)^{-1}\geq n^{2}/\phi(m)
, where
W(\rho)
is the product of the first
\rho
primes. We also recall from the previous subsection that when
m=O\left(\frac{n^{3/2}(\log\log n)^{1/2}}{(\log n)^{1/2}}\right)
, we have
{\cal R}(n,m)=\Theta\left(\psi(n,m)\right)
and when
m=\Omega\left(\frac{n^{3/2}(\log\log n)^{1/2}}{(\log n)^{1/2}}\right)
, we have
{\cal R}(m,n)=\Theta(\rho(n,m))
.

Proof of the upper bound in Theorem
1.6
.

We first consider the case where
m\leq\frac{n^{3/2}(\log\log n)^{1/2}}{(\log n)^{1/2}}
.
Let
r=C\psi(n,m)=\frac{Cm^{1/3}(m/\phi(m))}{(\log n)^{1/3}(\log\log n)^{2/3}}

for a sufficiently large constant
C
. Note that
r\geq\frac{n^{1/3}}{(\log n)^{2/3}}
.
Our aim is to construct an
r
-coloring of
[1,n-1]
such that
the set of subset sums of each color class does not contain
m
. We will do this in four steps.

Step 1.
For
k=2,3,\dots,\frac{r}{2}
, we form a color
class
S_{1}(k)=\{\lceil\frac{m}{k+1}\rceil,\lceil\frac{m}{k+1}\rceil+1,\dots,\lfloor\frac{m}{k}\rfloor\}\cap[n-1]
, while,
for
k=1
, we take
S_{1}(1)=\{\lceil\frac{m}{2}\rceil,\lceil\frac{m}{2}\rceil+1,\dots,m-1\}\cap[n-1]
. As defined, the color
classes may overlap, but we can safely assign any element in the overlap to any color class that includes it.
Crucially, no subset sum of
S_{1}(k)
can contain
m
, since the sum
of at most
k
elements from
S_{1}(k)
is less than
m
, while any
sum of
k+1
elements from
S_{1}(k)
is larger than
m
. Let
X_{1}=\bigcup_{k=1}^{r/2}S_{1}(k)
.

Step 2.
For each of the first
r/4
primes
p
which are coprime to
m
, we form a color class

S_{2}(p)=\{kp:k\in\mathbb{N}\}\cap[1,n-1]
, noting that no subset sum of

S_{2}(p)
can contain
m
, since each element of
\Sigma(S_{2}(p))

is a multiple of
p
. Let
X_{2}=\bigcup_{p\leq p_{r/4},p\nmid m}S_{2}(p)
.

Step 3.
Let
R=[1,n-1]\setminus(X_{1}\cup X_{2})
. The construction in
[
2
]
also uses color classes like those defined in Steps 1 and 2. They then arbitrarily partition the remaining elements
R
so that the sum of the elements in each of the classes is smaller than
m
. For our improvement, we need to be more careful. Note that elements in the remainder set
R
are natural numbers

t\leq\frac{2m}{r}
such that
t
is coprime to
W(r/4)/\gcd(W(r/4),m)
, that is, all prime divisors of
t
which are at most
p_{r/4}
are also prime divisors of
m
. In particular,
t
is coprime to the integer
d_{m}
given by Claim
5.12
, since
d_{m}
has only prime factors at most
(\log m)/64<r/4
which are not prime divisors of
m
.

With
\kappa
also as in Claim
5.12
, we next show that there exists a multiple
d
of
d_{m}
such that

\kappa(\phi(m)/m)r(\log\log n)/64\leq d\leq\kappa(\phi(m)/m)r(\log\log n)/32,

(19)

\gcd(d,m)=1
and the largest prime factor of
d
is at most
p_{r/4}
. Let
x=\frac{\kappa(\phi(m)/m)r\log\log n}{50d_{m}}
. Note that
x\geq\frac{\kappa r}{100d_{m}\log\log n}>n^{1/4}
, since
r\geq n^{1/3}/(\log n)^{2/3}
and
d_{m}\leq m^{1/32}\leq n^{1/16}
. Since
m
has at most
\log m\leq 2\log n
distinct prime factors,
there exists a prime
p<n^{1/100}
such that
p
does not divide
m
. Let
k
be the smallest positive integer such that
x/p^{k}<n^{1/100}
. Then
x/p^{k-1}\in[n^{1/100},n^{2/100}]
and, by the prime number theorem, the interval
[(1-1/100)x/p^{k-1},(1+1/100)x/p^{k-1}]
has at least
10^{-3}n^{1/100}/(\log n)
primes for sufficiently large
n
. Thus, there exists a prime
p^{\prime}
in this interval which does not divide
m
. Then
p^{\prime}p^{k-1}\in[(1-1/100)x,(1+1/100)x]
and
\gcd(p^{\prime}p^{k-1},m)=1
, since
p^{\prime}
and
p
are primes which do not divide
m
. We can now verify that
d=p^{\prime}p^{k-1}d_{m}
satisfies (
19
),
\gcd(d,m)=1
and the largest prime factor of
d
is at most
2n^{2/100}<p_{r/4}
(noting that all prime factors of
d_{m}
are at most
(\log m)/64<2n^{2/100}
). Since
d_{m}|d
, we also have
d/\phi(d)\geq d_{m}/\phi(d_{m})
. Furthermore, all elements of
R
are coprime to
d
, since any element in
R
is coprime to
W(r/4)/\gcd(W(r/4),m)
, whereas
d
is coprime to
m
and all prime factors of
d
are at most
p_{r/4}
.

Fix
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{\times}
. Then there exist
k
integers congruent to
s\pmod{d}
that sum to
m
only
if
sk\equiv m\pmod{d}
. Let
x_{s}
be the positive integer in
[d]
that is congruent to
s^{-1}m\pmod{d}
. Consider now the color classes

S_{s,1}=\left\{t:\,t\in R,t\equiv s\,(\bmod\,d),t\geq\frac{m}{x_{s}}\right\},

S_{s,2}=\left\{t:\,t\in R,t\equiv s\,(\bmod\,d),\frac{m}{d+x_{s}}\leq t<\frac{m}{x_{s}}\right\}.

If a sum of
k
elements in
S_{s,1}
is equal to
m\pmod{d}
,
then
k\geq x_{s}
. But then the sum of the
k
elements is larger than
m
. Similarly, if a sum of
k
elements in
S_{s,2}

is equal to
m\pmod{d}
, then
k=x_{s}
or
k\geq d+x_{s}
.
But the sum of
k
elements is less than

m
if
k=x_{s}
and larger than
m
if
k\geq d+x_{s}
. Thus,

m\notin\Sigma(S_{s,1})\cup\Sigma(S_{s,2})
. Note that in this step we have in total defined
2\phi(d)
color classes of the form
S_{s,1}
and
S_{s,2}
for
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{\times}
.

Step 4.
Let
R^{\prime}=R\setminus(\bigcup_{s\in\mathbb{Z}_{d}^{\times}}(S_{s,1}\cup S_{s,2})).
Then all elements of
R^{\prime}
are less than
m/d
. Thus, if we arbitrarily
partition
R^{\prime}
into sets of size at most
d
, then no set contains
a subset sum which is equal to
m
. Hence, we need at most
|R^{\prime}|/d

colors to color
R^{\prime}
so that no color class contains
m
as a subset
sum. Recall that any element in
R^{\prime}
is coprime to
W(r/4)/\gcd(W(r/4),m)
. By the second part of Lemma
5.9
, applied to the interval
[1,m/d]
, we have

\displaystyle|R^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq 256(m/d)\prod_{p|W(r/4),p\nmid m}(1-1/p)

\displaystyle\leq 256(m/d)\prod_{p|W(r/4)}(1-1/p)\prod_{p|m,p\leq r/4}(1-1/p)^{-1}

\displaystyle<\frac{500m(m/\phi(m))}{d\log r},

where we used that
r
is sufficiently large, so that
\prod_{p|W(r/4)}(1-1/p)\leq 1.1/\log r
, and
\prod_{p|m,p\leq r/4}(1-1/p)^{-1}\leq m/\phi(m)
.
Therefore, the number of color classes used in Step 4 is at most

\displaystyle\frac{500m(m/\phi(m))}{d^{2}\log r}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{64^{2}\cdot 500m(m/\phi(m))^{3}}{\kappa^{2}r^{2}(\log r)(\log\log n)^{2}}

\displaystyle<\frac{r}{16},

where the first inequality follows from (
19
) and in the second inequality we have assumed that the constant
C
is chosen sufficiently large.

Combining all four steps, the total number of colors we have used is at most

\displaystyle\frac{r}{2}+\frac{r}{4}+2\phi(d)+\frac{r}{16}\leq\frac{13r}{16}+\frac{2md}{\kappa\phi(m)\log\log n}\leq\frac{7r}{8},

where we have used Claim
5.12
, so that
d/\phi(d)\geq d_{m}/\phi(d_{m})\geq\kappa(\log\log m)\phi(m)/m\geq\kappa(\log\log n)\phi(m)/m
, and the bound (
19
). Thus, we
can use at most
r
colors to color
[1,n-1]
such that no monochromatic
subset sum is equal to
m
, as required.

Next we consider the case
m\in\left[\frac{n^{3/2}(\log\log n)^{1/2}}{(\log n)^{1/2}},\binom{n}{2}\right]
.
Let
r=C\rho(n,m)
, where
C
is a sufficiently large absolute constant. We
construct the coloring as follows.

Step 1.
For each of the first
7r/8
primes
p
that do not divide
m
, we construct a color class
S_{1}(p)=\{kp:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
. Let
X_{1}=\bigcup_{p\leq p_{7r/8},p\nmid m}S_{1}(p)
.

Step 2.
Let
R=[1,n-1]\setminus X_{1}
. The set
R
consists of those integers less than
n
which are coprime to
W(7r/8)/\gcd(W(7r/8),m)
. By Lemma
5.9
, the number of elements of
R
is at most

\displaystyle 256n\prod_{p|W(7r/8),p\nmid m}(1-1/p)

\displaystyle\leq 256n\prod_{p|W(\rho(n,m))m}(1-1/p)\cdot\prod_{p|\gcd(m,W(\rho(n,m)))}(1-1/p)^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq 256n(m/\phi(m))\prod_{p|W(\rho(n,m))m}(1-1/p),

where in the first inequality we used that
7r/8=7C\rho(n,m)/8>\rho(n,m)
, which holds by choosing the constant
C
to be sufficiently large, and in the second inequality we used
\prod_{p|\gcd(m,W(\rho(n,m)))}(1-1/p)^{-1}\leq\prod_{p|m}(1-1/p)^{-1}=m/\phi(m)
.

Since each element of
R
is less than
n
, if a color class contains at most

m/n
elements, then no sum of elements from the color class can equal

m
. Thus, we can use at most

\frac{|R|}{m/n}\leq 256n^{2}/\phi(m)\cdot\prod_{p|W(\rho(n,m))m}(1-1/p)\leq 256\rho(n,m)

colors to color the elements of
R
so that no monochromatic subset
sum equals
m
. The second inequality follows from the definition of
\rho(n,m)
, which is the smallest positive integer such that
\rho(n,m)\prod_{p|W(\rho(n,m))m}(1-1/p)^{-1}\geq n^{2}/\phi(m)
. Hence, the total number of colors we used is at most

\frac{7r}{8}+256\rho(n,m)\leq r,

assuming that
C
is a sufficiently large absolute constant.
∎

6
Long homogeneous progressions in subset sums

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.9
,
strengthening Theorem
1.8
, Szemerédi and Vu’s result
[
40
]
on arithmetic progressions in subset sums, by showing that the progression may be taken to be homogeneous. For our application to the Erdős–Graham problem in Section
7
, we will need a somewhat technical strengthening of this result, for which it will be useful to have the notation

\Sigma^{[h]}(A)=\left\{\sum_{s\in S}s:S\subseteq A,|S|\leq h\right\}.

The main result of this section, which includes Theorem
1.9
as a special case, is now as follows. To gain some intuition, we remark that for a typical set
A
which is not dominated by multiples of an integer at least
2
, we will simply have
d=1
.

Theorem 6.1
.

There exists an absolute constant
C>0

such that the following holds. For any subset
A
of
[n]
of size
m\geq C\sqrt{n}
,
there exists
d\geq 1
such that, for
A^{\prime}=\{x/d\,:\,x\in A,d|x\}
and
k=2^{50}n/m
,
\Sigma^{[k]}(A^{\prime})
contains an interval of length at least
n
.
Furthermore,

|A|-|A^{\prime}|\leq 2^{30}(\log n)^{3}+\frac{2^{30}n}{m}.

Theorem
1.9
immediately follows from Theorem
6.1
by noticing that
\Sigma(A)
contains the set
\{dy\,:\,y\in\Sigma^{[k]}(A^{\prime})\}
, which is a homogeneous arithmetic progression with common difference
d
.

As a crucial step in the proof of Theorem
6.1
, we first show that subsets of
\mathbb{Z}_{b}
satisfying a diversity condition have a large set of subset sums.
We will need to use the mod
b
analogue of
\Sigma^{[h]}(A)
, namely,

\Sigma_{b}^{[h]}(A)=\left\{\sum_{s\in S}s\bmod b:S\subseteq A,|S|\leq h\right\}.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
b
be a positive integer. Let
A
be a subset of
\mathbb{Z}_{b}
of size
m\in(80(\log b)^{2},b]
such that, for each
d|b
with
d\in[2,4b/m]
, there are at least
64(\log b)^{2}+8d
elements in
A
which are not divisible by
d
. Let
k=1280b/m
.
Then

|\Sigma_{b}^{[k+1]}(A)|\geq\min(m^{2}/256,b/4).

Proof.

Let
A^{\prime}
be a uniformly random subset of
A
of size
3m/4
and
A^{\prime\prime}=A\setminus A^{\prime}
. Let
{\cal E}_{1}
be the event that for some
d|b
with
d\in[2,4b/m]
, there are at most
8(\log b)^{2}+d
elements in
A^{\prime}
which are not divisible by
d
. Recall that for each
d|b
and
d\in[2,4b/m]
, at least
64(\log b)^{2}+8d
elements of
A
are not divisible by
d
. By the Chernoff bound for hypergeometric distributions, the probability that there are at most
8(\log b)^{2}+d
elements in
A^{\prime}
which are not divisible by
d
is at most
\exp(-64(\log b)^{2}/32)\leq 1/b^{2}
. By taking a union bound over all
d|b
, the probability that
{\cal E}_{1}
happens is then at most
1/b<1
. We may therefore fix a choice of
A^{\prime}
and
A^{\prime\prime}
so that
{\cal E}_{1}
does not hold.

We consider the following iterative process. Let
\Sigma_{b}(0)=A^{\prime\prime}
and let
A_{0}=A^{\prime}
. At each step

i\geq 1
, we pick an element
a_{i}
in
A_{i-1}
and let
A_{i}=A_{i-1}\setminus\{a_{i}\}
and

\Sigma_{b}(i)=\Sigma_{b}(i-1)\cup(\Sigma_{b}(i-1)+a_{i})
. Observe that the elements in
\Sigma_{b}(i)
can be written as the sum of one element in
A^{\prime\prime}
and a subset of
A^{\prime}
of size at most
i
, so
\Sigma_{b}(i)\subseteq\Sigma_{b}^{[i+1]}(A)
. Let
d_{i}|b
be such that
\langle A_{i-1}\rangle=d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{b/d_{i}}
, where
\langle X\rangle
denotes the subgroup generated by
X
. Note that, by definition,
d_{i}|d_{j}
if
i\leq j
. Furthermore,
|A_{i-1}|\leq b/d_{i}
and
|A_{i-1}|=3m/4-i+1\geq m/4
for
i\leq m/2
, so
d_{i}\leq 4b/m
for
i\leq m/2
. We will run the above process for at most
m/2
steps, so we only consider
i\leq m/2
throughout.

For each
i
, we say that step
i
is either a
growth phase
, an
unsaturated
phase
or a
saturated phase
. For each
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{b}/d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{d_{i}}
, let
S_{u}=\Sigma_{b}(i-1)\cap(u+d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b})
.
We then say that step
i
is a
growth phase
if
there exists
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{b}/d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{d_{i}}

such that
S_{u}
is
non-empty and has size at most
|A_{i-1}|/4
. We say that step
i
is
an
unsaturated phase
if it is not a growth phase and there
exists
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{b}/d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b}
such that
\frac{|A_{i-1}|}{4}<|S_{u}|<\frac{b}{4d_{i}}
.
Finally, if step
i
is neither a growth phase nor an unsaturated phase, then
it is a
saturated phase
. We remark that if
d_{i}=d
for all steps
i
in an interval
[x,y]
, then the interval can be partitioned into three (possibly empty) intervals such that the steps in the first interval are all growth phases, the steps in the second interval are all unsaturated phases and the steps in the third interval are all saturated phases.

We next discuss how to pick
a_{i}
, which depends on the type of phase. For
d|b
, let
\Sigma(d,i-1)=\Sigma_{b}(\{a_{1},\dots,a_{i-1}\}\cap d\mathbb{Z}_{b})
.
If step
i
is a growth phase, we pick
a_{i}
which maximizes
|\Sigma(d_{i},i)|-|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|
. If step
i
is an unsaturated or saturated phase, we pick
a_{i}
which maximizes
|\Sigma_{b}(i)|-|\Sigma_{b}(i-1)|
.

The following claims record important properties of the process we have defined.

Claim 1.
The first
(\log b)^{2}
steps are not growth phases.

Proof.
Consider
i\leq(\log b)^{2}
. Note that
i\leq m/2
. Since, for each
d|b
with
d\in[2,4b/m]
, there are more than
8(\log b)^{2}
elements in
A^{\prime}
which are not divisible by
d
, there must be at least
7(\log b)^{2}
elements in
A_{i-1}
which are not divisible by
d
. Hence,
d_{i}=1
. Thus, there is only one coset
u
of
d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b}
in
\mathbb{Z}_{b}
and
|S_{u}|\geq|\Sigma_{b}(0)|=m/4\geq|A_{i-1}|/4
, so
i
is not a growth phase. ∎

Claim 2.
There are at most
20(\log b)(\log(4b/m))
growth phases among the first
m/2
steps.

Proof.
Suppose that step
i
is a growth phase. By Lemma
5.11
, for
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{b}/d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b}
, if
S_{u}
is non-empty, then
|S_{u}|\geq|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|
. Since we are in a growth phase, there is some
u
such that
S_{u}
is non-empty and
|S_{u}|\leq|A_{i-1}|/4
. This implies that
|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|\leq|A_{i-1}|/4
. By Lemma
2.6
,
the set of
a\in d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b}
such that
|(\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)+a)\setminus\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|\leq\frac{1}{2}|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|

has size at most
2|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|\leq|A_{i-1}|/2
. Thus, there
exists
a_{i}\in A_{i-1}
such that
|\Sigma(d_{i},i)|\geq\frac{3}{2}|\Sigma(d_{i},i-1)|
.
As
|\Sigma(d_{i},i)|\leq b
for all
i
, there can be at most
1+\log_{3/2}b
successive growth
phases with
d_{i}=d
. Since
d_{1}\leq d_{2}\leq\dots\leq d_{m/2}\leq 4b/m
and
d_{i+1}\geq 2d_{i}
if
d_{i+1}\neq d_{i}
,
d_{i}
can take at most
1+\log_{2}(4b/m)
distinct values. This shows that there can be at most
(1+\log_{3/2}b)(1+\log_{2}(4b/m))<20(\log b)(\log(4b/m))

growth phases among the first
m/2
steps. ∎

Claim 3.
Let
i
be an unsaturated phase. Then
|\Sigma_{b}(i)|-|\Sigma_{b}(i-1)|\geq\frac{|A_{i-1}|}{16}
.

Proof.
Note that if
a\in A_{i-1}
, then
a\in d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b}
, since
A_{i-1}\subseteq d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b}
. Thus, for each
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{b}/d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b}
and
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{b}
,
x+a\in u+d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b}
if and only if
x\in u+d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b}
. Hence,
(\Sigma_{b}(i-1)+a)\cap(u+d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b})=(\Sigma_{b}(i-1)\cap(u+d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b}))+a=S_{u}+a
and
((\Sigma_{b}(i-1)+a)\setminus\Sigma_{b}(i-1))\cap(u+d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b}))=(S_{u}+a)\setminus S_{u}
. Let
u_{0}
be such that
|S_{u_{0}}|=\max_{u:|S_{u}|<b/4d_{i}}|S_{u}|
. We have

\displaystyle|(\Sigma_{b}(i-1)+a)\setminus\Sigma_{b}(i-1)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{u\in\mathbb{Z}_{b}/d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b}}|((\Sigma_{b}(i-1)+a)\setminus\Sigma_{b}(i-1))\cap(u+d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b})|

\displaystyle=\sum_{u\in\mathbb{Z}_{b}/d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b}}|(S_{u}+a)\setminus S_{u}|

\displaystyle\geq|(S_{u_{0}}+a)\setminus S_{u_{0}}|.

Let
k_{i}=1+\left\lfloor\frac{4|S_{u_{0}}|}{|A_{i-1}|}\right\rfloor\in\left[2,\frac{8|S_{u_{0}}|}{|A_{i-1}|}\right]
, noting that
|S_{u_{0}}|>|A_{i-1}|/4
since step
i
is not a growth phase.
Let
P_{i-1}\subseteq d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b}
be the set of elements

a\in d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b}
such that
|(S_{u_{0}}+a)\setminus S_{u_{0}}|<\frac{1}{2k_{i}}|S_{u_{0}}|
.
Note that
0\in P_{i-1}
and, by Lemma
2.7
, for any
a\in k_{i}P_{i-1}
,

|(S_{u_{0}}+a)\setminus S_{u_{0}}|<\frac{1}{2}|S_{u_{0}}|
.
Therefore, by Lemma
2.6
,
|k_{i}P_{i-1}|\leq 2|S_{u_{0}}|
.
Suppose now that
A_{i-1}\subseteq P_{i-1}
. Since
d_{i}
is defined so that
\langle A_{i-1}\rangle=d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{b/d_{i}}
,
A_{i-1}
is not a subset of a proper subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{b/d_{i}}
and, since
0\in P_{i-1}
,
P_{i-1}
is not contained in a
coset of a proper subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{b/d_{i}}
. Thus, by Lemma

2.3
and the fact that
|k_{i}P_{i-1}|\leq 2|S_{u_{0}}|<\frac{b}{2d_{i}}<|\mathbb{Z}_{b/d_{i}}|
,
|P_{i-1}|\leq\frac{2|k_{i}P_{i-1}|}{k_{i}}\leq\frac{4}{k_{i}}|S_{u_{0}}|
. However, by our choice of
k_{i}
,

|A_{i-1}|>\frac{4}{k_{i}}|S_{u_{0}}|
, which is a contradiction. Therefore,
A_{i-1}\cap P_{i-1}^{c}\neq\emptyset
and, since
a_{i}
is chosen so that
|\Sigma_{b}(i)|-|\Sigma_{b}(i-1)|=|(\Sigma_{b}(i-1)+a_{i})\setminus\Sigma_{b}(i-1)|
is maximized,

\displaystyle|(\Sigma_{b}(i-1)+a_{i})\cup\Sigma_{b}(i-1)|

\displaystyle\geq|\Sigma_{b}(i-1)|+\frac{1}{2k_{i}}|S_{u_{0}}|

\displaystyle\geq|\Sigma_{b}(i-1)|+\frac{|A_{i-1}|}{16|S_{u_{0}}|}|S_{u_{0}}|

\displaystyle=|\Sigma_{b}(i-1)|+\frac{|A_{i-1}|}{16}.

Thus, over any unsaturated phase
i
,
|\Sigma_{b}(i)|-|\Sigma_{b}(i-1)|\geq\frac{|A_{i-1}|}{16}
. ∎

Claim 4.
For each step
i\leq m/2
,
S_{u}=\Sigma_{b}(i-1)\cap(u+d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b})
is non-empty for every
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{b}/d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b}
.

Proof.
The claim holds trivially if
d_{i}=1
. Assume that
d_{i}>1
. Since
i\leq m/2
, we have
d_{i}|b
and
d_{i}\leq 4b/m
. For each
d|d_{i}
with
d>1
, we have
d|b
and
d\in[2,4b/m]
, so
A^{\prime}
contains at least
d-1
elements which are not divisible by
d
. By Lemma
5.8
,

\Sigma_{d_{i}}(\{a_{1},\dots,a_{i-1}\})=\Sigma_{d_{i}}(A^{\prime})=\mathbb{Z}_{d_{i}},

where we used that the elements of
A^{\prime}\setminus\{a_{1},\dots,a_{i-1}\}
are all divisible by
d_{i}
. The claim follows upon noting that we can identify
\mathbb{Z}_{b}/d_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{b}
with
\mathbb{Z}_{d_{i}}
and, under this identification,
S_{u}
is non-empty if and only if
u\in\Sigma_{d_{i}}(\{a_{1},\dots,a_{i-1}\})
. ∎

We now complete the proof of the lemma using these claims. First, assume that there is no saturated phase
i
with
i\leq\min(m/2,k)
. Then, among the first
\min(m/2,k)
steps, from Claims 1 and 2, at least
\max(\min(m/2,k)-20(\log b)(\log(4b/m)),\min((\log b)^{2},k))
steps are unsaturated phases. Note that if
k=1280b/m\leq 80(\log b)^{2}
, then
(\log b)^{2}\geq k/80
and

\max(\min(m/2,k)-20(\log b)(\log(4b/m)),\min((\log b)^{2},k))\geq\min((\log b)^{2},k)\geq k/80.

If
k=1280b/m\geq 80(\log b)^{2}
, then
20(\log b)(\log(4b/m))\leq k/2
and
20(\log b)(\log(4b/m))\leq m/4
by our assumption on
m
, so

\displaystyle\max(\min(m/2,k)-20(\log b)(\log(4b/m)),\min((\log b)^{2},k))

\displaystyle\geq\min(m/2,k)-20(\log b)(\log(4b/m))

\displaystyle\geq\min(m/4,k/2).

In either case, we have

\max(\min(m/2,k)-20(\log b)(\log(4b/m)),\min((\log b)^{2},k))\geq\min(m/4,k/80).

For each step
i
which is an unsaturated phase, we have, by Claim 3, that
|\Sigma_{b}(i)|-|\Sigma_{b}(i-1)|\geq|A_{i-1}|/16\geq m/64
. Hence, recalling that
k=1280b/m
, we get

|\Sigma_{b}^{[k+1]}(A)|\geq|\Sigma_{b}(\min(m/2,k))|\geq\min\left(\frac{m}{4},\frac{k}{80}\right)\cdot\frac{m}{64}=\min\left(\frac{m^{2}}{256},\frac{b}{4}\right).

If, instead, there is a saturated phase
i_{0}
with
i_{0}\leq\min(m/2,k)
, then, for each
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{b}/d_{i_{0}}\mathbb{Z}_{b}
with
S_{u}=\Sigma_{b}(i_{0}-1)\cap(u+d_{i_{0}}\mathbb{Z}_{b})
non-empty,
|S_{u}|\geq\frac{b}{4d_{i_{0}}}
. But Claim 4 implies that
S_{u}
is non-empty for all
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{b}/d_{i_{0}}\mathbb{Z}_{b}
, so that

|\Sigma_{b}^{[k+1]}(A)|\geq|\Sigma_{b}(i_{0})|\geq\sum_{u\in\mathbb{Z}_{b}/d_{i_{0}}\mathbb{Z}_{b}}|S_{u}|\geq\frac{b}{4d_{i_{0}}}\cdot d_{i_{0}}=\frac{b}{4}.

Hence, the desired conclusion holds in both cases.
∎

Let
\ell=2^{15}
. We say that a subset
A
of
[n]
of size
m

is
nice
if the following conditions hold:

1.

There is no
d\in[2,8\ell n/m]
such that
all but at most
512\ell(\log n)^{2}+64\ell d
elements of
A
are divisible by
d
.

2.

For each dyadic interval
I_{j}=[2^{j-1},2^{j})\cap[n]
, either
|A\cap I_{j}|=0
or
|A\cap I_{j}|\geq 64\ell(\log n)
.

The next lemma says that any large subset of
[n]
contains a multiple of a large nice set.

Lemma 6.3
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that the following holds. Let
A
be a subset of
[n]
of size
m\geq Cn^{1/2}
.
Then there is an integer
d
and a set
A^{\prime}
of integers such that

•

A^{\prime}
is nice,

•

\{dx:x\in A^{\prime}\}\subseteq A
and

•

|A|-|A^{\prime}|\leq 1000\ell(\log n)^{3}+\frac{256\ell n}{m}
.

Proof.

We consider the following iteration. Let
A_{0}=A
and
n_{0}=n
. Note that
A_{0}\subseteq[n_{0}]
. For each
i\geq 0
, if
|A_{i}|<m/2
, we stop. If
A_{i}
is nice, we let
A^{\prime}=A_{i}
and stop. Otherwise,
A_{i}\subseteq[n_{i}]
is not nice and
|A_{i}|\geq m/2
. If (
1
)
does not hold, we let
A_{i+1}=\{x/d_{i}:x\in A_{i},d_{i}|x\}
, where

d_{i}\in[2,8\ell n/|A_{i}|]
is such that all but at most
512\ell(\log n)^{2}+64\ell d_{i}

elements of
A_{i}
are divisible by
d_{i}
. Note that
d_{i}\leq 8\ell n/|A_{i}|\leq 16\ell n/m
. Let
n_{i+1}=n_{i}/d_{i}
. Then

|A_{i+1}|\geq|A_{i}|-512\ell(\log n)^{2}-64\ell d_{i}

and
A_{i+1}\subseteq[n_{i+1}]
. If (
1
) holds and (
2
) does
not hold, we remove all elements in
A_{i}
which are contained
in dyadic intervals
I_{j}
with
|A\cap I_{j}|<64\ell(\log n)

and let
A_{i+1}
be the resulting set. Let
n_{i+1}=n_{i}
, so
A_{i+1}\subseteq[n_{i+1}]
, and let
d_{i}=1
. In this case,
|A_{i+1}|\geq|A_{i}|-64\ell(\log n)(1+\log_{2}n)
.

We show that we will always stop and output a nice set with the required properties. Let
s
be the step where we stop. Note that there can be at most
\log_{2}n
steps where (
1
) does not hold. Furthermore, the number of steps where (
2
) does not hold is at most one more than the number of steps where (
1
) does not hold. Thus, we have

|A_{s}|\geq|A_{0}|-(1+\log_{2}n)(512\ell(\log n)^{2}+64\ell(\log n)(1+\log_{2}n))-64\ell\sum_{i\leq s-1:d_{i}>1}d_{i}.

(20)

Furthermore,
n_{s}=n/(\prod_{i\leq s-1}d_{i})
, so
A_{s}\subseteq[n/(\prod_{i\leq s-1}d_{i})]
. We also have that

\displaystyle|A_{s}|

\displaystyle\geq|A_{s-1}|-\max(64\ell(\log n)(1+\log_{2}n),512\ell(\log n)^{2}+64\ell d_{s-1})

\displaystyle\geq|A_{s-1}|-512\ell(\log n)^{2}-1024\ell^{2}n/m

\displaystyle\geq m/4,

where we used that
d_{s-1}\leq 16\ell n/m
,
|A_{s-1}|\geq m/2
,
m\geq C\sqrt{n}
for a sufficiently large constant
C
and
n
is sufficiently large. Hence,

\prod_{i\leq s-1}d_{i}\leq n/|A_{s}|\leq 4n/m.

Since
d_{i}\geq 2
for each
i\leq s-1
with
d_{i}>1
, we have
\sum_{i\leq s-1:d_{i}>1}d_{i}\leq\prod_{i\leq s-1}d_{i}\leq 4n/m
. Hence, combining with (
20
),

|A_{s}|\geq|A_{0}|-(1+\log_{2}n)(512\ell(\log n)^{2}+64\ell(\log n)(1+\log_{2}n))-64\ell\cdot\frac{4n}{m}\geq m-1000\ell(\log n)^{3}-\frac{256\ell n}{m}\geq\frac{m}{2},

assuming that
m\geq C\sqrt{n}
for
C
sufficiently large and
n
is sufficiently large. This implies that the iteration stops at step
s
because
A_{s}
is nice. The set
A^{\prime}=A_{s}
then satisfies all of the required properties.
∎

We are now in a position to prove the main result of this section, Theorem
6.1
.

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

By Lemma
6.3
, we can find a nice set
A^{\prime}
and an
integer
d
such that
\{dx:x\in A^{\prime}\}\subseteq A
and

\displaystyle|A|-|A^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\leq 1000\ell(\log n)^{3}+\frac{256\ell n}{m}.

In particular,
|A^{\prime}|\geq 5|A|/8
for
n
sufficiently large. Partition
A^{\prime}
into
\ell
sets
A_{1}^{\prime},\dots,A_{\ell}^{\prime}

as follows. Let
A_{<}^{\prime}
be the set consisting of the
7|A^{\prime}|/8

smallest elements in
A^{\prime}
and let
A^{\prime}_{>}
be the remaining elements.
Partition
A^{\prime}_{<}
into
\ell
sets
A_{<,1}^{\prime},\dots,A_{<,\ell}^{\prime}
, each of size
|A^{\prime}_{<}|/\ell
, and partition
A^{\prime}_{>}
into
\ell
sets
A_{>,1}^{\prime},\dots,A_{>,\ell}^{\prime}
, each of size
|A^{\prime}_{>}|/\ell
, uniformly at random. Let
A_{i}^{\prime}=A_{<,i}^{\prime}\cup A_{>,i}^{\prime}
.

Let
b_{i,1},b_{i,2},\dots,b_{i,|A^{\prime}_{>,i}|}
be a uniformly random enumeration of
A^{\prime}_{>,i}
and we then define two sets
B_{i,1}=\{b_{i,1},b_{i,2},\dots,b_{i,|A^{\prime}_{>,i}|/2}\}
and
B_{i,2}=\{b_{i,|A^{\prime}_{>,i}|/2+1},\dots,b_{i,|A^{\prime}_{>,i}|}\}
.
Let
k^{\prime}=2560\ell n/m+1
. Theorem
6.1
will follow easily from the next two claims.

Claim 1.
Suppose that
C
and
n
are sufficiently large. Then, with probability at least
3/4
, for all
i\in[\ell]
and all
j\in[|A^{\prime}_{>,i}|/2+1,|A^{\prime}_{>,i}|]
,

|\Sigma_{b_{i,j}}^{[k^{\prime}]}(A^{\prime}_{<,i}\cup B_{i,1})|\geq\frac{b_{i,j}}{4}.

Claim 2.
Let
M(i)
be the sum of the largest
k^{\prime}
elements in
B_{i,2}
, let
\overline{M}(i)
be the sum of the largest
2k^{\prime}
elements in
A^{\prime}_{i}
and let
M
be the sum of the largest
2\ell k^{\prime}
elements in
A^{\prime}
. Then, with probability at least
3/4
, for all
i\in[\ell]
,

M(i)\geq\frac{M}{8\ell}

and

\overline{M}(i)\leq\frac{4M}{\ell}.

Before proving these claims, we show how to complete the proof of Theorem
6.1
assuming that their conclusions both hold, which happens with probability at least
1/2
. For any subset
J
of
[|A^{\prime}_{>,i}|/2+1,|A^{\prime}_{>,i}|]
of size
k^{\prime}
, let
J=\{j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{k^{\prime}}\}
for
j_{1}<j_{2}<\dots<j_{k^{\prime}}
. A straightforward adaptation of Lemma
2.5
shows that for any set of integers
A
and any integer
m\notin A
, we have
|\Sigma^{[h+1]}(A\cup\{m\})|\geq|\Sigma^{[h]}(A)|+|\Sigma_{m}^{[h]}(A)|
. For each
v\leq k^{\prime}
, apply this statement with
h=k^{\prime}+v-1
,
m=b_{i,j_{v}}
and
A=A^{\prime}_{<,i}\cup B_{i,1}\cup\{b_{i,j_{1}},\dots,b_{i,j_{v-1}}\}
to conclude that

\displaystyle|\Sigma^{[k^{\prime}+v]}(A^{\prime}_{<,i}\cup B_{i,1}\cup\{b_{i,j_{1}},\dots,b_{i,j_{v}}\})|

\displaystyle\geq|\Sigma^{[k^{\prime}+v-1]}(A^{\prime}_{<,i}\cup B_{i,1}\cup\{b_{i,j_{1}},\dots,b_{i,j_{v-1}}\})|+|\Sigma_{b_{i,j_{v}}}^{[k^{\prime}+v-1]}(A^{\prime}_{<,i}\cup B_{i,1}\cup\{b_{i,j_{1}},\dots,b_{i,j_{v-1}}\})|

\displaystyle\geq|\Sigma^{[k^{\prime}+v-1]}(A^{\prime}_{<,i}\cup B_{i,1}\cup\{b_{i,j_{1}},\dots,b_{i,j_{v-1}}\})|+|\Sigma_{b_{i,j_{v}}}^{[k^{\prime}]}(A^{\prime}_{<,i}\cup B_{i,1})|.

Thus,

|\Sigma^{[2k^{\prime}]}(A^{\prime}_{<,i}\cup B_{i})|\geq|\Sigma^{[2k^{\prime}]}(A^{\prime}_{<,i}\cup B_{i,1}\cup\{b_{i,j_{1}},\dots,b_{i,j_{k^{\prime}}}\})|\geq\sum_{v\leq k^{\prime}}|\Sigma_{b_{i,j_{v}}}^{[k^{\prime}]}(A^{\prime}_{<,i}\cup B_{i,1})|.

By Claim 1, we have, for each
v\leq k^{\prime}
, that
|\Sigma_{b_{i,j_{v}}}^{[k^{\prime}]}(A^{\prime}_{<,i}\cup B_{i,1})|\geq b_{i,j_{v}}/4
. Thus,

|\Sigma^{[2k^{\prime}]}(A^{\prime}_{<,i}\cup B_{i})|\geq\frac{1}{4}\sum_{v\leq k^{\prime}}b_{i,j_{v}}=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{j\in J}b_{i,j}.

By choosing
J
to be the set of indices of the
k^{\prime}
largest elements of
B_{i,2}
, we obtain that

|\Sigma^{[2k^{\prime}]}(A^{\prime}_{i})|\geq\frac{1}{4}M(i).

Therefore, by Claim 2, we have that, for all
i\in[\ell]
,

|\Sigma^{[2k^{\prime}]}(A^{\prime}_{i})|\geq\frac{M}{32\ell}\geq\frac{1}{128}\frac{4M}{\ell}.

Also by Claim 2,
\Sigma^{[2k^{\prime}]}(A_{i}^{\prime})\subseteq[\overline{M}(i)]\subseteq[4M/\ell]
.
Therefore, by Lemma
2.2
with
q=4M/\ell
and
n=q/128
,
\Sigma^{[2k^{\prime}]}(A^{\prime}_{1})+\cdots+\Sigma^{[2k^{\prime}]}(A^{\prime}_{\ell})

contains an interval of length at least

\frac{1}{256}\cdot\frac{4M}{\ell}\cdot\ell=\frac{1}{64}M\geq\frac{1}{128}\ell k^{\prime}m>n,

where we used that
M
is the sum of the largest
2\ell k^{\prime}<|A^{\prime}|/2
elements of
A^{\prime}
, so that
M\geq 2\ell k^{\prime}|A^{\prime}|/2>\ell k^{\prime}m/2
, the bound
k^{\prime}\geq\frac{2560\ell n}{m}
and
\ell=2^{15}
. Thus,
\Sigma^{[2\ell k^{\prime}]}(A^{\prime})
contains an interval of length at least
n
.
∎

Proof of Claim 1.

Assume that, for some
i\in[\ell]
and
j\in[|A^{\prime}_{>,i}|/2+1,|A^{\prime}_{>,i}|]
,

|\Sigma_{b_{i,j}}^{[k^{\prime}]}(A^{\prime}_{<,i}\cup B_{i,1})|<\min\left(\frac{m^{2}}{1024\ell^{2}},\frac{b_{i,j}}{4}\right).

(21)

Note that the size of the set
A^{\prime}_{<,i}\cup B_{i,1}
, considered as a subset of
\mathbb{Z}_{b_{i,j}}
, is at least
\frac{7|A^{\prime}|}{8\ell}>\frac{m}{2\ell}
and at most
\frac{|A^{\prime}|}{\ell}<\frac{m}{\ell}
, since all elements of
A^{\prime}_{<,i}
are smaller than
b_{i,j}
and, hence, are distinct modulo
b_{i,j}
. But then, since
\frac{m}{2\ell}>80(\log b_{i,j})^{2}
, Lemma
6.2
with
b=b_{i,j}
and
A=A^{\prime}_{<,i}\cup B_{i,1}
implies that if (
21
) holds, there must be some
d\in[2,8\ell n/m]
such that all but at most
64(\log n)^{2}+8d
elements of
A^{\prime}_{<,i}\cup B_{i,1}
are divisible by
d
.

Since
A^{\prime}
is nice, for each
d\in[2,8\ell n/m]
, at least
512\ell(\log n)^{2}+64\ell d

elements of
A^{\prime}
are not divisible by
d
. By the pigeonhole principle, we obtain that, for each
d\in[2,8\ell n/m]
, either
A^{\prime}_{>}
or
A^{\prime}_{<}
contains at least
256\ell(\log n)^{2}+32\ell d
elements not divisible by
d
.

Note that
A^{\prime}_{<,i}
is distributed as a uniformly random subset of
A^{\prime}_{<}
of size
|A^{\prime}_{<}|/\ell
and
B_{i,1}
is distributed as a uniformly random subset of
A^{\prime}_{>}
of size
|A^{\prime}_{>}|/(2\ell)
. Consider the event
{\cal E}(i)
that, for some
d\in[2,8\ell n/m]
,
A^{\prime}_{<,i}\cup B_{i,1}
contains at most
64(\log n)^{2}+8d
elements which are not divisible by
d
. By the argument of Lemma
5.4
and a union bound over all
d\leq 8\ell n/m\leq n
,
{\cal E}(i)
happens with probability at most
n\exp(-256\ell(\log n)^{2}/(16\ell))<1/n
. Thus, by a union bound over all
i\in[\ell]
, for sufficiently large
n
, the probability of the event
\bigcup_{i\in[\ell]}{\cal E}(i)
is at most
1/4
.

By our earlier observations, (
21
) cannot hold under the complement of the event
\bigcup_{i\in[\ell]}{\cal E}(i)
, so, provided
m\geq C\sqrt{n}
for sufficiently large
C
,

|\Sigma_{b_{i,j}}^{[k^{\prime}]}(A^{\prime}_{<,i}\cup B_{i,1})|\geq\min\left(\frac{m^{2}}{1024\ell^{2}},\frac{b_{i,j}}{4}\right)=\frac{b_{i,j}}{4}

holds for all
i\in[\ell]
and all
j\in[|A^{\prime}_{>,i}|/2+1,|A^{\prime}_{>,i}|]
with probability at least
3/4
.
∎

Proof of Claim 2.

Since
A^{\prime}
is nice, for each dyadic interval
I_{j}
in
[n]
, either
A^{\prime}
is disjoint from
I_{j}
or
A^{\prime}
intersects
I_{j}
in at least
64\ell(\log n)
elements. Note that there exists
j_{0}
such that the dyadic intervals
I_{j}
which intersect
A^{\prime}
have at least
64\ell(\log n)
common elements with
A^{\prime}_{>}
for
j>j_{0}
,
|I_{j_{0}}\cap A^{\prime}_{>}|<64\ell(\log n)
and
I_{j}\cap A^{\prime}_{>}=\emptyset
for
j<j_{0}
. As in the proof of Lemma
5.4
, Chernoff’s inequality for hypergeometric distributions implies that the probability
|A^{\prime}_{>,i}\cap I_{j}|>2|A^{\prime}_{>}\cap I_{j}|/\ell
is at most
\exp(-|A^{\prime}_{>}\cap I_{j}|/(3\ell))\leq\exp(-2\log n)
. Similarly, the probability that
|B_{i,2}\cap I_{j}|<|A^{\prime}_{>}\cap I_{j}|/(4\ell)
is at most
\exp(-|A^{\prime}_{>}\cap I_{j}|/(32\ell))\leq\exp(-2\log n)
. Thus, by a union bound, with probability at least
3/4
, for each
i\in[\ell]
and
j>j_{0}
,

|B_{i,2}\cap I_{j}|\geq\frac{1}{4\ell}|A^{\prime}_{>}\cap I_{j}|

(22)

and

|A^{\prime}_{>,i}\cap I_{j}|\leq\frac{2}{\ell}|A^{\prime}_{>}\cap I_{j}|.

(23)

Assume now that (
22
) and (
23
) hold for all
i\in[\ell]
and
j>j_{0}
. Note that since
k^{\prime}=2560\ell n/m+1
and
m\geq C\sqrt{n}
, we have
|A^{\prime}_{>}|>2\ell k^{\prime}+64\ell(\log n)
. Let
X
be the set consisting of the largest
2\ell k^{\prime}
elements of
A^{\prime}_{>}
, which is the same as the set of the largest
2\ell k^{\prime}
elements of
A^{\prime}
. Observe that there is
j_{1}>j_{0}

such that, for all
j>j_{1}
,
X\supseteq A^{\prime}_{>}\cap I_{j}
and, for all
j<j_{1}
,
X\cap I_{j}=\emptyset
. Since, for each
j\geq j_{1}>j_{0}
and
i\in[\ell]
,
|B_{i,2}\cap I_{j}|\geq\frac{1}{4\ell}|A^{\prime}_{>}\cap I_{j}|\geq\frac{1}{4\ell}|X\cap I_{j}|
, we have that
B_{i,2}
contains a subset with
\left\lceil\frac{1}{4\ell}|X\cap I_{j}|\right\rceil
elements in
I_{j}
for each
j\geq j_{1}
. We next show that
\sum_{j\geq j_{1}}\left\lceil\frac{1}{4\ell}|X\cap I_{j}|\right\rceil<k^{\prime}
. Indeed, let
t
be the number of indices
j\geq j_{1}
such that
X\cap I_{j}\neq\emptyset
. Note that
\sum_{j\geq j_{1}}|X\cap I_{j}|=|X|=2\ell k^{\prime}>256\ell
and, for each
j>j_{1}
for which
X\cap I_{j}
is non-empty,
|X\cap I_{j}|=|A^{\prime}_{>}\cap I_{j}|\geq 64\ell(\log n)
. Thus,

t\leq 1+\frac{|X|}{64\ell(\log n)}<\frac{k^{\prime}}{16}.

(24)

Therefore,

\sum_{j\geq j_{1}}\left\lceil\frac{1}{4\ell}|X\cap I_{j}|\right\rceil\leq t+\sum_{j\geq j_{1}}\frac{1}{4\ell}|X\cap I_{j}|<\frac{k^{\prime}}{16}+\frac{k^{\prime}}{2}<k^{\prime}.

Since
B_{i,2}
contains a subset of size less than
k^{\prime}
with at least
\left\lceil\frac{1}{4\ell}|X\cap I_{j}|\right\rceil
elements in
I_{j}
for each
j\geq j_{1}
and
M(i)
is the sum of the
k^{\prime}
largest elements of
B_{i,2}
, one has

M(i)\geq\sum_{j\geq j_{1}}2^{j-1}\cdot\frac{1}{4\ell}|X\cap I_{j}|=\frac{1}{8\ell}\sum_{j\geq j_{1}}2^{j}\cdot|X\cap I_{j}|\geq\frac{M}{8\ell}.

We also have
|A^{\prime}_{>,i}\cap I_{j}|\leq\frac{2}{\ell}|A^{\prime}_{>}\cap I_{j}|=\frac{2}{\ell}|X\cap I_{j}|
for all
j>j_{1}
. Moreover,

\sum_{j\geq j_{1}}\left\lfloor\frac{2}{\ell}|X\cap I_{j}|\right\rfloor\geq\sum_{j\geq j_{1}}\frac{2}{\ell}|X\cap I_{j}|-t\geq 4k^{\prime}-\frac{k^{\prime}}{16}>2k^{\prime},

where we used the bound (
24
).
Thus, there exists a set of size at least
2k^{\prime}
containing
\left\lfloor\frac{2}{\ell}|X\cap I_{j}|\right\rfloor
elements in
I_{j}
for each
j\geq j_{1}
such that the elements of this set dominate the
2k^{\prime}
largest elements of
A^{\prime}_{>,i}
.
Hence,

\overline{M}(i)\leq\sum_{j\geq j_{1}}2^{j}\cdot\frac{2}{\ell}|X\cap I_{j}|\leq\frac{4M}{\ell},

completing the proof of Claim 2.
∎

Both Sárközy
[
35
]
and Szemerédi and Vu
[
41
]
also state results which apply to

\Sigma^{(h)}(A)=\left\{\sum_{s\in S}s:S\subseteq A,|S|=h\right\},

the set of subset sums formed by adding exactly
h
distinct elements from
A
.
By a small modification of our proof, we can also derive the following variant of Theorem
6.1
that applies in this context.

Theorem 6.4
.

There exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that the following holds. For any subset
A
of
[n]
of size
m\geq C\sqrt{n}
, there exists
d\geq 1
and
r\in[0,d-1]
such that, for
A^{\prime}=\{(x-r)/d\,:\,x\in A,d|(x-r)\}
and
k\geq Cn/m
,
\Sigma^{(k)}(A^{\prime})
contains an interval of length at least
n
. Furthermore,

|A|-|A^{\prime}|\leq C((\log n)^{3}+n/m).

That is, if
A\subset[n]
has size
m\geq C\sqrt{n}
and
k\geq Cn/m
, then
\Sigma^{(k)}(A)
contains an arithmetic progression of length at least
n
.
Since we do not need this variant and the proof is rather similar to that of Theorem
6.1
, we omit the details.

Instead, we conclude the section by proving Corollary
1.10
, that there is a constant
C
such that
H(n)
and
h(n)
are both at most
C\sqrt{n}
, where we recall that
H(n)
is the largest integer for which there are two subsets of
[n]
of size
H(n)
whose sets of subset sums have no non-zero common element and
h(n)
is the size of the largest non-averaging subset of
[n]
.

Proof of Corollary
1.10
.

For the bound on
H(n)
, we need to show that for any two subsets
S_{1},S_{2}\subset[n]
, each of size
m\geq C\sqrt{n}
, there are non-empty subsets
S_{1}^{\prime}\subset S_{1}
and
S_{2}^{\prime}\subset S_{2}
such that
\sum_{s_{1}\in S^{\prime}_{1}}s_{1}=\sum_{s_{2}\in S^{\prime}_{2}}s_{2}
.
To this end, order the elements of
S_{1}\cup S_{2}
in increasing order and let
M
be the median. Without loss of generality, we may assume that the smallest
m/2
elements from
S_{1}
are each at most
M
and the largest
m/2
elements from
S_{2}
are each at least
M
. Let
R_{1}\subset S_{1}
consist of the smallest
m/2
elements from
S_{1}
and
R_{2}\subset S_{2}
consist of the largest
m/2
elements of
S_{2}
.

Applying Theorem
6.1
to
R_{1}
, we see that, provided
C
is sufficiently large,
\Sigma^{[k]}(R_{1})
with
k=2^{52}n/m
contains a homogeneous arithmetic progression
P
of common difference
d\leq 4M/m
and length at least
2n
whose minimum element is at most
kM
. Note now, by the pigeonhole principle, that any
d
element sequence contains a subsequence (consisting of consecutive terms) whose sum is divisible by
d
. We may therefore partition
R_{2}
greedily into subsets
T_{1}\cup\cdots\cup T_{s}
, each of size at most
d
, such that for each
i<s
the sum of the elements in
T_{i}
is a multiple of
d
. Note that the sum of the elements in any
T_{i}
is at most
dn
, while the sum of all the elements in
R_{2}\setminus T_{s}
is at least
(m/2-d)M\geq kM
. It therefore follows that, for some
j
, the sum
\sum_{i=1}^{j}\sum_{t\in T_{i}}t
, which is a sum of elements from
S_{2}
, lies in the homogeneous arithmetic progression
P
.

For the bound on
h(n)
, we apply Straus’ inequality
h(n)\leq 2H(n)+2
(see
[
39
]
), whose proof we include for completeness. Indeed, suppose that we have a subset of
[n]
of size
p=2H(n)+3
, say
\{a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{p}\}
with
a_{1}<a_{2}<\dots<a_{p}
. Writing
q=H(n)+2
, we see that
a_{q}
is the median element and the sets
\{a_{q}-a_{i}:1\leq i<q\}
and
\{a_{j}-a_{q}:q<j\leq p\}
are both subsets of
[n]
of size
H(n)+1
. Therefore, by the definition of
H(n)
, there must be sets
I\subset[q-1]
and
J\subset[q+1,p]
such that
\sum_{i\in I}(a_{q}-a_{i})=\sum_{j\in J}(a_{j}-a_{q})
. Rearranging, we see that

a_{q}=\frac{1}{|I|+|J|}\sum_{i\in I\cup J}a_{i}
, so the set is not non-averaging.
∎

7
Subsets avoiding a given subset sum

Recall that
g(n,m)
is the maximum size of a subset of
[n]
with no subset sum equal to
m
. Using the results of Section
6
, we now prove Theorem
1.7
, giving the precise value of
g(n,m)
. Theorem
1.7
states that there is a constant
C
such that if
m\in\left[Cn(\log n),\frac{n^{2}}{12(\log n)^{2}}\right]
, then

g(n,m)=s(n,m):=\left\lfloor\frac{n}{\textrm{snd}(m)}\right\rfloor+\textrm{snd}(m)-2,

where
\textrm{snd}(m)
is the smallest positive integer which is not a divisor of
m
. Moreover, if
m\in\left[\frac{n^{2}}{12(\log n)^{2}},\binom{n+1}{2}\right]
, then
g(n,m)=\max\left(s(n,m),(1+o(1))\sqrt{2m}\right)
.

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.

We consider the cases
m\leq\frac{n^{2}}{12(\log n)^{2}}
and
m>\frac{n^{2}}{12(\log n)^{2}}
separately.

Case 1.

Cn(\log n)\leq m\leq\frac{n^{2}}{12(\log n)^{2}}
for
C
sufficiently large.

Let
A\subseteq[n]
be such
that
m\notin\Sigma(A)
. Assume that
|A|\geq s(n,m)+1
.
We claim that
\textrm{snd}(m)\leq 1.01\log m
for
m
sufficiently large. Indeed, if
\textrm{snd}(m)>1.01\log m
, then
m\geq\textrm{lcm}(1,2,\dots,1.01\log m)
. It is easy to see that
\textrm{lcm}(1,2,\dots,1.01\log m)=\exp\left(\sum_{x\leq 1.01\log m}\Lambda(x)\right)
, where
\Lambda
is the von Mangoldt function given by
\Lambda(x)=\log p
if
x=p^{k}
is a prime power and
\Lambda(x)=0
otherwise. But, by the prime number theorem,
\sum_{x\leq 1.01\log m}\Lambda(x)\geq 1.005\log m
for
m
sufficiently large, so that
m\geq\textrm{lcm}(1,2,\dots,1.01\log m)\geq\exp(1.005\log m)
, a contradiction. Thus,
\textrm{snd}(m)\leq 1.01\log m\leq 2.02\log n
and, in particular,
|A|\geq\frac{n}{2.02\log n}
.

Let
A^{*}
be a random
subset of
A
where each element is chosen independently with probability

1/10
. By Hoeffding’s inequality,
|A|/9\geq|A^{*}|\geq|A|/11
with high probability.
Suppose that
2\leq d\leq n
is such that there are at least
(\log n)^{3}
elements in
A
which are not divisible by
d
. Again by Hoeffding’s inequality, the probability that the number of elements in
A
which are not divisible by
d
is more than
20
times larger than the number of elements in
A^{*}
which are not divisible by
d
is at most
\exp(-(\log n)^{3}/800)
. Thus, by the union bound, the probability that there exists
d\in[2,n]
such that there are at least
(\log n)^{3}
elements in
A
which are not divisible by
d
and the number of elements in
A
which are not divisible by
d
is at least
20
times larger than the number of elements in
A^{*}
which are not divisible by
d
is at most
n\exp(-(\log n)^{3}/800)<1/4
. Denote this latter event by
{\cal E}
and assume from here on that
A^{*}
has been chosen so that
|A|/9\geq|A^{*}|\geq|A|/11
and
{\cal E}
does not hold.

By Theorem
6.1
, there exists
d
such that,
for
A^{\prime}=\{x/d:\,x\in A^{*},d|x\}
, we have
|A^{\prime}|\geq|A^{*}|-2^{30}(\log n)^{3}-\frac{2^{30}n}{|A^{*}|}

and
\Sigma^{[k]}(A^{\prime})
contains an interval
I
of length at least

n
for
k=2^{50}n/h
, where
h=|A^{*}|
. Note that
|A^{\prime}|\geq|A^{*}|-2^{30}(\log n)^{3}-\frac{2^{30}n}{|A^{*}|}\geq|A^{*}|-2^{40}(\log n)^{3}
, as
|A^{*}|\geq\frac{|A|}{11}\geq\frac{n}{22.22\log n}
. Since
{\cal E}
does not hold, there are at most
2^{50}(\log n)^{3}
elements
in
A
which are not divisible by
d
.

Let
A^{\prime\prime}=\{x/d:x\in A\setminus A^{*},d|x\}
. Since
|A^{*}|\leq|A|/9
and there are at most
2^{50}(\log n)^{3}
elements in
A
which are not divisible by
d
, the size of
A^{\prime\prime}
is at least
|A|-|A^{*}|-2^{50}(\log n)^{3}\geq 8|A|/9-2^{50}(\log n)^{3}\geq 0.87|A|
. Note that the smallest element of
I
is at most
nk/d\leq 2^{50}\frac{n}{h}\cdot\frac{n}{d}
and each element in
A^{\prime\prime}
is at most
n/d\leq n
. Therefore, by Lemma
2.1
,
\Sigma^{[k+|A^{\prime\prime}|]}(A^{\prime}\cup A^{\prime\prime})
contains the interval
[2^{50}\frac{n}{h}\cdot\frac{n}{d},\sum_{x\in A^{\prime\prime}}x)
.
We have

\sum_{x\in A^{\prime\prime}}x\geq\frac{|A^{\prime\prime}|^{2}}{2}\geq\frac{|A|^{2}}{2.7}\geq\frac{n^{2}}{12(\log n)^{2}}

and

\frac{n}{h}\cdot n\leq 30n(\log n).

Hence,
\Sigma(A)
contains all multiples
z
of
d
with

2^{60}n(\log n)\leq z\leq\frac{n^{2}d}{12(\log n)^{2}}
.
In particular, if
m\notin\Sigma(A)
, then
d\nmid m
. Thus,
d\geq\textrm{snd}(m)
.
Recall that at most
2^{50}(\log n)^{3}
elements of
A
are not
divisible by
d
. Therefore, if
d\geq\textrm{snd}(m)+1
, then

|A|\leq 2^{50}(\log n)^{3}+\left\lfloor\frac{n}{d}\right\rfloor\leq 2^{50}(\log n)^{3}+\frac{n}{\textrm{snd}(m)+1}<\frac{n}{\textrm{snd}(m)}-\frac{n}{4\textrm{snd}(m)^{2}}<s(n,m),

a contradiction. Thus,
d=\textrm{snd}(m)
.

Since
|A|\geq\left\lfloor\frac{n}{\textrm{snd}(m)}\right\rfloor+\textrm{snd}(m)-1
and at most
\left\lfloor\frac{n}{\textrm{snd}(m)}\right\rfloor

elements in
A
are divisible by
d
, there exist at least
d-1
elements in
A
which are not divisible by
d
. Let
\bar{A}
be a set of
d-1
such elements.
Note that
\bar{A}
is disjoint from
\{dx:x\in A^{\prime}\cup A^{\prime\prime}\}
. By Lemma
5.8
,

\Sigma_{d}(\bar{A})
contains a non-zero subgroup
d^{\prime}\mathbb{Z}_{d}
of
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
.
Since
d=\textrm{snd}(m)
,
d^{\prime}|m
for any
d^{\prime}|d
and
d^{\prime}\neq d
. Thus, there exists a subset of
\bar{A}
whose
sum
y
is congruent to
m
modulo
d
. Furthermore,
y
is at most
dn
since
|\bar{A}|=d-1
and all elements of
\bar{A}
are at most
n
. Noting that
d\leq 4\log n
, we
have
m-y\geq Cn(\log n)-nd\geq 2^{60}n(\log n)
for sufficiently large
C
. We also have
m-y\leq m\leq\frac{n^{2}}{12(\log n)^{2}}
. Hence,
m-y\in\Sigma(\{dx:x\in A^{\prime}\cup A^{\prime\prime}\})
, so
m\in\Sigma(\{dx:x\in A^{\prime}\cup A^{\prime\prime}\}\cup\bar{A})
. Thus, if
|A|\geq s(n,m)+1
,
then
m\in\Sigma(A)
. Hence,
g(n,m)\leq s(n,m)
. Since we already noted in the introduction that
g(n,m)\geq s(n,m)
, this completes the proof in this case.

Case 2.

\frac{n^{2}}{12(\log n)^{2}}\leq m\leq\binom{n+1}{2}
.

Let
A\subseteq[n]
be such that
m\notin\Sigma(A)
. Assume that

|A|\geq 1+\max\left(s(n,m),\sqrt{2m}(1+2^{50}(\log n)^{2}/n^{1/3})\right).

Let
A^{*}
be a random subset of
A
where each element is chosen independently with probability
n^{-1/3}
. By Hoeffding’s inequality,
0.9|A|/n^{1/3}\leq|A^{*}|\leq 1.1|A|/n^{1/3}
with high probability. As in the case above, we can again define an event
{\cal E}
, in this case that there exists
d\in[2,n]
such that there are at least
n^{1/3}(\log n)^{2}
elements in
A
which are not divisible by
d
and the number of elements in
A
which are not divisible by
d
is at least
2n^{1/3}
times larger than the number of elements in
A^{*}
which are not divisible by
d
, and show that it happens with probability at most
1/4
. We now fix
A^{*}
with
0.9|A|/n^{1/3}\leq|A^{*}|\leq 1.1|A|/n^{1/3}
such that
{\cal E}
does not hold.

By Theorem
6.1
,
there exists
d
such that, for
A^{\prime}=\{x/d:\,x\in A^{*},d|x\}
, we have
|A^{\prime}|\geq|A^{*}|-2^{30}(\log n)^{3}-\frac{2^{30}n}{|A^{*}|}

and
\Sigma(A^{\prime})
contains an interval
I
of length at least
n
. Note that
|A|>s(n,m)\geq\frac{n}{2.02\log n}
, so
|A^{*}|\geq\frac{0.9|A|}{n^{1/3}}\geq\frac{0.4n^{2/3}}{\log n}
and
|A^{\prime}|\geq|A^{*}|-2^{30}(\log n)^{3}-\frac{2^{30}n}{|A^{*}|}\geq|A^{*}|-2^{40}n^{1/3}(\log n)
. Since
{\cal E}
does not hold, there are at most
2^{41}n^{2/3}(\log n)
elements of
A
which are not divisible by
d
.

Since
|A|\geq s(n,m)+1
,
we must again have
d\leq\textrm{snd}(m)
. If
d=\textrm{snd}(m)
, then,
as above, we can find at most
d-1

elements of
A
which are not divisible by
d
and
whose sum is congruent to
m
modulo
d
. If
d<\textrm{snd}(m)
,
then
d|m
. In either case, there is a (possibly empty) sum
y
of at most
d-1

elements of
A
not divisible by
d
such
that
d|(m-y)
. Therefore, to show that
m\in\Sigma(A)
, it suffices
to show that
(m-y)/d\in\Sigma(\{x/d:x\in A,d|x\})
.

Note that
\Sigma(A^{\prime})
contains an interval
I
where the largest
element of
I
is at most
\sum_{x\in A^{\prime}}x\leq 1.1n^{5/3}/d
and each element in
\{x/d:x\in A\setminus A^{*},d|x\}
is at most
n/d
. By Lemma
2.1
,
\Sigma(\{x/d:x\in A,d|x\})
contains the interval
[1.1n^{5/3}/d,\sum_{z\in A\setminus A^{*},d|z}z/d]
.
The number of elements in
A\setminus A^{*}
which are divisible
by
d
is at least
|A|-|A^{*}|-2^{41}n^{2/3}(\log n)\geq|A|-1.1|A|/n^{1/3}-2^{41}n^{2/3}(\log n)\geq|A|(1-2^{42}n^{-1/3}(\log n)^{2})>\sqrt{2m}
.
Hence,

\sum_{z\in A\setminus A^{*},d|z}z/d\geq\sum_{i=1}^{\lceil\sqrt{2m}\rceil}i\geq m.

Thus,
\Sigma(\{x/d:x\in A,d|x\})
contains
(m-y)/d
, since
(m-y)/d\geq(m-dn)/d>1.1n^{5/3}/d

and
(m-y)/d<m
.

Hence,

g(n,m)\leq\max\left(s(n,m),\sqrt{2m}(1+2^{50}(\log n)^{2}/n^{1/3})\right).

Since
g(n,m)\geq s(n,m)
and
g(n,m)\geq\lfloor\sqrt{2m}\rfloor
as the interval
[\lfloor\sqrt{2m}\rfloor-1]
does not have a subset sum which equals
m
, we have

g(n,m)=\max\left(s(n,m),\sqrt{2m}(1+O((\log n)^{2}/n^{1/3}))\right),

completing the proof.
∎

Appendix A
Supplementary results for Section
4

A.1
The growth rate of
F

In Section
4
, we consider a sequence of positive integers
F=(f_{n})_{n\geq 1}
which satisfies
f_{n}=\sum_{i\leq\epsilon n}f_{i}
for all
n\geq n_{0}
. Here we establish the asymptotic for
F
claimed in the introduction.

Claim A.1
.

Let
F=(f_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of positive integers which satisfies
f_{n}=\sum_{i\leq\epsilon n}f_{i}
for all
n\geq n_{0}
. Then
f_{n}=\exp\left(\left(\frac{1}{2\log(1/\epsilon)}+o(1)\right)(\log n)^{2}\right)
.

Proof.

We first show by induction that there is a constant
C
for which
f_{n}\leq\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\log(1/\epsilon)}((\log n)^{2}+C)\right)
for all positive integers
n
, which would imply the upper bound in the claim. We can choose
C
sufficiently large so that this holds for all
n\leq\max(n_{0},10/\epsilon)
. Let
m>\max(n_{0},10/\epsilon)
. If
f_{n}\leq\exp\left(\frac{(\log n)^{2}+C}{2\log(1/\epsilon)}\right)
for all
n\leq m-1
, then

\displaystyle f_{m}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\leq\epsilon m}f_{i}\leq\epsilon m\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\log(1/\epsilon)}((\log(\epsilon m))^{2}+C)\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\log(1/\epsilon)}((\log m-\log(1/\epsilon))^{2}+C+2\log(1/\epsilon)\log(\epsilon m))\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\log(1/\epsilon)}((\log m)^{2}+C)\right),

completing the induction proof of the desired upper bound on
f_{n}
.

We now turn to proving the desired lower bound on
f_{n}
in the claim. Let
C^{\prime}=100\log(1/\epsilon)
. Let
\tilde{g}(x)=\exp\left(\frac{(\log x)^{2}-C^{\prime}\log x\log\log x}{2\log(1/\epsilon)}\right)
. Note that there is
x_{0}>0
depending only on
\epsilon
such that
(\log x)^{2}-C^{\prime}\log x\log\log x
is increasing for all
x\geq x_{0}
. Let
m(\epsilon)
be the least positive integer such that, for all
m\geq m(\epsilon)
,

\epsilon\tilde{g}(m)\leq\tilde{g}(m-1/\epsilon)-\tilde{g}(x_{0}/\epsilon).

It is easy to verify that such
m(\epsilon)
exists. Let
g(x)=\exp\left(\frac{(\log x)^{2}-C^{\prime}\log x\log\log x-C}{2\log(1/\epsilon)}\right)
, where
C
is a sufficiently large constant to be chosen later. We next show by induction that, for an appropriate choice of
C
,
f_{n}\geq g(n)
for all
n\geq 2
. We choose
C
sufficiently large that the above claim holds for all
n\leq\max(n_{0},x_{0},m(\epsilon))
.
Let
m\geq\max(n_{0},x_{0},m(\epsilon))
. If
f_{n}\geq g(n)
for all
n\leq m-1
, then

\displaystyle f_{m}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\leq\epsilon m}f_{i}\geq\sum_{i\leq\epsilon m}g(i)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{x_{0}}^{\epsilon m-1}g(x)dx=\int_{x_{0}/\epsilon}^{m-1/\epsilon}\epsilon g(\epsilon y)dy,

where in the last step we used the change of variable
y=x/\epsilon
. Note now that

\displaystyle g^{\prime}(y)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\log(1/\epsilon)}((\log y)^{2}-C^{\prime}\log y\log\log y-C)\right)\cdot\frac{2\log y-C^{\prime}-C^{\prime}\log\log y}{2y\log(1/\epsilon)}

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\log(1/\epsilon)}\left[(\log y)^{2}-C^{\prime}\log y\log\log y-C-2\log(1/\epsilon)\log y+2\log(1/\epsilon)\log(\log y/\log(1/\epsilon))\right]\right).

Thus,

\displaystyle g^{\prime}(y)/g(\epsilon y)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\log(1/\epsilon)}\left[(\log y)^{2}-C^{\prime}\log y\log\log y-C-2\log(1/\epsilon)\log y+2\log(1/\epsilon)\log(\log y/\log(1/\epsilon))\right]\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\log(1/\epsilon)}\left[-(\log(\epsilon y))^{2}+C^{\prime}\log(\epsilon y)\log\log(\epsilon y)+C\right]\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\log(1/\epsilon)}\left[2\log(1/\epsilon)\log(\log y/\log(1/\epsilon))+C^{\prime}\log(\epsilon)\log\log y\right]\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp(-C^{\prime}(\log\log y)/4)\leq\epsilon^{2},

where in the last inequality we used the fact that
C^{\prime}=100\log(1/\epsilon)
. Then

f_{m}\geq\int_{x_{0}/\epsilon}^{m-1/\epsilon}\epsilon g(\epsilon y)dy\geq\int_{x_{0}/\epsilon}^{m-1/\epsilon}\frac{g^{\prime}(y)}{\epsilon}dy\geq\frac{g(m-1/\epsilon)-g(x_{0}/\epsilon)}{\epsilon}\geq g(m),

where in the last inequality we used the definition of
m(\epsilon)
and the fact that
m\geq m(\epsilon)
. This completes the induction.
∎

A.2
Proof of Lemma
4.4

In this subsection, we give the proof of Lemma
4.4
, which is a key component in the proof of Theorem
4.3
. First, we recall the setting and the statement of the lemma. Let
\epsilon_{0}>0
be a sufficiently small constant.
Let
B=(b_{n})_{n\geq 1}
be an
\epsilon
-friendly sequence. For
j
sufficiently large, we choose
a_{j}
to be a uniform random integer in
[b_{j},b_{j+1})
which has no prime factor at most
(\max(1/\epsilon,1/\epsilon_{0}))^{4000}
and let
A=(a_{j})_{j\geq 1}
. For small
j
, we choose
a_{j}
to be an arbitrary integer in
[b_{j},b_{j+1})
. We let
h(i)
be the smallest integer for which
b_{h(i)}\geq 2^{i}
and
A_{i}=A\cap[b_{h(i)},b_{h(i+1)-1})
.

Lemma 4.4
.

There exist positive constants
\epsilon_{0}
,
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
such that the following holds. For
i
sufficiently large, with positive probability,
the set
A_{i}
has the property that, for any subset
A^{\prime}_{i}\subset A_{i}

with
|A^{\prime}_{i}|\geq(\min(\epsilon,\epsilon_{0})/4)|A_{i}|
,
A^{\prime}_{i}
contains
a subset
A^{\prime\prime}_{i}
with
|A^{\prime\prime}_{i}|\leq C_{1}i
such that
\Sigma(A^{\prime\prime}_{i})

contains every integer in the interval
[y,2y]
, where
y=C_{2}2^{i}i
.

The proof of this lemma has been consigned to an appendix because of its similarity to the proof of Lemma
2.8
. Indeed, the difference between the two proofs consists mainly of minor modifications to account for the non-uniformity in the distribution of the elements of
A_{i}
. However, for completeness, we give the proof in full, beginning with the following lemma, which is the analogue of Lemma
3.1
in this context.

Lemma A.2
.

For a sufficiently large positive constant
C_{0}
, the following holds. Assume that
\epsilon>0
is sufficiently small. Let
i
be sufficiently large and let
m
be an integer in
[2^{i},2^{i+1})
with no prime factor at most
\epsilon^{-4000}
. If
S
is a uniformly chosen random subsequence of
A_{i}
of size
C_{0}i
, then
|\Sigma_{m}(S)|<2^{i-2}
with probability less than
\epsilon^{1000C_{0}i}
.

Proof.

Let
w=\epsilon^{-4000}
. Denote by
X
the set of integers
[b_{h(i)},b_{h(i+1)-1})
with no prime divisor at most
w
. For each
j\in[h(i),h(i+1)-1)
, let
X_{j}
be the set of integers in
[b_{j},b_{j+1})
with no prime divisor at most
w
. Let
t=h(i+1)-h(i)-1
, which is the number of intervals
[b_{j},b_{j+1})
in
[b_{h(i)},b_{h(i+1)-1})
. Note that for each interval
I
of integers of sufficient length, the number of elements in the interval which are coprime to all the primes at most
w
is
(\tau+o(1))|I|
, where
\tau=\phi(W)/W
with
W
being the product of all primes at most
w
. Since
B
is a friendly sequence, we have
b_{j+1}-b_{j}\leq(b_{j^{\prime}+1}-b_{j^{\prime}})/c
for all
j\in[h(i)-1,h(i+1)-1]
and
j^{\prime}\in[h(i),h(i+1)-2]
. Thus,

2^{i}\leq b_{h(i+1)}-b_{h(i)-1}\leq(1+2/(ct))(b_{h(i+1)-1}-b_{h(i)}).

Since
t
tends to infinity as
i
tends to infinity, we have that
b_{h(i+1)-1}-b_{h(i)}\geq\frac{7}{8}2^{i}
for sufficiently large
i
. Hence, for large
i
, we have that

|X|\geq\frac{2^{i}\tau}{2}.

(25)

Again by properties of friendly sequences, the length of the intervals
[b_{j},b_{j+1})
for
j\in[h(i),h(i+1)-1)
are within a factor
1/c
of each other and the minimum length of an interval
[b_{j},b_{j+1})
with
j\in[h(i),h(i+1)-1)
tends to infinity as
i
tends to infinity. We thus obtain that all
|X_{j}|
with
j\in[h(i),h(i+1)-1)
are within a factor
2/c
of each other for
i
sufficiently large. Hence,

|X_{j}|\geq\frac{c|X|}{2t}.

(26)

Let
{\cal D}
be the distribution of a random integer in
[b_{h(i)},b_{h(i+1)-1})
, where the probability that an element
a\in X_{j}
is chosen is
\frac{1}{|X_{j}|t}
. Observe that the random sequence
S
is a sequence of
C_{0}i
random integers with distribution
{\cal D}
, subject to the condition that no two elements come from the same interval
[b_{j},b_{j+1})
.

Let
q=C_{0}i
. Let
S=(s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{q})
. Let
S_{j}=(s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{j})
denote the sequence consisting of the first
j
elements of
S
. Let
\delta=1/w
. Call
j\in[2,q]

bad
if

•

|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j})|\leq\frac{3}{2}|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|
and
|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|\leq\frac{2^{i}}{w}
or

•

|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j})|\leq(1+\delta)|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|
and
\frac{2^{i}}{w}<|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|<2^{i-2}
.

The following two claims will allow us to complete the proof of the lemma.

Claim 1.
The probability that
j
is bad conditioned on the choice of
S_{j-1}
is at most
p:=\frac{16}{cw\tau}
.

Claim 2.
If
|\Sigma_{m}(S)|<2^{i-2}
, then all but fewer than
2i
integers in
[2,q]
are bad.

Assuming Claim 1, for any
B\subset[2,q]
, the probability that all elements in
B
are bad is at most
p^{|B|}
. By Merten’s third theorem, we have
\tau=(e^{-\gamma}+o(1))/\log w\geq 1/(2\log w)
for sufficiently small
\epsilon
, so

p\leq\frac{32\log w}{cw}.

(27)

From Claim 2, if
|\Sigma_{m}(S)|<2^{i-2}
, then there is a set
B
of
q-2i
integers
i\in[2,q]
which are bad. Taking a union bound over all such choices of
B
, the probability that
|\Sigma_{m}(S)|<2^{i-2}
is at most

{q\choose q-2i}p^{|B|}={q\choose 2i}p^{|B|}<(eC_{0})^{2i}\left(\frac{32\log w}{cw}\right)^{C_{0}i-2i}<\epsilon^{1000C_{0}i},

where in the first inequality we used (
27
) and in the second inequality we assume a sufficiently large choice of
C_{0}
and note that
w=\epsilon^{-4000}
with
\epsilon
sufficiently small.
∎

To complete the proof, it remains to verify Claims 1 and 2.

Proof of Claim 1.
Fix
S_{j-1}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{j-1})
. Conditioned on this choice of
S_{j-1}
, we bound the probability that
j
is bad. Let
T
be the set of
k
such that
[b_{k},b_{k+1})
contains at least one of
s_{1},\ldots,s_{j-1}
. Observe that conditioned on
s_{1},\dots,s_{j-1}
, the distribution of
s_{j}
is supported on
\bigcup_{k\in[h(i),h(i+1)-1)\setminus T}X_{k}
and, for
x_{k}\in X_{k}
with
k\in[h(i),h(i+1)-1)\setminus T
, the conditional probability that
s_{j}
is equal to
x_{k}
is

\frac{1}{|X_{k}|(t-|T|)}\leq\frac{2}{|X_{k}|t}\leq\frac{4}{c|X|},

where we used (
26
).

If
|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|\geq 2^{i-2}
, then
j
cannot be bad (so the event that
j
is bad has probability zero). We may therefore restrict attention to the two cases
|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|\leq 2^{i}/w
and
2^{i}/w<|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|<2^{i-2}
.

For the first case, note, by Lemma
2.6
, that the number of
s
with
|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1}\cup\{s\})|\leq\frac{3}{2}|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|
is at most
\frac{|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|^{2}}{|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|/2}=2|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|
. Therefore, if
|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|\leq 2^{i}/w
, the probability that
j
is bad conditioned on
S_{j-1}
is at most

2|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|\cdot\frac{4}{c|X|}\leq\frac{16}{cw\tau}=p,

where in the inequality we used (
25
).

Suppose now that
2^{i}/w<|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|<2^{i-2}
. For a positive integer
D
, let
G_{D}
be the set of
s
such that
|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1}\cup\{s\})|\leq|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|+D
. Let
d=\lfloor\delta|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|\rfloor
, so
j
is bad in this case if and only if
s_{j}\in G_{d}
. Let
k=\lfloor\frac{1}{2\delta}\rfloor
, so
kd\leq|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|/2
. By Lemma
2.7
,
kG_{d}\subseteq G_{kd}
, so

\left|kG_{d}\right|\leq|G_{kd}|\leq 2|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|<2^{i-1}
, where the middle inequality is again by the consequence of Lemma
2.6
noted above.

If
|G_{d}|\leq\frac{m}{w}
, then
|G_{d}|\leq\frac{m}{w}\leq\frac{2^{i+1}}{w}=2\delta 2^{i}
.
Otherwise,
|G_{d}|>\frac{m}{w}
. In this case, since
m
has no prime divisor
at most
w
, no subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
has size larger than

\frac{m}{w}
. Thus,
G_{d}
cannot be contained in a coset
of a non-trivial subgroup. By Lemma
2.3
, since
\left|kG_{d}\right|\leq 2^{i-1}<m
, we must have

\left|kG_{d}\right|\geq(k+1)|G_{d}|/2\geq|G_{d}|/(4\delta)
. Hence,
|G_{d}|\leq 4\delta|kG_{d}|\leq 4\delta 2^{i-1}=2\delta 2^{i}
. Thus, in either case, conditioned on the choice of
S_{j-1}
, the probability that
j
is bad, which is the same as the probability that
s_{j}\in G_{d}
, is at
most

|G_{d}|\cdot\frac{4}{c|X|}\leq\frac{16\delta}{c\tau}=p,

where we again used (
25
). ∎

Proof of Claim 2.
As
S_{j-1}\subset S_{j}
for
j\in[2,q]
,
\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})\subset\Sigma_{m}(S_{j})
and, hence,
1\leq|\Sigma_{m}(S_{1})|\leq\cdots\leq|\Sigma_{m}(S_{q})|=|\Sigma_{m}(S)|<2^{i-2}
. Therefore, the number of
j
which are not bad with
|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|\leq 2^{i}/w
and
|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j})|\geq\frac{3}{2}|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|
is at most
\log_{3/2}2^{i}=i\frac{\log 2}{\log(3/2)}
, as we get a factor of
3/2
for each such
j
. Moreover, since
(1+\delta)^{\delta^{-1}\log_{2}w}\geq 2^{\log_{2}w}=w
, the number of elements
j
which are not bad with
2^{i-2}>|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|>2^{i}/w
and
|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j})|\geq(1+\delta)|\Sigma_{m}(S_{j-1})|
is at most
\delta^{-1}\log_{2}w=w\log_{2}w
, as we get a factor of
1+\delta
for each such
j
. Therefore, the number of
j\in[2,q]
which are not bad is at most
i\frac{\log 2}{\log(3/2)}+w\log_{2}w<2i
for sufficiently large
i
. ∎

We are now ready to prove Lemma
4.4
.

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

By replacing
\epsilon
with
\min(\epsilon,\epsilon_{0})
, we only need to prove Lemma
4.4
for
\epsilon\leq\epsilon_{0}
. Thus, by choosing
\epsilon_{0}
sufficiently small, it suffices to prove that the following holds for sufficiently small
\epsilon
. If
i
is sufficiently large, then any subset
A_{i}^{\prime}
of
A_{i}
with
|A_{i}^{\prime}|\geq(\epsilon/4)|A_{i}|
contains a subset
A_{i}^{\prime\prime}
with
|A_{i}^{\prime\prime}|\leq C_{1}i
such that
\Sigma(A_{i}^{\prime\prime})
contains every integer in the interval
[y,2y]
, where
y=C_{2}2^{i}i
.

For a given
C_{1}
and
i
sufficiently large, we have that
|A_{i}|\geq 400\epsilon^{-1}C_{1}i
. Consider a random partition of
A_{i}
into subsets of size
4\epsilon^{-1}C_{1}i
and consider a uniform random ordering of each subset as a sequence of integers. Let the obtained sequences be
A_{i,1},\dots,A_{i,u}
.

We will show that for an appropriate choice of
C_{1}
, there exists a positive constant
C_{2}
such that, with positive probability, the following event
{\cal E}
holds. For all
k\leq u
and all subsequences
A^{\prime}_{i,k}
of
A_{i,k}
of size
(\epsilon/4)|A_{i,k}|
,
\Sigma(A^{\prime}_{i,k})
contains the interval
[y,2y]
for
y=C_{2}2^{i}i
.

Fix
k\leq u
and fix a subset
I^{\prime}
of
[4\epsilon^{-1}C_{1}i]
of size
C_{1}i
. Let
A^{\prime}_{i,k}
be the subsequence of
A_{i,k}
consisting of elements with index in
I^{\prime}
. Let
\ell
be a constant to be chosen later. We partition
I^{\prime}
into a subset
I^{\prime\prime}
of size
7C_{1}i/8
and
\ell
subsets
I^{\prime\prime}_{k,1},\dots,I^{\prime\prime}_{k,\ell}
of equal size such that each subset in the partition consists of consecutive terms from
I^{\prime}
. Let
A^{\prime\prime}_{i,k}
be the elements with index in
I^{\prime\prime}
and, for each
j\in[\ell]
, let
S_{k,j}
be the elements with index in
I^{\prime\prime}_{k,j}
. Let
J^{\prime}_{k,j}
be the first
|I^{\prime\prime}_{k,j}|/2
elements of
I^{\prime\prime}_{k,j}
and let
J^{\prime\prime}_{k,j}
be the remaining elements. Let
S^{\prime}_{k,j}
be the elements with index in
J^{\prime}_{k,j}
and let
S^{\prime\prime}_{k,j}
be the elements with index in
J^{\prime\prime}_{k,j}
. Then
S^{\prime}_{k,j}
has the same distribution as a random subsequence of
A_{i}
of length
|S_{k,j}|/2
. We choose
C_{1}=16\ell C_{0}
, so that
|S^{\prime}_{k,j}|=C_{0}i
, where
C_{0}
is the constant defined in Lemma
A.2
. By Lemma
A.2
and a union bound, we have that
|\Sigma_{m}(S^{\prime}_{k,j})|\geq 2^{i-2}
for all
m\in[2^{i},2^{i+1})
with no prime factor at most
\epsilon^{-4000}
with probability at least
1-2^{i}\epsilon^{1000C_{0}i}>1-\epsilon^{800C_{0}i}
, assuming that
\epsilon
is sufficiently small.
Thus, by another union bound, with probability at least
1-\ell\epsilon^{800C_{0}i}
,
|\Sigma_{m}(S^{\prime}_{k,j})|\geq 2^{i-2}
for all
j\leq\ell
and all
m\in S^{\prime\prime}_{k,j}
. By repeated application of Lemma
2.5
, we have that

|\Sigma(S_{k,j})|=|\Sigma(S^{\prime}_{k,j}\cup S^{\prime\prime}_{k,j})|\geq|S^{\prime\prime}_{k,j}|2^{i-2}\geq\frac{1}{16}2^{i}|A^{\prime}_{i,k}|/(4\ell).

We also have that
\Sigma(S_{k,j})
is a subset of the interval
[0,2^{i}|A^{\prime}_{i,k}|/(4\ell)]
. Furthermore,
\Sigma(S_{k,j})
is not contained in any arithmetic progression with common difference greater than
1
, as otherwise there exists
1<d\leq 16
such that all elements of
S_{k,j}
are divisible by
d
, contradicting the fact that elements of
A_{i}
do not have prime factors at most
\epsilon^{-4000}
. Thus, choosing
\ell=33
,
by Lemma
2.2
, we have that
S_{k,1}+\ldots+S_{k,\ell}
contains an interval of length at least
2^{i}|A^{\prime}_{i,k}|/(4\ell)
. Hence,
\Sigma(A^{\prime}_{i,k}\setminus A^{\prime\prime}_{i,k})
contains an interval
[a,b]
of length at least
2^{i}|A^{\prime}_{i,k}|/(4\ell)>2^{i+1}
. Note that
a<b\leq 2^{i-2}|A^{\prime}_{i,k}|
. By Lemma
2.1
, we then have that
\Sigma(A^{\prime}_{i,k})
contains the interval
[a,b+\sum_{x\in A^{\prime\prime}_{i,k}}x]\supset[y,2y]
for
y=\frac{1}{4}2^{i}|A^{\prime}_{i,k}|=\frac{C_{1}}{4}2^{i}i
. Let
C_{2}=\frac{C_{1}}{4}
.

By taking a union bound over all possible choices of
I^{\prime}
, the probability that there exists a subsequence
A^{\prime}_{i,k}
of
A_{i,k}
of size
(\epsilon/4)|A_{i,k}|
such that
\Sigma(A^{\prime}_{i,k})
does not contain the interval
[y,2y]
for
y=C_{2}2^{i}i
is at most
{|A_{i,k}|\choose(\epsilon/4)|A_{i,k}|}\ell\epsilon^{800C_{0}i}
. By a union bound over all
k\leq u
, we then obtain that the event
{\cal E}
holds with probability at least

1-u{|A_{i,k}|\choose(\epsilon/4)|A_{i,k}|}\ell\epsilon^{800C_{0}i}\geq 1-2^{i+7}(4e/\epsilon)^{C_{1}i}\epsilon^{800C_{0}i}\geq 1-\epsilon^{200C_{0}i}>0,

where we used that
\epsilon
is sufficiently small,
C_{1}=16\ell C_{0}
with
C_{0}
sufficiently large,
\ell=33
and
u\leq 2^{i+1}
.

Assume now that the event
{\cal E}
holds. For any subset
A^{\prime}_{i}
of
A_{i}
such that
|A^{\prime}_{i}|\geq(\epsilon/4)|A_{i}|
, there exists
k\leq u
such that
|A^{\prime}_{i}\cap A_{i,k}|\geq(\epsilon/4)|A_{i,k}|
. Thus, defining
A^{\prime\prime}_{i}
to be an arbitrary subset of
A^{\prime}_{i}\cap A_{i,k}
of size
(\epsilon/4)|A_{i,k}|=C_{1}i
, we have that
\Sigma(A^{\prime\prime}_{i})
contains the interval
[y,2y]
for
y=C_{2}2^{i}i
, as required.
∎

Appendix B
Supplementary results for Section
5

B.1
Number-theoretic estimates

This short section contains the proofs of some number-theoretic estimates which were used in Section
5
.
We will need the following simple lemma.

Lemma B.1
.

One has

\frac{m}{\zeta(2)\phi(m)}\leq\prod_{p|m}\left(1+\frac{1}{p}\right)\leq\sum_{u|m}\frac{1}{u}\leq\prod_{p|m}\left(1+\frac{1}{p-1}\right)=\frac{m}{\phi(m)}.

Proof.

By considering the squarefree divisors of
m
, we have

\sum_{u|m}\frac{1}{u}\geq\prod_{p|m}\left(1+\frac{1}{p}\right).

On the other hand,

\sum_{u|m}\frac{1}{u}\leq\prod_{p|m}\left(1+\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{2}}+\cdots\right)=\prod_{p|m}\left(1+\frac{1}{p-1}\right).

Furthermore,

\prod_{p|m}\left(1+\frac{1}{p}\right)=\prod_{p|m}\left(1+\frac{1}{p-1}\right)\cdot\prod_{p|m}\left(1-\frac{1}{p^{2}}\right)=\frac{m}{\phi(m)}\prod_{p|m}\left(1-\frac{1}{p^{2}}\right)\geq\frac{m}{\zeta(2)\phi(m)}.\qed

Our first aim is to prove Lemma
5.2
, which gives upper and lower bounds on the number of integers in an interval with certain number-theoretic properties. The following lemma, of a similar flavor, is a key component in the proof. Recall that
W(r)=\prod_{i=1}^{r}p_{i}
, where
p_{i}
is the
i^{\textrm{th}}
prime, and
\tau(r,m)=\phi(W(r)m)/(W(r)m)=\prod_{p|W(r)m}(1-1/p)
.

Lemma B.2
.

Let
r
,
n
and
m
be positive integers such that
m\in[n,\binom{n}{2}]
,
r\leq n
and
r
is sufficiently large. For any interval
I=[x,2x)
with
x\geq n^{1/6}
, the number of integers in
I
which are coprime to
W(r)m
is at most
8\tau(r,m)x
. If also
x\geq r^{1.5}
, then the number of integers in
I
which are coprime to
W(r)m
is at least
\frac{1}{4}\tau(r,m)x
.

Proof.

By
[
32
, Theorem 7.11]
, for each interval
I=[x,2x)
with
x\geq p_{r}/2
,
the number of integers in
I
which are coprime to
W(r)
is at most
(1+o(1))\frac{x}{\log p_{r}}\leq 2\tau(r,1)x
, where we used that
p_{r}=(1+o(1))r\log r
and (
31
). For
x<p_{r}/2
, the number of integers in
I
which are coprime to
W(r)
is
0<2\tau(r,1)x
. If also
x\geq r^{1.5}>p_{r}
, then the number of integers in
I
which are coprime to
W(r)
is at least
\left(\frac{1}{2}-o(1)\right)\frac{x}{\log p_{r}}\geq\frac{1}{2}\tau(r,1)x
, again using (
31
).

Consider the case
r\geq(\log m)/100
. Then
\tau(r,1)\leq 1/\log r\leq 4\tau(r,m)
by (
31
) and (
34
),
so the number of integers in
I
which are coprime to
W(r)m
is at most
8\tau(r,m)x
. For
x\geq r^{1.5}
, we have seen that there are at least
\frac{1}{2}\tau(r,1)x
integers in
I
which are coprime to
W(r)
. For each prime factor
p
of
m
that is larger than
p_{r}>r
, the number of integers in
I
divisible by
p
and coprime to
W(r)
is the same as the number of integers in
[x/p,2x/p)
coprime to
W(r)
, which is at most
2\tau(r,1)x/p
. Since there are at most
(\log m)/(\log r)
such prime factors, the number of integers in
I
which are coprime to
W(r)m
is at least

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tau(r,1)x-\frac{\log m}{\log r}\cdot\frac{2\tau(r,1)x}{r}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\tau(r,1)x-\frac{200r\tau(r,1)x}{r\log r}

\displaystyle>\tau(r,m)x/4,

where, in the first inequality, we used the assumption
r\geq(\log m)/100
and, in the second inequailty, we used that
r
is sufficiently large and
\tau(r,m)\leq\tau(r,1)
by (
30
).

Next, consider the case
