r<(\log m)/100
. By the inclusion-exclusion principle, the number of integers in
I=[x,2x)
which are coprime to
W(r)m
is

x+\sum_{k=1}^{M}(-1)^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1}<p_{2}<\dots<p_{k},\\
p_{1},p_{2},\dots,p_{k}|W(r)m\end{subarray}}\frac{x}{p_{1}p_{2}\cdots p_{k}}+O(2^{M}),

which is within an additive
O(2^{M})
of
x\prod_{p|W(r)m}(1-1/p)
, where
M
is the number of distinct primes that divide
W(r)m
. Since
M\leq r+2(\log m)/(\log\log m)<(\log n)/10
and
x\geq n^{1/6}
, the number of integers in
I
coprime to
W(r)m
is at least
\frac{1}{2}\tau(r,m)x
and at most
2\tau(r,m)x
.
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
r
,
n
and
m
be positive integers such that
m\in[n,\binom{n}{2}]
,
r\leq n
and
r
is sufficiently large. For any interval
I=[x,2x)
with
x\geq n^{1/4}
, there are at most
8(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)x
integers in
I
of the form
qu
, where
u|m
,
u\leq x^{1/16}
and
q
is coprime to
W(r)m
. If also
x\geq r^{2}
, then there are at least
\frac{1}{8}(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)x
integers in
I
of this form.

Proof.

Observe that for
x\geq n^{1/4}
and each fixed
u|m
with
u\leq x^{1/16}
, Lemma
B.2
implies that the number of integers in
I
of the form
qu
where
q
is coprime to
W(r)m
, which is the same as the number of integers in
[x/u,2x/u)
which are coprime to
W(r)m
, is at most
8\tau(r,m)x/u
, where we used that
x/u\geq n^{1/6}
. If also
x\geq r^{2}
, then Lemma
B.2
similarly implies that the number of integers in
I
of the form
qu
where
q
is coprime to
W(r)m
is at least
\frac{1}{4}\tau(r,m)x/u
, where we used that
x/u\geq r^{1.5}
.

Hence, the number of integers in
I
of
the form
qu
, where
u|m
,
u\leq x^{1/16}
and
q
is coprime
to
W(r)m
, is at least

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\tau(r,m)\sum_{u|m,\,u\leq x^{1/16}}\frac{x}{u}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}\tau(r,m)\left(\sum_{u|m}\frac{1}{u}-\frac{\sigma(m)}{x^{1/16}}\right)x

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}\tau(r,m)\cdot\frac{m}{2\phi(m)}x

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{8}(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)x,

where
\sigma(m)
is the number of positive divisors of
m
, which is smaller
than
m^{1/100}
for
m
sufficiently large, and we used Lemma
B.1
in the second inequality. Similarly, the number
of integers in
I
of the form
qu
, where
u|m
,
u\leq x^{1/16}

and
q
is coprime to
W(r)m
, is at most

8\tau(r,m)\sum_{u|m,\,u\leq x^{1/16}}\frac{x}{u}\leq 8(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)x,

where we again used Lemma
B.1
.
∎

We now prove Lemma
5.9
, which gives an upper bound on the number of integers in an arithmetic progression which are coprime to
W(r)/\gcd(W(r),m)
. The proof employs the Selberg sieve.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
r
and
n
be sufficiently large positive integers and
m\in[n,\binom{n}{2}]
. Let
X
be an arithmetic progression of size
|X|\geq r^{1/16}
with common difference
b\leq n
. Then the number of elements
of
X
which are coprime to
W(r)/\gcd(W(r),m)
is at most

\frac{256|X|\log\log n}{\log r}.

Furthermore, when
b=1
, the number of elements
of
X
which are coprime to
W(r)/\gcd(W(r),m)
is at most

256|X|\prod_{p|W(r),p\nmid m}(1-1/p).

Proof.

First, we prove the lemma in the case where the elements of the arithmetic progression are coprime to
b
. By the Selberg sieve
[
32
, Theorem 3.8]
, applied with
q=b
and
P=W(r)/\gcd(W(r),bm)
, which is coprime to
b
, the number of integers coprime to
W(r)/\gcd(W(r),bm)
contained in any arithmetic progression of length
k\geq r^{1/16}
and common
difference
b
is at most

2k\prod_{p|(W(r)/\gcd(W(r),bm)),\,p\leq\sqrt{k}}\frac{p-1}{p}\leq 2k\prod_{p|(W(r)/\gcd(W(r),bm)),\,p\leq r^{1/32}}\frac{p-1}{p}.

Since each prime
p\leq r^{1/32}<r
is either a divisor of
\gcd(W(r),bm)
or a divisor of
W(r)/\gcd(W(r),bm)
, for
r
sufficiently large, we have that

\left(\prod_{p|\gcd(W(r),bm)}\frac{p-1}{p}\right)\cdot\left(\prod_{p|(W(r)/\gcd(W(r),bm)),\,p\leq r^{1/32}}\frac{p-1}{p}\right)\leq\prod_{p\leq r^{1/32}}\frac{p-1}{p}\leq\frac{32}{\log r},

where we used Mertens’ third theorem.
Since
W(r)/\gcd(W(r),bm)\,|\,W(r)/\gcd(W(r),m)
, the number of integers coprime to
W(r)/\gcd(W(r),m)
contained in any arithmetic progression of length
k\geq r^{1/16}
and common
difference
b
is at most

\left(\prod_{p|\gcd(W(r),bm)}\frac{p}{p-1}\right)\cdot\frac{64k}{\log r}=\frac{\gcd(W(r),bm)}{\phi(\gcd(W(r),bm))}\cdot\frac{64k}{\log r},

(28)

assuming that the elements of the arithmetic progression are coprime to
b
.

If the elements of
X
are not coprime to
b
, let
d
be the greatest common divisor of
b
and the elements of
X
. Let
Y=\{x/d:x\in X\}
. Then
Y
is an arithmetic progression of size
|X|
and common difference
b/d
whose elements are coprime to
b/d
. Furthermore, the number of elements of
X
coprime to
W(r)/\gcd(W(r),m)
is at most the number of elements of
Y
coprime to
W(r)/\gcd(W(r),m)
. By (
28
), the number of elements of
Y
coprime to
W(r)/\gcd(W(r),m)
is at most

\frac{\gcd(W(r),bm/d)}{\phi(\gcd(W(r),bm/d))}\cdot\frac{64|Y|}{\log r}\leq\frac{\gcd(W(r),bm)}{\phi(\gcd(W(r),bm))}\cdot\frac{64|X|}{\log r},

where we used that

\frac{\gcd(W(r),bm/d)}{\phi(\gcd(W(r),bm/d))}=\prod_{p|\gcd(W(r),bm/d)}\frac{p}{p-1}\leq\prod_{p|\gcd(W(r),bm)}\frac{p}{p-1}=\frac{\gcd(W(r),bm)}{\phi(\gcd(W(r),bm))}.

Thus, for any arithmetic progression
X
with common difference
b
, the number of integers coprime to
W(r)/\gcd(W(r),m)
in
X
is at most

\frac{\gcd(W(r),bm)}{\phi(\gcd(W(r),bm))}\cdot\frac{64|X|}{\log r}.

(29)

The first claim in the lemma follows immediately upon noticing that
bm\leq n^{3}
, so
\frac{\gcd(W(r),bm)}{\phi(\gcd(W(r),bm))}<2\log\log(bm)<4\log\log n
.

The second claim in the lemma follows from (
29
) by observing that when
b=1
,

\displaystyle\frac{\phi(\gcd(W(r),bm))}{\gcd(W(r),bm)}\cdot\log r

\displaystyle\geq\prod_{p|\gcd(W(r),m)}\frac{p-1}{p}\cdot\frac{1}{2\tau(r,1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\prod_{p|\gcd(W(r),m)}\frac{p-1}{p}\prod_{p|W(r)}\frac{p}{p-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\prod_{p|W(r),p\nmid m}(1-1/p)^{-1},

where we used (
31
) in the first inequality.
∎

B.2
Further estimates for Subsection
5.1

In this subsection, we collect several important estimates that are used throughout Subsection
5.1
.
To this end, let
n
be a sufficiently large positive integer and
m\in[n,\binom{n}{2}]
. For a positive integer
\rho
, recall that
W(\rho)=\prod_{i=1}^{\rho}p_{i}
and
\tau(\rho,m)=\phi(W(\rho)m)/(W(\rho)m)=\prod_{p|W(\rho)m}(1-1/p)
. We define
\rho(n,m)
to be the smallest positive integer
\rho
such that

\rho/\tau(\rho,m)\geq n^{2}/\phi(m).

Note that
\rho/\tau(\rho,m)
is increasing as a function of
\rho
and

\frac{m}{\phi(m)}\cdot\frac{W(\rho)}{\phi(W(\rho))}\geq\frac{1}{\tau(\rho,m)}\geq\max\left(\frac{m}{\phi(m)},\frac{W(\rho)}{\phi(W(\rho))}\right).

(30)

For sufficiently large
\rho
, we have, by Mertens’ third theorem, that

\frac{1}{\tau(\rho,1)}=\frac{W(\rho)}{\phi(W(\rho))}\in[1.6\log\rho,1.8\log\rho].

(31)

Thus,

\tau(\rho,m)\geq\frac{\phi(m)/m}{2\log\rho}.

(32)

Hence, for
\rho
sufficiently large with
\rho\leq n
,

\frac{1}{\tau(\rho,m)}\leq 4\log\rho\log\log m\leq 8\log n\log\log n.

(33)

Furthermore, for
\rho\geq 10\log m/\log\log m
, noting that
m
has at most
2\log m/\log\log m
distinct prime factors larger than
10\log m/\log\log m
, we have

\prod_{p|m,p>p_{\rho}}(1-1/p)^{-1}\leq\left(1-\frac{\log\log m}{10\log m}\right)^{-2\log m/\log\log m}\leq 2,

so

\frac{1}{\tau(\rho,m)}\in[\log\rho,4\log\rho].

(34)

The next claim gives the order of
\rho(n,m)
when
m\leq n^{2}/(\log n)^{2}
.

Claim B.3
.

For
m\leq n^{2}/(\log n)^{2}
,

\rho(n,m)=\Theta\left(\frac{n^{2}/\phi(m)}{\log(n^{2}/\phi(m))}\right).

Proof.

Since
m\leq n^{2}/(\log n)^{2}
, we have
n^{2}/\phi(m)>n^{2}/m\geq(\log n)^{2}
.
Moreover, if
\rho
is a positive integer such that
\rho<10\log m/\log\log m
, then, by (
33
), we have that

\rho/\tau(\rho,m)\leq\frac{10\log m}{\log\log m}\cdot 4\log\rho\log\log m<(\log n)^{2}<n^{2}/\phi(m).

Thus, we must have
\rho(n,m)\geq 10\log m/\log\log m
.

If now
\rho
is a positive integer such that
\rho\geq 10\log m/\log\log m
, we have
\tau(\rho,m)^{-1}\in[\log\rho,4\log\rho]
by (
34
). Therefore, if
\rho\geq 16\frac{n^{2}/\phi(m)}{\log(n^{2}/\phi(m))}
, then, by monotonicity of
\rho\mapsto\frac{\rho}{\tau(\rho,m)}
,

\frac{\rho}{\tau(\rho,m)}\geq\frac{16\frac{n^{2}}{\phi(m)}\cdot\log\left(\frac{16n^{2}/\phi(m)}{\log(n^{2}/\phi(m))}\right)}{\log(n^{2}/\phi(m))}\geq 8\frac{n^{2}}{\phi(m)}

and so
\rho(n,m)\leq 16\frac{n^{2}/\phi(m)}{\log(n^{2}/\phi(m))}
. On the other hand, if
10\log m/\log\log m\leq\rho\leq\frac{1}{16}\frac{n^{2}/\phi(m)}{\log(n^{2}/\phi(m))}
, then

\frac{\rho}{\tau(\rho,m)}\leq\frac{\frac{n^{2}}{\phi(m)}\cdot 4\log\left(\frac{n^{2}/\phi(m)}{16\log(n^{2}/\phi(m))}\right)}{16\log(n^{2}/\phi(m))}\leq\frac{1}{4}\frac{n^{2}}{\phi(m)}

and so
\rho(n,m)\geq\frac{1}{16}\frac{n^{2}/\phi(m)}{\log(n^{2}/\phi(m))}
, as required.
∎

Recall that
\psi(n,m)=\frac{m^{1/3}(m/\phi(m))}{(\log n)^{1/3}(\log\log n)^{2/3}}
and
{\cal R}(n,m)=\min\left(\psi(n,m),\rho(n,m)\right)
. Using Claim
B.3
, it is easy to show that
{\cal R}(n,m)=\Theta\left(\psi(n,m)\right)
when
m=O\left(\frac{n^{3/2}(\log\log n)^{1/2}}{(\log n)^{1/2}}\right)
and
{\cal R}(m,n)=\Theta(\rho(n,m))
when
m=\Omega\left(\frac{n^{3/2}(\log\log n)^{1/2}}{(\log n)^{1/2}}\right)
.

Recall that in Subsection
5.1
, we define
r=c{\cal R}(n,m)
for a sufficiently small absolute constant
c
. The next claim establishes the existence of the integer
y
used in Lemma
5.1
.

Claim B.4
.

Let
n
and
m\in[n,\binom{n}{2}]
be positive integers such that
n
and
\rho(n,m)
are sufficiently large. Let
r=c{\cal R}(n,m)
, where
c>0
is sufficiently small. Then there exists an integer
y<n/2
with

m\in\left[\frac{y^{2}(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)}{25r},\frac{y^{2}(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)}{15r}\right].

Moreover, one may choose
y
such that

y\geq\max(r^{2},n^{3/5})

(35)

and

64(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)\frac{y}{r\log r}>n^{1/4}.

(36)

Proof.

We consider the cases
n\leq m\leq\frac{n^{3/2}(\log\log n)^{1/2}}{(\log n)^{1/2}}
and
\frac{n^{3/2}(\log\log n)^{1/2}}{(\log n)^{1/2}}<m\leq\binom{n}{2}
separately.

Case 1:

n\leq m\leq\frac{n^{3/2}(\log\log n)^{1/2}}{(\log n)^{1/2}}
.

In this case, we have

\frac{n^{1/3}}{(\log n)^{2/3}}\leq r\leq\frac{cCm^{1/3}(m/\phi(m))}{(\log n)^{1/3}(\log\log n)^{2/3}}
, where
C
is some absolute constant independent of all other parameters. Since
r\geq\frac{n^{1/3}}{(\log n)^{2/3}}
, by (
34
), we have
1/\tau(r,m)\in[\log r,4\log r]
. We also have

\sqrt{\frac{mr\log r}{m/\phi(m)}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{cCm^{4/3}\log n}{(\log n)^{1/3}(\log\log n)^{2/3}}}\leq n\sqrt{cC}.

Thus, for sufficiently small
c
, there exists an integer
y
such that
y<\sqrt{\frac{25mr}{(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)}}<\frac{n}{2}
and
y>\sqrt{\frac{15mr}{(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)}}
.
This integer
y
then satisfies
y<n/2
and

m\in\left[\frac{y^{2}(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)}{25r},\frac{y^{2}(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)}{15r}\right].

Furthermore, we have

\frac{r^{3}(m/\phi(m))}{m(\log n)}\leq\frac{(cC)^{3}m(m/\phi(m))^{4}}{m(\log n)^{2}(\log\log n)^{2}}<1/2.

(37)

Since
r\geq\frac{n^{1/3}}{(\log n)^{2/3}}>n^{3/10}
, we also have

y>\sqrt{\frac{15mr\log r}{m/\phi(m)}}>\sqrt{\frac{mr\log n}{m/\phi(m)}}>r^{2}>n^{3/5},

where we used (
37
) in the third inequality.

Case 2:

\frac{n^{3/2}(\log\log n)^{1/2}}{(\log n)^{1/2}}<m\leq\binom{n}{2}
.

In this case, we have
cC^{-1}\rho(n,m)\leq r\leq cC\rho(n,m)
, where
C
is again an absolute constant and we assume that
\rho(n,m)
is sufficiently large. By the definition of
\rho(n,m)
,

\sqrt{\frac{mr/\tau(r,m)}{m/\phi(m)}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{2cCmn^{2}/\phi(m)}{m/\phi(m)}}\leq n\sqrt{2cC}.

Thus, for sufficiently small
c
, there exists an integer
y
such that
y<\sqrt{\frac{25mr}{(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)}}<\frac{n}{2}
and
y>\sqrt{\frac{15mr}{(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)}}
.
This integer
y
then satisfies
y<n/2
and

m\in\left[\frac{y^{2}(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)}{25r},\frac{y^{2}(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)}{15r}\right].

If
\frac{n^{3/2}(\log\log n)^{1/2}}{(\log n)^{1/2}}<m<n^{7/4}
, we have
\rho(n,m)\geq n^{1/8}
, so
\tau(\rho(n,m),m)\leq 1/\log\rho(n,m)\leq 10/\log n
by (
34
). Using this, we obtain

\phi(m)^{2}\geq\frac{m^{2}}{4(\log\log m)^{2}}\geq\frac{n^{3}}{16(\log\log n)(\log n)}\geq 4\tau(\rho(n,m),m)^{2}n^{3}.

If
m\geq n^{7/4}
, we also easily have

\tau(\rho(n,m),m)^{2}n^{3}\leq n^{3}\leq\frac{1}{4}\phi(m)^{2}.

Since
\frac{\rho(n,m)}{\tau(\rho(n,m),m)}\in[\frac{n^{2}}{\phi(m)},\frac{2n^{2}}{\phi(m)}]
by the definition of
\rho(n,m)
, we obtain
\rho(n,m)\leq\sqrt{n}
in both ranges
\frac{n^{3/2}(\log\log n)^{1/2}}{(\log n)^{1/2}}<m<n^{7/4}
and
n^{7/4}\leq m\leq\binom{n}{2}
.
Moreover, since
r\geq cC^{-1}\rho(n,m)
,

\tau(r,m)=\tau(\rho(n,m),m)\prod_{i\in(r,\rho(n,m)],p_{i}\nmid m}(1-1/p_{i})^{-1}\leq\frac{1.8\log\rho(n,m)}{1.6\log r}\tau(\rho(n,m),m)\leq 25\tau(\rho(n,m),m),

where we used that
\rho(n,m)
is sufficiently large.
Hence,

y\geq\sqrt{\frac{mr}{25(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)}}\geq\frac{\sqrt{cC^{-1}}}{25}\sqrt{\frac{m\rho(n,m)}{(m/\phi(m))\tau(\rho(n,m),m)}}\geq\frac{n\sqrt{cC^{-1}}}{25}\geq c^{2}C^{2}\rho(n,m)^{2}\geq r^{2},

(38)

where we used the definition of
\rho(n,m)
, the bound
\rho(n,m)\leq\sqrt{n}
and assumed
c
is sufficiently small. Furthermore, from (
38
), for
n
sufficiently large, we have

y\geq\frac{n\sqrt{cC^{-1}}}{25}>n^{3/5}.

Thus, in both Case 1 and Case 2, there exists a choice of
y<n/2
with

m\in\left[\frac{y^{2}(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)}{25r},\frac{y^{2}(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)}{15r}\right]

such that (
35
) also holds. Moreover, (
36
) holds, since

64(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)\frac{y}{r\log r}\geq\frac{32y^{1/2}}{(\log n)^{2}}>n^{1/4},

where we used that
y\geq\max(r^{2},n^{3/5})
,
\log r\leq\log n
and, by (
32
),
(m/\phi(m))\tau(r,m)\geq\frac{1}{2\log r}\geq\frac{1}{2\log n}
.
∎
